3.2.1
In modes

We define the positive-frequency in-states to be those modes for which there is only one component with incoming momentum (heading towards the dyon). Labelling these by the sign of the incoming particle’s electric charge
{\mathfrak{s}}
, they are given explicitly by

{{{\mathfrak{u}}}}^{\rm in}_{+,k}=\begin{bmatrix}[l]\begin{pmatrix}e^{-ikr}\\
e^{ik(r-2\epsilon)}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{r}\right)^{ieQ}\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm in}}\end{pmatrix}\\[11.38109pt]
\begin{pmatrix}0\\
e^{i(k-ev)r}e^{-i(2k-ev)\epsilon}\,e^{-i{\mathfrak{a}}}{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm in}}\end{pmatrix}\end{bmatrix}{e^{-i(k-\frac{ev}{2})t}\left(\frac{r}{r_{0}}\right)^{ieQ/2}}

(67)

and

{{{\mathfrak{u}}}}^{\rm in}_{-,k}=\begin{bmatrix}[l]\begin{pmatrix}0\\
e^{i(k+ev)r}e^{-i(2k+ev)\epsilon}\,e^{i{\mathfrak{a}}}{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}\end{pmatrix}\\[11.38109pt]
\begin{pmatrix}e^{-ikr}\\
e^{ik(r-2\epsilon)}\,\left(\frac{r}{\epsilon}\right)^{ieQ}\,\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}\end{pmatrix}\end{bmatrix}{e^{-i(k+\frac{ev}{2})t}\left(\frac{r}{r_{0}}\right)^{-ieQ/2}\,}\,.

(68)

where

k=\omega+\frac{1}{2}{\mathfrak{s}}ev\geq 0
. (These can be obtained from (
65
) and (
66
) by choosing
\bm{\kappa}_{++}=1,\bm{\kappa}_{--}=0
for
{\mathfrak{u}}^{{\rm in}}_{+,k}
and
\bm{\kappa}_{--}=1,\bm{\kappa}_{++}=0
for
{\mathfrak{u}}^{{\rm in}}_{-,k}
.) The negative-frequency in-modes are similarly defined by

{{{\mathfrak{v}}}}^{\rm in}_{+,k}=\begin{bmatrix}[l]\begin{pmatrix}e^{ikr}\\
e^{-ik(r-2\epsilon)}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{r}\right)^{ieQ}\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm in}}\end{pmatrix}\\[11.38109pt]
\begin{pmatrix}0\\
e^{-i(k+ev)r}e^{i(2k+ev)\epsilon}\,e^{-i{\mathfrak{a}}}{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm in}}\end{pmatrix}\end{bmatrix}{e^{i(k+\frac{ev}{2})t}\left(\frac{r}{r_{0}}\right)^{ieQ/2}}

(69)

and

{{{\mathfrak{v}}}}^{\rm in}_{-,k}=\begin{bmatrix}[l]\begin{pmatrix}0\\
e^{-i(k-ev)r}e^{i(2k-ev)\epsilon}\,e^{i{\mathfrak{a}}}{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}\end{pmatrix}\\[11.38109pt]
\begin{pmatrix}e^{ikr}\\
e^{-ik(r-2\epsilon)}\,\left(\frac{r}{\epsilon}\right)^{ieQ}\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}\end{pmatrix}\end{bmatrix}{e^{i(k-\frac{ev}{2})t}\left(\frac{r}{r_{0}}\right)^{-ieQ/2}},

(70)

where
k=\omega-\frac{1}{2}{\mathfrak{s}}ev\geq 0
. In both of these expressions
r_{0}
is an arbitrary length that contributes only to the overall Coulomb phase.

The coefficients
\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm in}}
,
\mathcal{T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}
,
\mathcal{T}^{-+}_{{\rm in}}
and
\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}
appearing in these expressions are
{\mathfrak{a}}
-independent quantities given as explicit functions of the fermion-dyon couplings by

\mathcal{T}^{++}_{\rm in}=-\frac{\left|\begin{array}[]{ll}\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{21}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{24}\\
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{41}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{44}\end{array}\right|}{\left|\begin{array}[]{ll}\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{22}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{24}\\
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{42}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{44}\end{array}\right|},\;{\cal T}^{+-}_{\rm in}=-\frac{\left|\begin{array}[]{ll}\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{23}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{24}\\
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{43}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{44}\end{array}\right|}{\left|\begin{array}[]{ll}\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{22}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{24}\\
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{42}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{44}\end{array}\right|},\;{\cal T}^{-+}_{\rm in}=-\frac{\left|\begin{array}[]{ll}\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{22}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{21}\\
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{42}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{41}\end{array}\right|}{\left|\begin{array}[]{ll}\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{22}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{24}\\
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{42}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{44}\end{array}\right|},\;\mathcal{T}^{--}_{\rm in}=-\frac{\left|\begin{array}[]{ll}\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{22}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{23}\\
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{42}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{43}\end{array}\right|}{\left|\begin{array}[]{ll}\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{22}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{24}\\
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{42}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{44}\end{array}\right|}\,.

(71)

Written directly in terms of the boundary couplings, these become

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm in}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(\mathcal{C}^{s}_{++}+i\mathcal{C}^{ps}_{++})(i-\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{--}+\mathcal{C}^{v}_{--})+(\mathcal{C}^{s}_{-+}+i\mathcal{C}^{ps}_{-+})(\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{+-}-\mathcal{C}^{v}_{+-})}{-|\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{+-}-\mathcal{C}^{v}_{+-}|^{2}+(-i+\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{++}-\mathcal{C}^{v}_{++})(\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{--}-\mathcal{C}^{v}_{--}-i)},

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(\mathcal{C}^{s}_{--}+i\mathcal{C}^{ps}_{--})(i-\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{++}+\mathcal{C}^{v}_{++})+(\mathcal{C}^{s}_{+-}+i\mathcal{C}^{ps}_{+-})(\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{-+}-\mathcal{C}^{v}_{-+})}{-|\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{+-}-\mathcal{C}^{v}_{+-}|^{2}+(-i+\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{++}-\mathcal{C}^{v}_{++})(\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{--}-\mathcal{C}^{v}_{--}-i)},

(72)

\displaystyle{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm in}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(\mathcal{C}^{s}_{-+}+i\mathcal{C}^{ps}_{-+})(i-\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{++}+\mathcal{C}^{v}_{++})+(\mathcal{C}^{s}_{++}+i\mathcal{C}^{ps}_{++})(\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{-+}-\mathcal{C}^{v}_{-+})}{-|\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{+-}-\mathcal{C}^{v}_{+-}|^{2}+(-i+\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{++}-\mathcal{C}^{v}_{++})(\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{--}-\mathcal{C}^{v}_{--}-i)},

\displaystyle{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(\mathcal{C}^{s}_{+-}+i\mathcal{C}^{ps}_{+-})(i-\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{--}+\mathcal{C}^{v}_{--})+(\mathcal{C}^{s}_{--}+i\mathcal{C}^{ps}_{--})(\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{+-}-\mathcal{C}^{v}_{+-})}{-|\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{+-}-\mathcal{C}^{v}_{+-}|^{2}+(-i+\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{++}-\mathcal{C}^{v}_{++})(\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{--}-\mathcal{C}^{v}_{--}-i)}.

When the boundary action
S_{\rm dyon2}
is real (and so the
{\cal C}^{\scriptscriptstyle A}_{ij}
are hermitian) these satisfy the following unitarity conditions

|\mathcal{T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}|^{2}=|\mathcal{T}^{-+}_{{\rm in}}|^{2}=1-|\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm in}}|^{2}=1-|\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}|^{2}\,,

(73)

as well as

\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}{\cal T}^{-+\,*}_{{\rm in}}+{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}\mathcal{T}^{++\,*}_{{\rm in}}=0,

(74)

as identities (see Appendix
D
for a derivation). These relations imply that the
in
amplitudes only carry 4 real parameters’ worth of information; they can always be written

\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm in}}=\rho\,e^{i\theta_{++}}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}=\rho\,e^{i\theta_{--}},

(75)

and

{\mathcal{T}}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}=\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}\,e^{i\theta_{+-}}\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathcal{T}}^{-+}_{{\rm in}}=-\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}\,e^{i(\theta_{++}+\theta_{--}-\theta_{+-})},

(76)

where
\rho
,
\theta_{++}
,
\theta_{+-}
and
\theta_{--}
are the four independent real parameters.

3.2.2
Out modes

A set of outgoing modes can be constructed in precisely the same way, with the positive frequency modes given by

{{{\mathfrak{u}}}}^{{\rm out}}_{+,k}=\begin{bmatrix}[l]\begin{pmatrix}e^{-ik(r-2\epsilon)}\left(\frac{r}{\epsilon}\right)^{ieQ}\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm out}}\\
e^{ikr}\end{pmatrix}\\[11.38109pt]
\begin{pmatrix}e^{-i(k-ev)r}e^{i(2k-ev)\epsilon}\,e^{-i{\mathfrak{a}}}{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm out}}\\
0\end{pmatrix}\end{bmatrix}{e^{-i(k-\frac{ev}{2})t}\left(\frac{r}{r_{0}}\right)^{-ieQ/2}},

(77)

and

{{{\mathfrak{u}}}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,k}=\begin{bmatrix}[l]\begin{pmatrix}e^{-i(k+ev)r}e^{i(2k+ev)\epsilon}\,e^{i{\mathfrak{a}}}{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm out}}\\
0\end{pmatrix}\\[11.38109pt]
\begin{pmatrix}e^{-ik(r-2\epsilon)}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{r}\right)^{ieQ}\,\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm out}}\\
e^{ikr}\end{pmatrix}\end{bmatrix}{e^{-i(k+\frac{ev}{2})t}\left(\frac{r}{r_{0}}\right)^{ieQ/2}},

(78)

with

k=\omega+\frac{1}{2}{\mathfrak{s}}ev\geq 0
(these can be obtained from (
63
) and (
64
) by choosing
\bm{\kappa}_{+-}=1
,
\bm{\kappa}_{-+}=0
for
{\mathfrak{u}}^{{\rm out}}_{+,k}
and
\bm{\kappa}_{-+}=1,\bm{\kappa}_{+-}=0
for
{\mathfrak{u}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,k}
). The negative-frequency out-modes are

{{{\mathfrak{v}}}}^{{\rm out}}_{+,k}=\begin{bmatrix}[l]\begin{pmatrix}e^{ik(r-2\epsilon)}\left(\frac{r}{\epsilon}\right)^{ieQ}\,\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm out}}\\
e^{-ikr}\end{pmatrix}\\[11.38109pt]
\begin{pmatrix}e^{i(k+ev)r}e^{-i(2k+ev)\epsilon}\,e^{-i{\mathfrak{a}}}{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm out}}\\
0\end{pmatrix}\end{bmatrix}{e^{i(k+\frac{ev}{2})t}\left(\frac{r}{r_{0}}\right)^{-ieQ/2}},

(79)

and

{{{\mathfrak{v}}}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,k}=\begin{bmatrix}[l]\begin{pmatrix}e^{i(k-ev)r}e^{-i(2k-ev)\epsilon}\,e^{i{\mathfrak{a}}}{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm out}}\\
0\end{pmatrix}\\[11.38109pt]
\begin{pmatrix}e^{ik(r-2\epsilon)}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{r}\right)^{ieQ}\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm out}}\\
e^{-ikr}\\
\end{pmatrix}\end{bmatrix}{e^{i(k-\frac{ev}{2})t}\,\left(\frac{r}{r_{0}}\right)^{ieQ/2}},

(80)

for which
k=\omega-\frac{1}{2}{\mathfrak{s}}ev\geq 0
.

The
{\mathfrak{a}}
-independent coefficients
\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm out}},\mathcal{T}^{+-}_{{\rm out}},\mathcal{T}^{-+}_{{\rm out}},\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm out}}
appearing in these expressions are defined in terms of the dyon-fermion couplings by

\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm out}}=-\frac{\left|\begin{array}[]{ll}\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{12}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{13}\\
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{32}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{33}\end{array}\right|}{\left|\begin{array}[]{ll}\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{11}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{13}\\
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{31}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{33}\end{array}\right|},\;{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm out}}=-\frac{\left|\begin{array}[]{ll}\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{11}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{12}\\
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{31}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{32}\end{array}\right|}{\left|\begin{array}[]{ll}\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{11}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{13}\\
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{31}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{33}\end{array}\right|},\;{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm out}}=-\frac{\left|\begin{array}[]{ll}\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{14}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{13}\\
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{34}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{33}\end{array}\right|}{\left|\begin{array}[]{ll}\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{11}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{13}\\
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{31}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{33}\end{array}\right|},\;\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm out}}=-\frac{\left|\begin{array}[]{ll}\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{11}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{14}\\
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{31}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{34}\end{array}\right|}{\left|\begin{array}[]{ll}\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{11}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{13}\\
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{31}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{33}\end{array}\right|},

(81)

and so

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm out}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i(\mathcal{C}^{ps}_{++}+i\mathcal{C}^{s}_{++})(-i+\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{--}+\mathcal{C}^{v}_{--})+(-i\mathcal{C}^{ps}_{-+}+\mathcal{C}^{s}_{-+})(\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{+-}+\mathcal{C}^{v}_{+-})}{|\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{+-}+\mathcal{C}^{v}_{+-}|^{2}-(-i+\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{--}+\mathcal{C}^{v}_{--})(\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{++}+\mathcal{C}^{v}_{++}-i)},

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm out}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i(\mathcal{C}^{ps}_{--}+i\mathcal{C}^{s}_{--})(-i+\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{++}+\mathcal{C}^{v}_{++})+(-i\mathcal{C}^{ps}_{+-}+\mathcal{C}^{s}_{+-})(\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{-+}+\mathcal{C}^{v}_{-+})}{|\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{+-}+\mathcal{C}^{v}_{+-}|^{2}-(-i+\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{--}+\mathcal{C}^{v}_{--})(\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{++}+\mathcal{C}^{v}_{++}-i)},

(82)

\displaystyle{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm out}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i(\mathcal{C}^{ps}_{-+}+i\mathcal{C}^{s}_{-+})(-i+\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{++}+\mathcal{C}^{v}_{++})+(-i\mathcal{C}^{ps}_{++}+\mathcal{C}^{s}_{++})(\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{-+}+\mathcal{C}^{v}_{-+})}{|\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{+-}+\mathcal{C}^{v}_{+-}|^{2}-(-i+\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{--}+\mathcal{C}^{v}_{--})(\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{++}+\mathcal{C}^{v}_{++}-i)},

\displaystyle{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm out}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i(\mathcal{C}^{ps}_{+-}+i\mathcal{C}^{s}_{+-})(-i+\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{--}+\mathcal{C}^{v}_{--})+(-i\mathcal{C}^{ps}_{--}+\mathcal{C}^{s}_{--})(\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{+-}+\mathcal{C}^{v}_{+-})}{|\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{+-}+\mathcal{C}^{v}_{+-}|^{2}-(-i+\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{--}+\mathcal{C}^{v}_{--})(\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{++}+\mathcal{C}^{v}_{++}-i)}.

When the boundary action
S_{\rm dyon2}
is real (so the
{\cal C}^{\scriptscriptstyle A}_{ij}
’s are hermitian) these satisfy

|\mathcal{T}^{+-}_{{\rm out}}|^{2}=|\mathcal{T}^{-+}_{{\rm out}}|^{2}=1-|\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm out}}|^{2}=1-|\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm out}}|^{2}\,.

(83)

and

\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm out}}{\cal T}^{-+\,*}_{{\rm out}}+{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm out}}\mathcal{T}^{++\,*}_{{\rm out}}=0,

(84)

in the same way as found earlier for
{\cal T}_{{\rm in}}
. There is of course a good reason why
{\cal T}_{{\rm out}}
and
{\cal T}_{{\rm in}}
satisfy similar conditions: they are not independent of one another. Since both the
in
and
out
bases are complete, the
\mathcal{T}_{{\rm out}}
amplitudes can be expressed in terms of the
\mathcal{T}_{{\rm in}}
amplitudes. This leads to the relations (see Appendix
D
)

\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm out}}=(\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm in}})^{*},\quad\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm out}}=(\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm in}})^{*},\quad\mathcal{T}^{+-}_{{\rm out}}=(\mathcal{T}^{-+}_{{\rm in}})^{*}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{T}^{-+}_{{\rm out}}=(\mathcal{T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}})^{*}\,,

(85)

and so the
out
amplitudes also depend only on the four parameters given in (
75
) and (
76
).

The implications of the
{\cal T}_{{\rm in}}
and
{\cal T}_{{\rm out}}
amplitudes for fermion-dyon scattering problems are explored in some detail in §
5
and §
6
below and suffice it to say that they carry all of the information about the dyon that is relevant to describing transitions amongst the
S
-wave fermion modes described above, at least at leading order at low energies. See §
4
for a discussion of the values predicted for these quantities by specific microscopic choices for the underlying dyon solution.

But the above discussion introduces a minor puzzle: why are there only four free real parameters within, say, the
{\cal T}_{{\rm in}}
’s when there are 16 possible real parameters in the hermitian effective couplings
{\cal C}_{ij}^{\scriptscriptstyle A}
appearing in
S_{\rm dyon2}
in
e.g.
eqs. (
40
) and (
41
)? We argue in §
3.4
that all but four of the effective couplings
{\cal C}_{ij}^{\scriptscriptstyle A}
are redundant (in the precise EFT sense
[
16
]
), but before doing so the next section first develops a required tool. Along the way it also resolves a dangling technical issue: how to handle systematically the
{\mathfrak{a}}
-dependence that is embedded in boundary conditions like (
50
) or (
53
).

3.3
Dyonic response

Up to this point we have treated the matrix
{\cal B}
as if it were specified completely once the effective couplings
{\cal C}^{\scriptscriptstyle A}_{ij}
are, but the appearance of the field
{\mathfrak{a}}
within
{\cal B}
makes this not quite true. Having
{\cal B}
be a function of
{\mathfrak{a}}
complicates its use – as in (
53
) – to find fermionic mode functions. This section provides two complementary ways to handle this field-dependence: perturbation theory and the Born-Oppenheimer approximation.

3.3.1
Fermion-dyon perturbation theory

The first approach starts with the observation that not
every
term in the matrix
{\cal B}
depends on
{\mathfrak{a}}
. If the coefficients
{\cal C}^{\scriptscriptstyle A}_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}
with
{\mathfrak{s}}\neq{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}
were for some reason much smaller than the others then they could be ignored when formulating the near-dyon fermion boundary conditions with the effects of
{\mathfrak{a}}
-dependent terms then included perturbatively.

For instance, suppose
S_{\rm dyon2}
of (
48
) could be written
S_{\rm dyon2}^{(0)}+S_{\rm dyon2}^{\rm int}
with
15
15

15

We choose the unperturbed boundary terms here fairly arbitrarily and all that is important for our arguments is that the dominant piece be
{\mathfrak{a}}
-independent.

S_{\rm dyon2}^{(0)}:=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{{\mathfrak{s}}=\pm}\int{\rm d}t\,i\overline{\chi}_{{\mathfrak{s}}}\Gamma_{c}\,\chi_{{\mathfrak{s}}}\,,

(86)

and

S_{\rm dyon2}^{\rm int}=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{{\mathfrak{s}},{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}=\pm}\int{\rm d}t\,\overline{\chi}_{{\mathfrak{s}}}\Bigl(\delta\mathcal{C}^{s}_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}+i\delta\mathcal{C}^{ps}_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}\Gamma_{c}-i\delta\mathcal{C}^{v}_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}\Gamma^{0}+i\delta\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}\Gamma^{1}\Bigr)\,\chi_{{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}\;e^{\frac{i}{2}({\mathfrak{s}}-{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime})\,{\mathfrak{a}}}\,,

(87)

where the coefficients
\delta{\cal C}^{\scriptscriptstyle A}_{ij}
are regarded as being perturbatively small
16
16

16

Although, as noted above, the
{\cal C}^{\scriptscriptstyle A}_{ij}
’s are not generically suppressed by the loop-counting parameter
\alpha=e^{2}/(4\pi)
it is possible that in some circumstances some of them are suppressed by another small quantity.
and we again specialize to the case of an approximately static dyon (for which
\dot{y}^{\alpha}\simeq\delta^{\alpha}_{0}
). In this case only
S_{\rm dyon2}^{(0)}
need play a role in determining the boundary conditions of the free modes appearing in the field expansions within the interaction picture.

With this choice the boundary condition matrix of (
51
) becomes
O_{\mathcal{B}}^{(0)}=iI\otimes\Gamma_{c}
and so the matrix appearing in (
54
) becomes

\mathcal{B}^{(0)}=\Gamma^{0}\left[\Gamma^{1}+O_{\cal B}^{(0)}\right]=\begin{pmatrix}1&-1&0&0\\[5.69054pt]
1&-1&0&0\\[5.69054pt]
0&0&1&-1\\[5.69054pt]
0&0&1&-1\end{pmatrix}\,.

(88)

which clearly has rank two. The boundary condition satisfied by the free mode functions at
r=\epsilon
therefore is

\Bigl(\Gamma^{1}+i\Gamma_{c}\Bigr)\bm{\chi}(\epsilon,t)=0\,.

(89)

The continuum normalized free
in
and
out
mode functions that satisfy (
89
) then are

{{\mathfrak{u}}}^{{\rm in}0}_{+,k}=\begin{bmatrix}[l]\begin{pmatrix}e^{-ikr}\\
e^{ik(r-2\epsilon)}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{r}\right)^{ieQ}\end{pmatrix}\\[14.22636pt]
\hskip 14.22636pt\begin{pmatrix}\hskip 14.22636pt0\hskip 14.22636pt\\
\hskip 14.22636pt0\hskip 14.22636pt\end{pmatrix}\end{bmatrix}{e^{-i(k-\frac{ev}{2})t}\left(\frac{r}{r_{0}}\right)^{ieQ/2}},\hskip 8.19447pt{{\mathfrak{u}}}^{{\rm in}0}_{-,k}=\begin{bmatrix}[l]\hskip 14.22636pt\begin{pmatrix}\hskip 14.22636pt0\hskip 14.22636pt\\
\hskip 14.22636pt0\hskip 14.22636pt\end{pmatrix}\\[14.22636pt]
\begin{pmatrix}e^{-ikr}\\
e^{ik(r-2\epsilon)}\,\left(\frac{r}{\epsilon}\right)^{ieQ}\end{pmatrix}\end{bmatrix}{e^{-i(k+\frac{ev}{2})t}\left(\frac{r}{r_{0}}\right)^{-ieQ/2}\,},

(90)

{{\mathfrak{v}}}^{{\rm in}0}_{+,k}=\begin{bmatrix}[l]\begin{pmatrix}e^{ikr}\\
e^{-ik(r-2\epsilon)}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{r}\right)^{ieQ}\end{pmatrix}\\[14.22636pt]
\hskip 14.22636pt\begin{pmatrix}\hskip 14.22636pt0\hskip 14.22636pt\\
\hskip 14.22636pt0\hskip 14.22636pt\end{pmatrix}\end{bmatrix}{e^{i(k+\frac{ev}{2})t}\left(\frac{r}{r_{0}}\right)^{ieQ/2}},\hskip 8.19447pt{{\mathfrak{v}}}^{{\rm in}0}_{-,k}=\begin{bmatrix}[l]\hskip 14.22636pt\begin{pmatrix}\hskip 14.22636pt0\hskip 14.22636pt\\
\hskip 14.22636pt0\hskip 14.22636pt\end{pmatrix}\\[14.22636pt]
\begin{pmatrix}e^{ikr}\\
e^{-ik(r-2\epsilon)}\,\left(\frac{r}{\epsilon}\right)^{ieQ}\end{pmatrix}\end{bmatrix}{e^{i(k-\frac{ev}{2})t}\left(\frac{r}{r_{0}}\right)^{-ieQ/2}},

(91)

(where
k\geq 0
) and

{{\mathfrak{u}}}^{{\rm out}0}_{+,k}=\begin{bmatrix}[l]\begin{pmatrix}e^{-ik(r-2\epsilon)}\left(\frac{r}{\epsilon}\right)^{ieQ}\\
e^{ikr}\end{pmatrix}\\[14.22636pt]
\hskip 14.22636pt\begin{pmatrix}\hskip 14.22636pt0\hskip 14.22636pt\\
\hskip 14.22636pt0\hskip 14.22636pt\end{pmatrix}\end{bmatrix}{e^{-i(k-\frac{ev}{2})t}\left(\frac{r}{r_{0}}\right)^{-ieQ/2}},\hskip 8.19447pt{{\mathfrak{u}}}^{{\rm out}0}_{-,k}=\begin{bmatrix}[l]\hskip 14.22636pt\begin{pmatrix}\hskip 14.22636pt0\hskip 14.22636pt\\
\hskip 14.22636pt0\hskip 14.22636pt\end{pmatrix}\\[14.22636pt]
\begin{pmatrix}e^{-ik(r-2\epsilon)}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{r}\right)^{ieQ}\\
e^{ikr}\end{pmatrix}\end{bmatrix}{e^{-i(k+\frac{ev}{2})t}\left(\frac{r}{r_{0}}\right)^{ieQ/2}},

(92)

{{\mathfrak{v}}}^{{\rm out}0}_{+,k}=\begin{bmatrix}[l]\begin{pmatrix}e^{ik(r-2\epsilon)}\left(\frac{r}{\epsilon}\right)^{ieQ}\\
e^{-ikr}\end{pmatrix}\\[14.22636pt]
\hskip 14.22636pt\begin{pmatrix}\hskip 14.22636pt0\hskip 14.22636pt\\
\hskip 14.22636pt0\hskip 14.22636pt\end{pmatrix}\end{bmatrix}{e^{i(k+\frac{ev}{2})t}\left(\frac{r}{r_{0}}\right)^{-ieQ/2}},\hskip 8.19447pt{{\mathfrak{v}}}^{{\rm out}0}_{-,k}=\begin{bmatrix}[l]\hskip 14.22636pt\begin{pmatrix}\hskip 14.22636pt0\hskip 14.22636pt\\
\hskip 14.22636pt0\hskip 14.22636pt\end{pmatrix}\\[14.22636pt]
\begin{pmatrix}e^{ik(r-2\epsilon)}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{r}\right)^{ieQ}\\
e^{-ikr}\\
\end{pmatrix}\end{bmatrix}{e^{i(k-\frac{ev}{2})t}\,\left(\frac{r}{r_{0}}\right)^{ieQ/2}},

(93)

and so the unperturbed modes satisfy
{\cal T}_{{\rm in}}^{++}={\cal T}_{\rm in}^{--}=1
and
{\cal T}_{{\rm in}}^{+-}={\cal T}_{{\rm in}}^{-+}=0
. These modes are normalized so that

\int^{\infty}_{\epsilon}\mathrm{d}r\,({{\mathfrak{u}}}^{{\rm in}0}_{\mathfrak{s},k})^{\dagger}\,{{\mathfrak{u}}}^{{\rm in}0}_{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime},k^{\prime}}=\int^{\infty}_{\epsilon}\mathrm{d}r\,({{\mathfrak{v}}}^{{\rm in}0}_{\mathfrak{s},k})^{\dagger}\,{{\mathfrak{v}}}^{{\rm in}0}_{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime},k^{\prime}}=2\pi\delta(k-k^{\prime})\,\delta_{\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}},

(94)

and

\int^{\infty}_{\epsilon}\mathrm{d}r\,({{\mathfrak{u}}}^{{\rm in}0}_{\mathfrak{s},k})^{\dagger}\,{{\mathfrak{v}}}^{{\rm in}0}_{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime},k^{\prime}}=2\pi\delta(k+k^{\prime})\,\delta_{\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{s^{\prime}}},

(95)

and similarly for the
out
modes.

From here on perturbation theory proceeds in the standard way by moving to the interaction picture and expanding the fermion field operator using these mode functions,

\displaystyle\bm{\chi}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{{\mathfrak{s}}=\pm}\int^{\infty}_{0}\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\Bigl[{\mathfrak{u}}^{{\rm in}0}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}(x)\,{a}^{{\rm in}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}+{\mathfrak{v}}^{{\rm in}0}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}(x)\,(\overline{a}^{{\rm in}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k})^{\star}\Bigr]

(96)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{{\mathfrak{s}}=\pm}\int^{\infty}_{0}\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\Bigl[{\mathfrak{u}}^{{\rm out}0}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}(x)\,{a}^{{\rm out}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}+{\mathfrak{v}}^{{\rm out}0}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}(x)\,(\overline{a}^{{\rm out}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k})^{\star}\Bigr],

where the creation/annihilation operators satisfy the usual algebra
\{a^{{\rm in}}_{\mathfrak{s},k},\,(a^{{\rm in}}_{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime},k^{\prime}})^{\star}\}=\delta(k-k^{\prime})\,\delta_{\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}}
and
\{\overline{a}^{{\rm in}}_{\mathfrak{s},k},\,(\overline{a}^{{\rm in}}_{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime},k^{\prime}})^{\star}\}=\delta(k-k^{\prime})\,\delta_{\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}}
appropriate for fermions (and similarly for the
out
operators). Because both the
in
and
out
modes are complete, each can be expanded in terms of the other, allowing the
in
and
out
operators to be related by

a^{{\rm out}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}=e^{-2ik\epsilon}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{r_{0}}\right)^{i{\mathfrak{s}}eQ}a^{{\rm in}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}\quad\text{and}\quad({\overline{a}}^{{\rm out}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k})^{\star}=e^{2ik\epsilon}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{r_{0}}\right)^{i{\mathfrak{s}}eQ}\,({\overline{a}}^{{\rm in}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k})^{\star},

(97)

and their adjoints. The above Bogoliubov relations depend on the arbitrary scale
r_{0}
. Physical predictions should not depend on
r_{0}
and in §
5
– which uses the perturbative framework set up here – we rephase the
out
operators to absorb the
r_{0}
dependence.

The perturbative formalism is useful for some kinds of questions – such as the discussion of redundancy in §
3.4
– and is used in §
5
to compute some scattering processes (which can be compared with calculations performed in §
6
using the alternative approach we describe next). But it is not guaranteed that specific microscopic realizations of the dyon must yield effective couplings for which such a perturbative approach is a good approximation. This is true in particular for massless fermion scattering in the classical nonabelian dyon background because in this case conservation of chirality implies the vanishing of all of the
{\mathfrak{a}}
-independent boundary couplings
{\cal C}^{\scriptscriptstyle A}_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}
with
{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}={\mathfrak{s}}
.

3.3.2
Born-Oppenheimer evolution of
{\mathfrak{a}}

A more ambitious approach to computing the fermion-
{\mathfrak{a}}
interactions takes advantage of the fact that the mass
{\cal I}\sim(\alpha m_{g})^{-1}
appearing in the
{\mathfrak{a}}
kinetic term is much larger than the generic dyon scale
m_{g}^{-1}
in the semiclassical limit (for which
\alpha=e^{2}/4\pi\ll 1
). This means that the energy associated with
{\mathfrak{a}}
excitation is order
{\cal I}^{-1}\sim\alpha m_{g}
and so is negligible – even within the low energy theory – for fermion momenta in the range
\alpha m_{g}\ll k\ll m_{g}
.

Furthermore, the equations of motion say that
\dot{\mathfrak{a}}\propto{\mathfrak{p}}/{\cal I}
and so the timescale for
{\mathfrak{a}}
to respond to order-unity changes in
{\mathfrak{p}}
are order
\tau\sim{\cal I}
. By contrast the timescale for a relativistic fermionic wave packet of size
L
to interact with a much smaller dyon is order
L
(which cannot be smaller than
1/k
for fermions with momentum
k
). So the rotor response is slow compared to the fermion interaction time for fermion momenta in the range
\alpha m_{g}\ll L^{-1}\mathrel{\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$<$\kern-8.50006pt\lower 4.30554pt\hbox{$\sim$}}}k
.

In the regime
\alpha m_{g}\ll k
the ‘rotor’ field response to fermions is slow and costs little energy in much the same way that the position of heavy atomic nuclei respond to much lighter and faster electrons in everyday solids. This electron/nucleus analogy suggests using the Born-Oppenheimer approximation
[
54
]
to describe the interactions of relativistic fermions with the slower dyonic excitation
{\mathfrak{a}}
. In this approximation one first solves for the motion of the fast degrees of freedom (the light fermions) with the slow degrees of freedom (the nuclear positions
{\bf R}
or the field
{\mathfrak{a}}
) imagined to be fixed classical variables. The idea is that the slow variables behave classically because they do not have time to respond at all when hit by the fast ones. Once the fast evolution is computed one calculates an effective Hamiltonian describing the dynamics of the slow degrees of freedom obtained by averaging over the fast degrees of freedom. This Hamiltonian is then used to solve for how the slower variables evolve in response to its interactions with the much faster system.

As applied to dyon-fermion interactions the first part of the Born-Oppenheimer procedure asks for a solution to fermion evolution with the field
{\mathfrak{a}}
regarded as a fixed classical background. This is precisely what is done in §
3.2
above when finding
the fermion scattering states in the presence of an
{\mathfrak{a}}
-dependent boundary condition like (
53
). These are used in §
6
to calculate
scattering rates also for fixed dyon configurations. We return to the issue of how to determine
the dyon reponse in §
6.2
below, but first close this section by gathering together several loose ends and conceptual issues to do with using the PPEFT framework in the monopole/dyon setting.

3.4
Redundant interactions

In this section we return to the puzzle alluded to at the end of §
3.2
above: why are the physical effects of the 16 real parameters in the couplings
{\cal C}^{\scriptscriptstyle A}_{ij}
in the action
S_{\rm dyon2}
all encoded in amplitudes
{\cal T}_{{\rm in}}
(or
{\cal T}_{{\rm out}}
) that involve only 4 parameters. Why are there not more scattering options available given the number of possible effective interactions? Part of the reason for this reduction is the rank-2 condition we assume for
{\cal B}({\mathfrak{a}})
, since – as shown in Appendix
D.2
– this imposes 8 real conditions on the components of
{\cal B}({\mathfrak{a}})
(and so also on the
{\cal C}^{\scriptscriptstyle A}_{ij}
’s). But the fact that the remaining 8 independent effective couplings only produce a 4-parameter number of scattering outcomes strongly suggests that some of the remaining effective couplings are actually redundant (in the precise EFT sense reviewed for example in
[
16
]
).

One way in which an effective operator can be seen to be redundant is if it can be removed using a field redefinition. When working in perturbation theory there is a very simple diagnostic for when such a field redefinition exists: one asks whether the operator vanishes when it is evaluated at the solution to the leading order field equations. In the present instance the fermion field equation at the position
r=\epsilon
is actually just the boundary condition itself, and this suggests a redundancy test that can be performed, at least if the dyon-fermion interactions are treated perturbatively (as they are in §
3.3.1
when perturbing the dyon effective couplings around the action
S^{(0)}_{\rm dyon2}
).

When perturbing in this way we first ask what conditions the
\delta{\cal C}^{\scriptscriptstyle A}_{ij}
’s must satisfy to ensure that the perturbed boundary condition remains rank two. Perturbing the rank-
2
conditions like (
242
) of the appendix to linear order in the
\delta{\cal C}
s shows that
{\cal B}
remains rank two only if the coefficients
\delta{\cal C}^{pv}_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}
and
\delta{\cal C}^{ps}_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}
all vanish. For the remaining couplings the redundancy test then asks how many of the effective couplings in (
87
) survive when simplified using the lowest-order fermion boundary condition
(\Gamma^{1}+i\Gamma_{c}){\bm{\chi}}(\epsilon)=0
that follows from the unperturbed dyon-fermion action (
86
). Notice that because
\Gamma_{c}=\Gamma^{0}\Gamma^{1}
this is equivalent to the condition
i\Gamma^{0}{\bm{\chi}}(\epsilon)={\bm{\chi}}(\epsilon)
and because
\Gamma^{0}
is antihermitian this implies
{\bm{\chi}}^{\dagger}(i\Gamma^{0})={\bm{\chi}}^{\dagger}
when evaluated at
r=\epsilon
, and so also
\overline{\bm{\chi}}=\bm{\chi}^{\dagger}
there. These conditions allow us to rewrite

\overline{\chi}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\epsilon)\chi_{{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}(\epsilon)=\chi^{\dagger}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\epsilon)i\Gamma^{0}\chi_{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}}(\epsilon)=\chi^{\dagger}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\epsilon)\chi_{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}}(\epsilon)=\overline{\chi}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\epsilon)i\Gamma^{0}\chi_{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}}(\epsilon),

(98)

where the first equality uses the definition
{\overline{{\bm{\chi}}}}:=i{\bm{\chi}}^{\dagger}\Gamma^{0}
. This shows that within perturbative calculations the couplings
\delta\mathcal{C}^{s}_{\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}}{\overline{\chi}}_{\mathfrak{s}}\chi_{{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}-i\delta{\cal C}^{v}_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}{\overline{\chi}}_{\mathfrak{s}}\Gamma^{0}\chi_{{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}
appearing in expression (
87
) for
S_{\rm dyon2}^{\rm int}
should only contribute to physical predictions through the combination
\delta\mathcal{C}^{s}_{\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}}-\delta{\cal C}^{v}_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}
.

This is consistent with the more general expressions like (
72
) for the amplitudes
{\cal T}_{\rm in}
and
{\cal T}_{\rm out}
within the Born-Oppenheimer framework, since for rank-2 perturbations about the unperturbed couplings
{\cal C}^{ps}_{++}={\cal C}^{ps}_{--}=1
these give

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm in}}\simeq 1+i(\delta{\cal C}^{v}_{++}-\delta{\cal C}^{s}_{++}),\quad\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}\simeq 1+i(\delta{\cal C}^{v}_{--}-\delta{\cal C}^{s}_{--}),

\displaystyle{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}\simeq i(\delta{\cal C}^{v}_{+-}-\delta{\cal C}^{s}_{+-}),\quad\hbox{and}\quad{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm in}}\simeq i(\delta{\cal C}^{v}_{-+}-\delta{\cal C}^{s}_{-+}),

(99)

showing at linear order that
\delta{\cal C}^{s}_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}
and
\delta{\cal C}^{v}_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}
really do only appear in the combination
\delta{\cal C}^{s}_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}-\delta{\cal C}^{v}_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}
.

These arguments suggest that within the perturbative framework one combination of
\delta{\cal C}^{s}_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}
and
\delta{\cal C}^{v}_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}
is redundant in the sense that it can be removed by performing a field redefinition at the position of the dyon worldline. Indeed, writing

{\overline{{\bm{\chi}}}}{\cal C}^{s}{\bm{\chi}}-i{\overline{{\bm{\chi}}}}{\cal C}^{v}\Gamma^{0}{\bm{\chi}}=\tfrac{1}{2}{\overline{{\bm{\chi}}}}({\cal C}^{s}+{\cal C}^{v})(1-i\Gamma^{0}){\bm{\chi}}+\tfrac{1}{2}{\overline{{\bm{\chi}}}}({\cal C}^{s}-{\cal C}^{v})(1+i\Gamma^{0}){\bm{\chi}}\,,

(100)

we seek a redefinition that removes the first term. But under a small variation
{\bm{\chi}}\to{\bm{\chi}}+\delta{\bm{\chi}}
at
r=\epsilon
the variation of the bulk action and the unperturbed boundary action (
86
) becomes

\delta S(r=\epsilon)=-\tfrac{1}{2}{\overline{{\bm{\chi}}}}\Bigl(-\Gamma^{1}+i\Gamma_{c}\Bigr)\delta{\bm{\chi}}-\tfrac{1}{2}\delta{\overline{{\bm{\chi}}}}\Bigl(\Gamma^{1}+i\Gamma_{c}\Bigr){\bm{\chi}}\,,

(101)

which for
\delta{\bm{\chi}}=iA\,\Gamma_{c}{\bm{\chi}}
(with hermitian
A
in flavour space) gives

\delta S(r=\epsilon)=-\tfrac{1}{2}i\,{\overline{{\bm{\chi}}}}A\Bigl[(-\Gamma^{1}+i\Gamma_{c})\Gamma_{c}+\Gamma_{c}(\Gamma^{1}+i\Gamma_{c})\Bigr]{\bm{\chi}}=\,{\overline{{\bm{\chi}}}}A\Bigl(1-i\Gamma^{0}\Bigr){\bm{\chi}}\,,

(102)

showing that
A
can be chosen to remove the
{\cal C}^{s}+{\cal C}^{v}
term in (
100
), as claimed.

A very similar reduction in the number of independent couplings also happens for more prosaic applications of the PPEFT framework to fermions. When used to describe the influence of a non-pointlike nucleus on electronic energy levels within an atom there turn out to be more effective interactions describing various types of nuclear effective couplings on the nuclear worldline than there are independent nuclear contributions to electronic energy levels. In that case the redundancy of many of the apparent nuclear moments at low energies ensures that nuclear uncertainties enter into atomic calculations in fewer ways than one would naively expect
[
27
,
28
]

4
RG methods and catalysis

We now turn to the issue of
\epsilon
-dependence. The mode functions and scattering amplitudes found above depend in detail on the boundary conditions imposed at the surface
\partial P
of the gaussian pillbox that surrounds each source. But how can it be that physical quantities depend on an arbitrary radius
r=\epsilon
that defines the boundary of this pillbox?

This question is precisely what PPEFT methods are good for: they show how effective couplings like the
{\cal C}^{\scriptscriptstyle A}_{ij}
’s appearing in the dyon’s effective action must depend implicitly on (
i.e.
run with)
\epsilon
in order to ensure that physical predictions are
\epsilon
-independent. A side benefit of this discussion is that it shows how divergences that arise (even at the classical level) in the values of the fields as
\epsilon\to 0
get renormalized into the effective couplings of the dyon action, along the lines described in
[
56
]
. In this language the physical scales of the UV physics – such as
\mu
in the action (
1
) – are described within the EFT as RG-invariant scales associated with this running.

In this section we set up how this works for
S
-wave scattering from the dyon and in particular ask why this running makes scattering for monopoles so different from scattering from other small massive objects like nuclei in atoms (for which the corresponding issues are described in
[
26
]
and briefly summarized in Appendix
B
). We discuss in turn two ways the running of effective couplings of fermions to dyons differ from their couplings to more run-of-the-mill compact objects: (
i
) dimensional scaling changes associated with the kinematics of the fermion
S
-wave (which lies at the root of why dyon-fermion scattering is insensitive to dyon size), and (
ii
) the large-scale effects to do with the nontrivial fermionic interacting vacuum (what is sometimes called the fermion ‘condensate’).

4.1
Scaling for compact objects

The most important difference between fermion-dyon and fermion-nucleus scattering is the size of the interaction rates to which one is led. For fermion-nucleus scattering the RG invariant scale that sets the size of scattering cross sections depends on the nucleus’ small radius.
17
17

17

More precisely, the RG scales expressing the implications of the nuclear strong interactions of pionic atoms, say, are set by the nuclear radius while the nucleus’ electromagnetic effects relevant for electron-nuclear interactions are suppressed relative to this by powers of the fine-structure constant
[
27
,
28
]
.
But for fermion-dyon scattering the
S
-wave cross sections are
not
suppressed by the small dyonic size
R\sim m_{g}^{-1}
even at energies
E\ll m_{g}
. Although the mechanism for this (the special kinematics of
S
-wave scattering) has been well-understood for quite some time
[
8
,
9
]
our discussion here embeds this understanding into the wider PPEFT framework and shows how it can be understood using the same standard EFT reasoning that also applies to nuclei.

Scaling for nuclei

For nuclei the bulk fermion fields satisfy the Dirac equation away from the nucleus (in the ‘bulk’)

(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to8.55695pt{\hfil/\hfil}\hss}D+m)\psi=0\,,

(103)

subject to nucleus-dependent boundary conditions at the surface
r=\epsilon
of some small gaussian pillbox described by an effective action similar to the one described above. The main difference from the dyon case is that the absence of a magnetic monopole implies the total spin
j
is half-integral rather than integral (see Appendix
B
).

For instance, for
r>\epsilon
the parity-even solutions are

\psi=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\psi_{\scriptscriptstyle L}\\
\psi_{\scriptscriptstyle R}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}f(r)\,U^{+}(\theta,\phi)+ig(r)\,U^{-}(\theta,\phi)\\
f(r)\,U^{+}(\theta,\phi)-ig(r)\,U^{-}(\theta,\phi)\end{array}\right)\,,

(104)

and a similar expression exists for parity-odd ones. Here
U^{\pm}
are appropriate spinor harmonics and the radial functions
f(r)
and
g(r)
with mode frequency
\omega
solve the radial equations

f^{\prime}=\left(m+\omega\right)g\quad\hbox{and}\quad g^{\prime}+\frac{2g}{r}=\left(m-\omega\right)f\,,

(105)

where primes denote differentiation with respect to
r
. The general solution to these coupled first-order equations is a linear combination of two linearly independent solutions

f(r)=C_{1}f_{1}(r)+C_{2}f_{2}(r)\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad g(r)=C_{1}g_{1}(r)+C_{2}g_{2}(r)\,,

(106)

where
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are the two expected integration constants.

Physical predictions (
e.g.
for atomic energy levels or scattering cross sections) can be expressed in terms of
C_{2}/C_{1}
and this ratio is in turn determined in terms of nuclear properties through the near-nucleus boundary conditions, which give the ratio of the functions
f
and
g
at
r=\epsilon
in terms of the effective couplings
\hat{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}
for fermion bilinears of the form
\int_{\scriptscriptstyle W}{\rm d}t\;\hat{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}\,{\overline{\psi}}M_{i}\psi
in the nucleus’ world-line action (where
M_{i}
are a basis of Dirac matrices). More precisely, the boundary condition at the surface of a gaussian pillbox centered on the nucleus is – compare with eqs. (
40
) and (
41
):

\hat{\cal C}=\left(\frac{g}{f}\right)_{r=\epsilon}\,.

(107)

Here
\hat{\cal C}
is a linear combination of dimensionless quantities
\hat{\cal C}_{i}=\hat{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}/(4\pi\epsilon^{2})
– compare with (
47
) (see
[
26
]
for details).
Eq. (
107
) provides the solution to how the properties of the source influence the bulk solutions for
\psi
in the source’s vicinity: the couplings
\hat{\cal C}_{i}
determine
(g/f)_{r=\epsilon}
through the near-source boundary condition and this in turn fixes the ratios of integration constants
C_{2}/C_{1}
that control the size of observable phenomena.

But it is still a potential puzzle why physical predictions can depend on the relatively arbitrary radius,
r=\epsilon
, of the Gaussian pillbox, which is just a scale that regularizes the boundary conditions that need not be directly related to the underlying physical scales such as the physical size of the actual compact object within the pillbox (which we denote by
R
). The precise value of
\epsilon
must therefore drop out of predictions for observables. In detail, this happens because any explicit
\epsilon
-dependence arising in calculations of an observable cancels an implicit
\epsilon
-dependence buried within the ‘bare’ quantities
\hat{\cal C}_{i}
. Physical predictions remain
\epsilon
-independent if quantities like
\hat{\cal C}_{i}(\epsilon)
are chosen to be
\epsilon
-dependent in a way that ensures that ratios like
C_{2}/C_{1}
remain fixed as
\epsilon
is varied.

This gives us another way to interpret eq. (
107
): rather than reading it as fixing
f/g
at
r=\epsilon
given fixed values of the
\hat{\cal C}_{i}
we can instead read it as telling us how the
\hat{\cal C}_{i}(\epsilon)
must depend on
\epsilon
in order to ensure that
C_{2}/C_{1}
remains
\epsilon
-independent. Within this rereading physical observables depend only on the
trajectory

(\epsilon,\hat{\cal C}_{i}(\epsilon))
rather than depending on
\epsilon
and
\hat{\cal C}_{i}(\epsilon)
separately. Changes of
\epsilon
with
C_{2}/C_{1}
fixed (and so also with physical observables fixed) can be regarded as defining a renormalization-group (RG) flow of the
\hat{\cal C}_{i}(\epsilon)
’s, and physical observables must be invariant with respect to this flow.

It turns out that this kind of RG flow defines a natural RG-invariant length scale, and it is this length scale that both appears in physical predictions (such as for scattering cross sections) and is determined in terms of physical scales like
R
when matching effective couplings to the full UV theory of the source. To see how this scale arises in terms of radial mode functions like
f(r)
and
g(r)
it is convenient to choose
\epsilon
such that
R\ll\epsilon\ll a
where
a
is a characteristic length scale of the bulk theory far from the source (for instance, for nuclei in atoms we might have
R
of order the nuclear size and
a
of order the Bohr radius). Radial mode functions are often well-approximated by power laws in this regime, with

f(r)=C_{1}\left(\frac{r}{a}\right)^{{\mathfrak{z}}-1}+C_{2}\left(\frac{r}{a}\right)^{-{\mathfrak{z}}-1}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad g(r)=\widetilde{C}_{1}\left(\frac{r}{a}\right)^{{\mathfrak{z}}-1}+\widetilde{C}_{2}\left(\frac{r}{a}\right)^{-{\mathfrak{z}}-1}\,,

(108)

for some power
18
18

18

Within atoms, for instance, the relevant power is
{\mathfrak{z}}=[(j+\tfrac{1}{2})^{2}-(Z\alpha)^{2}]^{1/2}
.

{\mathfrak{z}}
with
\widetilde{C}_{i}\propto C_{i}
in a way that depends on the relative small-
r
asymptotic behaviour of
f_{i}(r)
and
g_{i}(r)
. One of these solutions dominates for sufficiently small
r
while the other wins when
r
is sufficiently big. The precise crossover radius
R_{\star}
between these two regimes depends only on the value of the constant
C_{2}/C_{1}
, and so provides a convenient RG-invariant characterization of the coupling evolution, and one typically finds
e.g.
scattering cross sections with bulk fields of size
\sigma\sim\pi R_{\star}^{2}
for scattering of long-wavelength modes (
kR_{\star}\ll 1
) despite couplings like
\hat{\cal C}_{i}
being dimensionless – see
[
26
]
.

S
-wave scaling for dyons and catalysis

With the above story in mind we can now use the same EFT language to see why
S
-wave scattering from dyons is so different from scattering from other compact objects. The key issue is not whether couplings like
\hat{\cal C}_{i}
are dimensionless or not (they are dimensionless for both nuclei and dyons). The key issue is the size to be expected for RG-invariant scales like
R_{\star}
.

The main issue is the difference between eqs. (
105
) for nuclei and (
26
) for dyons. For nuclei (
105
) has two linearly independent solutions and so admits two different power-law type asymptotic forms like (
108
) the transition between which defines the RG-invariant scale
R_{\star}
. But for dyons the
S
-wave condition removes one of these solutions leaving just a single first-order equation (
108
) for each choice of external quantum numbers. This means there is never a transition between two asymptotic power-law regimes; there is only one power-law for each type of mode. As a result there is no RG-invariant scale
R_{\star}
on which measureable things like scattering cross sections can depend.

For
S
-wave scattering from dyons the situation is similar to what would have happened for nucleons if for some reason we were required to choose
C_{2}=0
. In this case using the asymptotic form (
108
) in (
107
) would imply that
\hat{\cal C}
is
\epsilon
-independent. The
\epsilon
-independence of physical quantities for
S
-wave dyons similarly requires quantities like
{\cal T}_{\rm in}
or
{\cal T}_{\rm out}
to be
\epsilon
-independent (as we see below explicitly), and this asks all of the dimensionless rank-two couplings
{\cal C}_{i}
to themselves directly be
\epsilon
-independent. This makes
S
-wave dyon scattering from massless fermions scale invariant and so its size is mainly set by the projection of any incoming wave onto the
S
-wave state, leading to cross sections that vary as
\sigma\propto\pi/k^{2}
when
kR\ll 1
rather than
\sigma\propto\pi R^{2}
(as we verify explicitly below). This is true for any
S
-wave process regardless of whether or not the reaction in question violates a flavour symmetry (like baryon number).

Arguments like these relying on the uniqueness of the
S
-wave kinematics are standard ones
[
8
,
9
]
for explaining the large size of catalysis cross sections. What the above arguments do is provide them with an EFT veneer that shows why they do not undermine the usual notions of decoupling (once these are carefully formulated).

Matching

To this point the effective couplings
{\cal C}_{i}
and amplitudes
{\cal T}_{\rm in}
have been treated as arbitrary parameters. But they really should be regarded as being functions of microscopic parameters in any specific theory and so take definite values once a given microscopic dyon construction is chosen. Physical predictions for things like fermion-dyon scattering cross sections within specific microscopic theories are then obtained by substituting the appropriate
values for the
{\cal C}_{i}
’s (or
{\cal T}_{\rm in}
) into the general expressions for
e.g.
scattering cross sections given in §
5
and §
6
below.

For simple calculations of semiclassical scattering of massless fermions moving in a classical dyon field chirality is conserved by the Dirac equation. As discussed below eq. (
26
) the change of direction of radial motion required by
S
-wave scattering implies
{\mathfrak{h}}={\mathfrak{c}}{\mathfrak{s}}
must change sign during scattering and so conservation of
{\mathfrak{c}}
implies
{\mathfrak{s}}
must change. This means that fermion charge is always exchanged with the dyon and so
{\cal T}_{\rm in}^{++}={\cal T}_{\rm in}^{--}=0
for this type of semiclassical scattering. This then implies –
c.f.
the unitarity conditions (
73
) –
{{\cal T}}^{-+}_{{\rm in}}=e^{-i\delta},{{\cal T}}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}=e^{i\delta^{\prime}}
for some phases
\delta,\delta^{\prime}
. This is the situation that applies to the majority of microscopic fermion scattering calculations (where typically
\delta^{\prime}=\delta
) performed in the presence of a classical dyon
[
32
,
48
,
49
,
33
]
.

Alternatively Kazama
et.al.

[
53
]
compute scattering processes where the fermion moving within the dyonic background has an anomalous magnetic moment. In this case chirality is not conserved and chirality-changing processes dominate, corresponding to the case
\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm in}}=\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}=i\,\text{sign}(\kappa)
(where
\kappa
is a parameter of their model) and
\mathcal{T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}=\mathcal{T}^{-+}_{{\rm in}}=0
. In both chirality-preserving and chirality-breaking cases our expressions for cross sections and currents found in later sections agree with theirs once restricted to these choices.

4.2
Interaction effects

In practice the motion of a free fermion within a fixed dyonic background does not provide a good approximation to fermion-dyon scattering. The free-fermion-moving-in-a-fixed-background approximation breaks down because charge-changing fermion interactions with the rotor field
{\mathfrak{a}}
described by the amplitudes
{\cal T}_{\rm in}^{+-}
and
{\cal T}_{\rm in}^{-+}
significantly distort the ground state within the fermionic sector (more about this in §
6.1
below) and this distortion cannot be neglected
[
8
,
9
,
34
,
35
,
36
,
37
,
39
]
. The radial extent of the fermionic vacuum polarization can extend outside the dyon to distances of order the fermion Compton wavelength and so can be much larger than the underlying classical dyon solution itself.

The back-reaction of this kind of dynamics can appreciably alter the RG flow of couplings like the
{\cal C}_{i}
’s once
\epsilon
is large enough to include a significant component of fermionic polarization within the gaussian pillbox. In this case it is the new values for
{\cal C}_{i}(\epsilon)
that are relevant when computing quantities like
{\cal T}_{\rm in}
and the above conclusion that
{\cal T}_{\rm in}
’s are
\epsilon
-independent changes. The results of this type of evolution are studied in
[
8
,
9
,
34
,
39
]
, and for general models the full interpretation of the resulting physics remains incomplete (see for example
[
21
,
22
]
for recent discussions). In the specific model considered here, however, the upshot is fairly simple: the Coulomb energies associated with the fermionic vacuum distortions turn out to convert the UV semiclassical prediction
{\cal T}_{\rm in}^{++}={\cal T}_{\rm in}^{--}=0
for
\epsilon
of order the monopole scale into the new prediction
\mathcal{T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}=\mathcal{T}^{-+}_{{\rm in}}=0
for
\epsilon
large enough to include the fermionic vacuum distortions
[
39
]
. This conversion is intuitive inasmuch as the underlying distortion of the fermionic vacuum is driven by nonzero
{\cal T}_{\rm in}^{+-}
and
{\cal T}_{\rm in}^{-+}
(as we see in §
6
below)

More generally the coefficients appropriate to any other particular microscopic dyon construction can in principle be obtained in a similar way by matching the EFT to the microscopic theory of interest, once this is known. When doing this matching we typically choose
\epsilon
such that
R\ll\epsilon\ll a
. For the simplest applications
R\sim m_{g}^{-1}
is of order the dyon size, but for applications including fermion condensation in the dyon field
R
is instead of order the fermion’s Compton wavelength
R\sim m_{\psi}^{-1}
. In either case taking
R\ll\epsilon\ll a
remains justified provided the low-energy focus is on sufficiently large
a
.

5
Perturbative calculations

We next turn to calculating some simple dyon-fermion processes as functions of the general effective couplings described above. We do so first in this section treating the fermion-dyon interactions perturbatively, and then in the next section repeat the process within the Born-Oppenheimer approximation (which works to all orders in the fermion-dyon interactions when the rotor field
{\mathfrak{a}}
is treated classically).

We start by computing several reaction rates treating the dyon-fermion interactions perturbatively, within the framework described in §
3.3.1
above. This choice is just made for convenience to explore some perturbative consquences of the fermion-dyon interactions, and might not be the dominant reaction for particular microscopic descriptions of the underlying dyon. We return to the more broadly applicable general case without this perturbative approximation in §
6
below.

The dyon-localized part of the hamiltonian governing dyon-fermion interactions obtained from the lagrangian (
40
) (and including the electrostatic background and fluctuation field) is

H_{\rm dyon}=e{\mathfrak{p}}\,\widehat{A}_{0}(r=\epsilon)+\frac{1}{2{\cal I}}\left({\mathfrak{p}}-\frac{\vartheta}{2\pi}\right)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\Bigl[{\overline{\bm{\psi}}}\,{\mathfrak{C}}({\mathfrak{a}})\,{\bm{\psi}}\Bigr]_{r=\epsilon}

(109)

where
{\mathfrak{p}}
is the canonical momentum for
{\mathfrak{a}}
given in (
42
). In this section we drop the Coulomb fluctuation
\widehat{A}_{0}
and perturb the fermion boundary action about a simple
{\mathfrak{a}}
-independent boundary action
along the lines described in §
3.3.1
. Specializing to the
S
-wave and switching to 2D fields, we split the bulk and boundary-localized hamiltonian into unperturbed and perturbed parts,
H_{0}+H_{\rm int}
. The unperturbed hamiltonian is
19
19

19

We follow standard practice here and keep the rotor kinetic term despite it being order
(e^{2}/4\pi)\mu
in magnitude and so nominally being a higher-loop size.

\displaystyle H_{0}=\frac{1}{2{\cal I}}\left({\mathfrak{p}}-\frac{\vartheta}{2\pi}\right)^{2}+\frac{i}{2}\sum_{{\mathfrak{s}}=\pm}\left({\overline{{\chi}}}_{{\mathfrak{s}}}\Gamma_{c}\chi_{{\mathfrak{s}}}\right)_{r=\epsilon}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{{\mathfrak{s}}=\pm}\int^{\infty}_{\epsilon}{\rm d}r\,{\overline{{\chi}}}_{{\mathfrak{s}}}\left[\Gamma^{1}\overset{\leftrightarrow}{\partial}_{1}-i{\mathfrak{s}}\,\Gamma^{0}\left(ev-\frac{eQ}{r}\right)\right]\chi_{{\mathfrak{s}}},

(110)

in which the first term describes the free rotor dynamics and the second term gives the boundary conditions at
r=\epsilon
satisfied by the fermions, whose bulk dynamics in the presence of the background dyon charge is given by the last term.

The unperturbed boundary term
\frac{i}{2}\left({\overline{\bm{\chi}}}\Gamma_{c}\bm{\chi}\right)_{r=\epsilon}
is chosen such that (
i
) it does not involve the rotor field
{\mathfrak{a}}
; (
ii
) the corresponding boundary matrix
{\cal B}^{(0)}
has rank
2
and (
iii
) the resulting modes describe fermion reflection from the dyon with no phase change at
r=\epsilon
: that is,
{\cal T}^{++}={\cal T}^{--}=1
to zeroeth order in perturbation theory. This choice implies the interaction picture field
\bm{\chi}
satisfies the boundary condition (
89
) and can be expanded in terms of either the modes
{\mathfrak{u}}^{\rm in0}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k},{\mathfrak{v}}^{\rm in0}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}
or
{\mathfrak{u}}^{\rm out0}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k},{\mathfrak{v}}^{\rm out0}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}
, given in equations (
90
)-(
93
).

The interaction hamiltonian in the interaction picture is then given by

H_{\rm int}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{{\mathfrak{s}},{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}=\pm}\overline{\chi}_{{\mathfrak{s}}}\Bigl(\delta\mathcal{C}^{s}_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}+i\delta\mathcal{C}^{ps}_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}\Gamma_{c}-i\delta\mathcal{C}^{v}_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}\Gamma^{0}+i\delta\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}\Gamma^{1}\Bigr)\,\chi_{{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}\;e^{\frac{i}{2}({\mathfrak{s}}-{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime})\,{\mathfrak{a}}_{\scriptscriptstyle I}}\,,

(111)

where the fermion fields are evaluated at
r=\epsilon
and
{\mathfrak{a}}_{\scriptscriptstyle I}
is the interaction picture rotor field

{\mathfrak{a}}_{\scriptscriptstyle I}(t):=\;e^{\frac{i}{2\mathcal{I}}\Pi^{2}t}{\mathfrak{a}}\,e^{-\frac{i}{2\mathcal{I}}\Pi^{2}t}\,,

(112)

with
\Pi:={\mathfrak{p}}-(\vartheta/2\pi)
. As discussed in §
3.4
this perturbed problem also has rank two only when
\delta{\cal C}^{pv}_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}=\delta{\cal C}^{ps}_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}=0
, which we henceforth assume (though this is not crucial for this perturbative discussion). In terms of creation and annihilation operators eq. (
111
) can be written in the normal-ordered form

\displaystyle H_{\rm int}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E_{0}+\sum_{\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}=\pm}\int^{\infty}_{0}\frac{\mathrm{d}k\,\mathrm{d}k^{\prime}}{2\pi}\,(\delta\mathcal{C}^{s}_{\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}}-\delta\mathcal{C}^{v}_{\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}})\Big[({a}^{{\rm in}}_{\mathfrak{s},k})^{\star}{a}^{{\rm in}}_{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime},k^{\prime}}e^{i(k-k^{\prime})t}+({a}^{{\rm in}}_{\mathfrak{s},k})^{\star}(\overline{{a}}^{{\rm in}}_{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime},k^{\prime}})^{\star}e^{i(k+k^{\prime})t}

(113)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\overline{{a}}^{{\rm in}}_{\mathfrak{s},k}{{a}}^{{\rm in}}_{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime},k^{\prime}}e^{-i(k+k^{\prime})t}-(\overline{{a}}^{{\rm in}}_{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime},k^{\prime}})^{\star}\overline{{a}}^{{\rm in}}_{\mathfrak{s},k}e^{-i(k-k^{\prime})t}\Big]\;e^{\frac{i}{2\mathcal{I}}\Pi^{2}t}e^{\frac{i}{2}(\mathfrak{s}-\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})({\mathfrak{a}}-evt)}e^{-\frac{i}{2\mathcal{I}}\Pi^{2}t}

where we absorb an
r_{0}
-dependent phase using an appropriate constant shift of
{\mathfrak{a}}
. We also drop factors of
e^{i(k\pm k^{\prime})\epsilon}
because our EFT framework requires we choose
\epsilon
to be smaller than the characteristic bulk length scales of interest, as described in §
4
. In particular this requires us to restrict our attention to the regime where
k\epsilon
,
k^{\prime}\epsilon
and
ev\epsilon
are all much smaller than unity.

In (
113
)
E_{0}
denotes the vacuum expectation value of the interacting hamiltonian
H_{\rm int}
, given by

E_{0}:=\bra{0}H_{\rm int}\ket{0}=\frac{eQ}{4\pi\epsilon}(\delta\mathcal{C}^{s}_{--}-\delta\mathcal{C}^{v}_{--}-\delta\mathcal{C}^{s}_{++}+\delta\mathcal{C}^{v}_{++}),

(114)

as shown in Appendix
G
. Although this diverges as
\epsilon\to 0
it can be absorbed into the counterterm describing the mass of the dyon. Notice these expressions depend on the boundary couplings only through the combination
\delta{\cal C}^{s}-\delta{\cal C}^{v}
, as argued must be the case in §
3.4
.

The remainder of this section uses the above setup to calculate some scattering observables within this perturbative framework.

5.1
Processes involving dyon charge eigenstates

We start by recording the amplitudes for single-fermion scattering processes assuming the dyon is chosen to be in a charge –
i.e.
momentum – eigenstate at both the initial and final times and working to first order in perturbation theory. By focusing here on single-particle scattering we avoid the complications of multiparticle state-definitions in a monopole background described in
[
19
,
20
]
and the twist operators described in
[
21
,
22
]
.

5.1.1
Pair production

Among the charge-changing processes mediated at leading order by (
113
) is the production or absorption of particle-antiparticle pairs carrying net charge. The amplitude obtained at leading order from (
113
) for the production of a pair of positive charge –
i.e.
for
\ket{0}\ket{{\Pi}}\rightarrow({a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,k})^{\star}(\overline{a}^{{\rm out}}_{-,k^{\prime}})^{\star}\ket{0}\ket{{\Pi}^{\prime}}
– is

\displaystyle{\cal A}\Bigl[\Pi\to\Pi^{\prime}+f_{+}(k)+\bar{f}_{-}(k^{\prime})\Bigr]=-\delta_{{\Pi}^{\prime},{\Pi}+1}\delta\left(\omega_{+,k}+{\overline{\omega}}_{-,k^{\prime}}+\frac{\Pi^{\prime 2}-\Pi^{2}}{2\mathcal{I}}\right)i\left(\delta\mathcal{C}^{s}_{+-}-\delta\mathcal{C}^{v}_{+-}\right),

(115)

where
\omega_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}=k-\frac{1}{2}{\mathfrak{s}}ev
is the energy of a particle with charge
{\mathfrak{s}}e/2
and momentum
k
and
\overline{\omega}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}=k+\frac{1}{2}{\mathfrak{s}}ev
is the energy of an antiparticle with charge
-{\mathfrak{s}}{e}/2
and momentum
k
.

The amplitude for producing a negatively charged pair –
i.e.
for
\ket{0}\ket{{\Pi}}\rightarrow({a}^{{\rm out}}_{-,k})^{\star}(\overline{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,k^{\prime}})^{\star}\ket{0}\ket{{\Pi}^{\prime}}
– similarly is

\displaystyle{\cal A}\Bigl[\Pi\to\Pi^{\prime}+f_{-}(k)+\bar{f}_{+}(k^{\prime})\Bigr]=-\delta_{{\Pi}^{\prime},{\Pi}-1}\delta\left(\omega_{-,k}+{\overline{\omega}}_{+,k^{\prime}}+\frac{\Pi^{\prime 2}-\Pi^{2}}{2\mathcal{I}}\right)i\left(\delta\mathcal{C}^{s}_{-+}-\delta\mathcal{C}^{v}_{-+}\right).

(116)

These show that the rotor level can only change by one unit, as required for it to absorb or emit the charge lost or gained by the fermions. The fermion similarly gains or loses the energy required by this transition. These processes cause rotor states with nonzero
\Pi
to decay towards
\Pi=0
by emitting fermion pairs. It is energetically possible to emit positively charged pairs when
k+k^{\prime}=ev-\frac{1+2\Pi}{2{\cal I}}\geq 0
and so is only possible when
\Pi\leq{\cal I}ev-\frac{1}{2}
. It is similarly possible to emit negatively charged pairs if
k+k^{\prime}=-ev-\frac{1-2\Pi}{2{\cal I}}\geq 0
and this is only possible when
\Pi\geq{\cal I}ev+\frac{1}{2}
. No pair production occurs for electrically neutral particle-antiparticle pairs since energy conservation implies this can only happen if
k=k^{\prime}=0
.

Writing
{\cal A}={\cal M}\,\delta(E_{f}-E_{i})
and using these amplitudes in Fermi’s golden rule gives the following differential decay rate

\mathrm{d}\Gamma=2\pi|{\cal M}|^{2}\,\delta(E_{f}-E_{i})\,\frac{{\rm d}k}{2\pi}\,\frac{{\rm d}k^{\prime}}{2\pi}\,.

(117)

Using this and performing the final-state momentum integrals, the integrated rate for emitting positively charged fermions is nonzero for initial dyon momenta satisfying
\Pi<{\cal I}ev-\frac{1}{2}\sim(4\pi/e)(v/\mu)
, and is given by

\Gamma\Bigl[\Pi\to(\Pi+1)+f_{+}(k)+\bar{f}_{-}(k^{\prime})\Bigr]=\frac{1}{2\pi}\left(ev-\frac{2\Pi+1}{2{\cal I}}\right)|\delta{\cal C}^{s}_{+-}-\delta{\cal C}^{v}_{+-}|^{2}\,.

(118)

The integrated rate for emitting negatively charged pairs is similarly nonzero when
\Pi>{\cal I}ev+\frac{1}{2}
with

\Gamma\Bigl[\Pi\to(\Pi-1)+f_{-}(k)+\bar{f}_{+}(k^{\prime})\Bigr]=\frac{1}{2\pi}\left(-ev+\frac{2\Pi-1}{2{\cal I}}\right)|\delta{\cal C}^{s}_{-+}-\delta{\cal C}^{v}_{-+}|^{2}\,.

(119)

5.1.2
Scattering cross sections

The Hamiltonian (
113
) also describes scattering processes. The amplitude for the charge-changing process
({a}^{{\rm in}}_{+,k})^{\star}\ket{0}\ket{{\Pi}}\rightarrow({a}^{{\rm out}}_{-,k^{\prime}})^{\star}\ket{0}\ket{{\Pi}^{\prime}}
is given by

{\cal A}\Bigl[f_{+}(k)+\Pi\to\Pi^{\prime}+f_{-}(k^{\prime})\Bigr]=-\delta_{{\Pi}^{\prime},{\Pi}-1}\delta\left(\omega_{-,k^{\prime}}-\omega_{+,k}+\frac{{\Pi^{\prime}}^{2}-\Pi^{2}}{2\mathcal{I}}\right)\,i\left(\delta\mathcal{C}^{s}_{-+}-\delta\mathcal{C}^{v}_{-+}\right),

(120)

where (as before)
\omega_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}=k-{\mathfrak{s}}\frac{ev}{2}
. Similarly the amplitude for
({a}^{{\rm in}}_{-,k})^{\star}\ket{0}\ket{{\Pi}}\rightarrow({a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,k^{\prime}})^{\star}\ket{0}\ket{{\Pi}^{\prime}}
is

{\cal A}\Bigl[f_{-}(k)+\Pi\to\Pi^{\prime}+f_{+}(k^{\prime})\Bigr]=-\delta_{{\Pi}^{\prime},{\Pi}+1}\delta\left(\omega_{+,k^{\prime}}-\omega_{-,k}+\frac{{\Pi^{\prime}}^{2}-\Pi^{2}}{2\mathcal{I}}\right)\,i\left(\delta\mathcal{C}^{s}_{+-}-\delta\mathcal{C}^{v}_{+-}\right).

(121)

The analogous amplitudes for charge-changing antiparticle transitions can be inferred from those above by crossing symmetry. These reactions can proceed so long as the initial fermion energy satisfies
\omega_{i}\geq\frac{1}{2{\cal I}}({\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}2\Pi+1)-{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}\frac{ev}{2}
, where
{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}
is the charge of the final particle, since
\omega_{f}\geq-{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}\frac{ev}{2}
for single particle
scattering.

The effective interactions in (
113
) also describe scattering processes that do not exchange charge or energy with the dyon (and so necessarily flip the
4
D chirality).
The amplitude for an incoming positively charged particle to scatter to an outgoing particle of the same charge is

{\cal A}\Bigl[f_{+}(k)+\Pi\to\Pi^{\prime}+f_{+}(k^{\prime})\Bigr]=-\delta_{{\Pi}^{\prime},{\Pi}}\,\delta\left(\omega_{+,k^{\prime}}-\omega_{+,k}\right)i\Bigl[\,\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}{\rm d}t\,\bra{0}H_{\rm int}\ket{0}+\left(\delta\mathcal{C}^{s}_{++}-\delta\mathcal{C}^{v}_{++}\right)\Bigr],

(122)

where we omit the zeroth order term in the perturbative expansion of the
S
-matrix. The above expression includes a contribution from the vacuum survival amplitude, given in terms of
the vacuum expectation value of
H_{\rm int}
. Although we formally include this in amplitudes such as (
122
) and (
123
) below, we are primarily interested in
inclusive
scattering processes in which the number of pairs produced by the dyon is unmeasured, as explained in more detail in §
6
. In that case, the vacuum survival amplitude, which describes a process in which no pairs are produced, drops out of physical predictions such as cross section results. The corresponding amplitude for a negatively charged incoming particle is

{\cal A}\Bigl[f_{-}(k)+\Pi\to\Pi^{\prime}+f_{-}(k^{\prime})\Bigr]=-\delta_{{\Pi}^{\prime},{\Pi}}\delta\left(\omega_{-,k^{\prime}}-\omega_{-,k}\right)i\Bigl[\,\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}{\rm d}t\,\bra{0}H_{\rm int}\ket{0}+\left(\delta\mathcal{C}^{s}_{--}-\delta\mathcal{C}^{v}_{--}\right)\Bigr].

(123)

The amplitudes for transitions between antiparticles of the same charge can be calculated similarly.

Fermion-dyon scattering reactions are most usefully described in terms of 4D cross sections rather than 2D scattering rates, so we pause to make the connection to these explicit. Because incoming initial states in 4D are plane waves far from the dyon, they are not prepared in the
S
-wave. Their scattering rates are therefore the product of the 2D
S
-wave scattering rate times the probability,
p_{s}
, of finding the incoming plane-wave in the
S
-wave. The 4D cross section then is obtained by dividing by the incoming 4D particle flux
{\mathfrak{F}}_{i}
.

Combining these factors leads to the following factorized expression for the 4D single-particle
S
-wave differential cross section
{\rm d}\sigma_{s}
computed using the above amplitudes with
{\cal A}={\cal M}\,\delta(E_{f}-E_{i})
:

{\rm d}\sigma_{s}\Bigl[f_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{c}}}(k)+\Pi\to\Pi^{\prime}+f_{{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}}(k^{\prime})\Bigr]=\frac{p_{s}}{{\mathfrak{F}}_{i}}\;{\rm d}\Gamma_{2}=\frac{\pi}{k^{2}}\,\delta_{{\mathfrak{s}},{\mathfrak{c}}}\delta_{{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime},-{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}}\,|{\cal M}|^{2}\,\delta(E_{f}-E_{i})\,{\rm d}k^{\prime}\,,

(124)

where the factors
p_{s}
and
{\mathfrak{F}}_{i}
are computed explicitly in Appendix
E
, where we also show how the
2
D rates are calculated. The factor
\delta_{{\mathfrak{s}},{\mathfrak{c}}}
ensures the result is nonzero only when
{\mathfrak{s}}={\mathfrak{c}}
corresponding to the observation made below eq. (
28
) that
{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{c}}=+1
for any incoming
S
-wave fermion. Similarly
{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}=-1
for any outgoing fermion.
20
20

20

We restore the chirality label
{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}
on the final
S
-wave state to make all the quantum numbers explicit in
{\rm d}\sigma_{s}
Notice the proportionality to
1/k^{2}
ensures the cross section scales with energy as does the unitarity bound (so long as the fermion mass is negligible).

For instance, using the amplitudes for fermion scattering given
above we find the charge-changing cross section to be

{\rm d}\sigma_{s}\Bigl[f_{++}(k)+\Pi\rightarrow\Pi^{\prime}+f_{-+}(k^{\prime})\Bigr]=\frac{\pi}{k^{2}}\,\delta_{\Pi^{\prime},\Pi-1}\delta\left(k^{\prime}-k+ev+\frac{1-2\Pi}{2{\cal I}}\right)|\delta{\cal C}^{s}_{-+}-\delta{\cal C}^{v}_{-+}|^{2}\,{{\rm d}k^{\prime}}\,.

(125)

Integrating over the final fermion momentum and marginalizing over the unmeasured final dyon momentum then gives the total charge-changing cross section

\sigma_{s}\Bigl[f_{++}(k)+\Pi\rightarrow(\Pi-1)+f_{-+}(k^{\prime})\Bigr]=\Theta\left(k-ev+\frac{2\Pi-1}{2{\cal I}}\right)\,\frac{\pi}{k^{2}}\,|\,\delta{\cal C}^{s}_{-+}-\delta{\cal C}^{v}_{-+}|^{2}\,.

(126)

In the same way for the charge-changing
f_{-\,-}(k)+\Pi\rightarrow(\Pi+1)+f_{+-}(k^{\prime})
transition, we get

\displaystyle\sigma_{s}\Bigl[f_{-\,-}(k)+\Pi\rightarrow(\Pi+1)+f_{+-}(k^{\prime})\Bigr]=\Theta\left(k+ev-\frac{2\Pi+1}{2{\cal I}}\right)\frac{\pi}{k^{2}}|\delta{\cal C}^{s}_{+-}-\delta{\cal C}^{v}_{+-}|^{2}.

(127)

Similar expressions can be found for the cross section for processes where the
4
D chirality of the fermions changes but not their charge (such as the amplitudes (
122
) and (
123
)) but we do not provide them explicitly here because they depend more sensitively on our initial choice of unperturbed boundary conditions. Expressions for these processes are instead derived in §
6
below using the Born-Oppenheimer approximation.

5.2
Transitions between dyon field eigenstates

For the purposes of comparing with Born-Oppenheimer results in §
6
it is worth computing the same processes as above but this time choosing the initial and final rotor states to be eigenstates of
{\mathfrak{a}}
rather than
{\mathfrak{p}}
or
\Pi
. Strictly speaking, the interaction picture operator

{\mathfrak{a}}_{\scriptscriptstyle I}(t)={\mathfrak{a}}+\frac{\Pi\,t}{{\cal I}}\,,

(128)

is not conserved and so its eigenstates need not agree at different times. However because
{\cal I}^{-1}\sim\alpha\mu\ll\mu
in the semiclassical limit, it is a good approximation to neglect the time-dependence of
{\mathfrak{a}}
and so amplitudes for transitions between
{\mathfrak{a}}
eigenstates simplify considerably, because
{\mathfrak{a}}
is approximately conserved.

5.2.1
Pair production

For instance, for static
{\mathfrak{a}}
the amplitude to produce a particle-antiparticle pair of positive charge is found by taking the matrix element of (
111
), leading to

{\cal A}\Bigl[{\mathfrak{a}}\to{\mathfrak{a}}^{\prime}+f_{+}(k)+\bar{f}_{-}(k^{\prime})\Bigr]\simeq-\delta_{{{\mathfrak{a}}}^{\prime}{{\mathfrak{a}}}}\delta\left(\omega_{+,k}+{\overline{\omega}}_{-,k^{\prime}}\right)e^{i{\mathfrak{a}}}\,i\left(\delta\mathcal{C}^{s}_{+-}-\delta\mathcal{C}^{v}_{+-}\right).

(129)

The corresponding amplitude for producing a negatively charged pair vanishes in this approximation because the energy conservation condition now implies
k+k^{\prime}+ev=0
, which is never satisfied for
k,k^{\prime}\geq 0
,
ev>0
. As we shall see, the above amplitudes exactly match the ones listed in §
6
below, once these are evaluated in the perturbative regime.

The resulting total rate for producing fermion pairs (marginalized over their unmeasured quantum numbers and the dyon final state) then is

\Gamma\Bigl[{\mathfrak{a}}\to{\mathfrak{a}}^{\prime}+f_{+}(k)+\bar{f}_{-}(k^{\prime})\Bigr]\simeq\frac{ev}{2\pi}\Bigl|\delta{\cal C}^{s}_{+-}-\delta{\cal C}^{v}_{+-}\Bigr|^{2}.

(130)

5.2.2
Scattering

The
S
-wave amplitudes for charge-changing fermion scattering in the same static-
{\mathfrak{a}}
limit are

{\cal A}\Bigl[f_{+}(k)+{\mathfrak{a}}\to{\mathfrak{a}}^{\prime}+f_{-}(k^{\prime})\Bigr]=-\delta_{{\mathfrak{a}}^{\prime}{\mathfrak{a}}}\delta\left(\omega_{-,k^{\prime}}-\omega_{+,k}\right)e^{-i{\mathfrak{a}}}\,i\left(\delta\mathcal{C}^{s}_{-+}-\delta\mathcal{C}^{v}_{-+}\right),

(131)

and

{\cal A}\Bigl[f_{-}(k)+{\mathfrak{a}}\to{\mathfrak{a}}^{\prime}+f_{+}(k^{\prime})\Bigr]=-\delta_{{{\mathfrak{a}}}^{\prime}{\mathfrak{a}}}\delta\left(\omega_{+,k^{\prime}}-\omega_{-,k}\right)e^{i{\mathfrak{a}}}\,i\left(\delta\mathcal{C}^{s}_{+-}-\delta\mathcal{C}^{v}_{+-}\right).

(132)

The corresponding total total cross section for charge-exchange processes in which the dyon remains in an
{\mathfrak{a}}
eigenstate then is

\sigma_{s}\Bigl[f_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}}(k)+{\mathfrak{a}}\to{\mathfrak{a}}+f_{-{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}}(k^{\prime})\Bigr]=\Theta\left(k-{\mathfrak{s}}ev\right)\,\frac{\pi}{k^{2}}\,\Bigl|\delta{\cal C}^{s}_{-{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}}-\delta{\cal C}^{v}_{-{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}}\Bigr|^{2}\,.

(133)

Notice that both pair production and scattering involve only a fairly simple condition on
k
(if any) as opposed to the fairly complicated restrictions on
\Pi
that arose when the dyon was prepared in a charge eigenstate. This relative simplicity arises because the
{\mathfrak{a}}
eigenstate always has an overlap with
\Pi
eigenstates for which the processes are energetically allowed. The
{\mathfrak{a}}
-eigenstate expressions are easier to compare with the Born-Oppenheimer, and agree within their common domain of validity with the cross sections found in §
6
.

6
Born-Oppenheimer approximation

We next compute the pair-production and scattering implied by the full mode functions described within the Born-Oppenheimer approximation of §
3.2
, for which the fermions are quantized with the bosonic field
{\mathfrak{a}}
initially treated as a classical field. We start in §
6.1
by examining how the fast degrees of freedom (the fermions) evolve in the presence of a static classical rotor field
{\mathfrak{a}}
, then continue in §
6.2
with some observations about the rotor’s response.

For these purposes recall that dyon interactions with
S
-wave fermions are described by the hamiltonian
H=H_{\rm dyon}+H_{\rm bulk}
that is the sum of the dyon-localized terms of (
109
), reproduced for convenience here,
21
21

21

We follow standard practice here and keep the rotor kinetic term despite it being order
(e^{2}/4\pi)m_{g}
in magnitude and so nominally being higher-loop in size. It can make sense to do so to the extent that all of the rotor’s responses arise at this same order (and are included).

H_{\rm dyon}=\frac{1}{2{\cal I}}\left[{\mathfrak{p}}(t)-\frac{\vartheta}{2\pi}\right]^{2}+e{\mathfrak{p}}\,\widehat{A}_{0}(r=\epsilon,t)+\frac{1}{2}\Bigl[{\overline{\bm{\chi}}}\,O_{\cal B}({\mathfrak{a}})\,{\bm{\chi}}\Bigr]_{r=\epsilon,t}

(134)

where
O_{\cal B}({\mathfrak{a}})
is given in terms of the couplings
{\cal C}_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}^{\scriptscriptstyle A}
by (
51
) and the bulk 2D hamiltonian is

\displaystyle H_{\rm bulk}=H_{\scriptscriptstyle C}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{{\mathfrak{s}}=\pm}\int^{\infty}_{\epsilon}{\rm d}r\,{\overline{{\chi}}}_{{\mathfrak{s}}}\left[\Gamma^{1}\overset{\leftrightarrow}{\partial}_{1}-i{\mathfrak{s}}\,\Gamma^{0}\left(ev-\frac{eQ}{r}+e\widehat{A}_{0}\right)\right]\chi_{{\mathfrak{s}}}\,,

(135)

with
H_{\scriptscriptstyle C}
denoting the part of the Maxwell action depending on the Coulomb field
\widehat{A}_{0}
. We follow previous work and perturb in the Coulomb interactions involving
\widehat{A}_{0}
, whose contributions to the energy are suppressed by powers of
e^{2}
. We differ from the warm-up calculations of §
5
by
not
splitting
O_{\cal B}({\mathfrak{a}})
into an unperturbed and perturbed piece; instead treating the interaction with
{\mathfrak{a}}
using Born-Oppenheimer methods (as motivated in §
3.3.2
). In practice this means that we treat
{\mathfrak{a}}
as a fixed classical field when determining the
fermionic response and then return to ask how this slower rotor field evolves in response to interactions with the faster fermions.

6.1
Fermion evolution

We start by calculating the Bogoliubov coefficients that relate
in
and
out
modes, neglecting the Coulomb back-reaction of the distortions of the fermion ground state. Subsequent subsections then consider some of the implications of this fermionic distortion such as to pair-production rates and scattering cross sections.

6.1.1
Bogoliubov relations

The starting point expands the
S
-wave fermion field in terms of the
in
and
out
bases for the fermion modes in the presence of a dyon background described in §
3.2
:

\displaystyle\bm{\chi}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{{\mathfrak{s}}=\pm}\int^{\infty}_{0}\frac{{\rm d}k}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,\Bigl[{\mathfrak{u}}^{\rm in}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}(x)\,a^{\rm in}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}+{\mathfrak{v}}^{\rm in}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}(x)\,({\overline{a}}^{\rm in}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k})^{\star}\Bigr]

(136)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{{\mathfrak{s}}=\pm}\int^{\infty}_{0}\frac{{\rm d}k}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,\Bigl[{\mathfrak{u}}^{\rm out}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}(x)\,a^{\rm out}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}+{\mathfrak{v}}^{\rm out}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}(x)\,({\overline{a}}^{\rm out}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k})^{\star}\Bigr]

with mode functions defined in eqs. (
67
) through (
70
) and (
77
) through (
80
) and particle and antiparticle creation and annihilation operators satisfying

\displaystyle\Bigl\{{a}^{\rm in}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k},({a}^{\rm in}_{{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime},k^{\prime}})^{\star}\Bigr\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Bigl\{{\overline{{a}}}^{\rm in}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k},({\overline{{a}}}^{\rm in}_{{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime},k^{\prime}})^{\star}\Bigr\}=\delta(k-k^{\prime})\,\delta_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}},

\displaystyle\hbox{and}\quad\Bigl\{{a}^{\rm out}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k},({a}^{\rm out}_{{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime},k^{\prime}})^{\star}\Bigr\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Bigl\{{\overline{{a}}}^{\rm out}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k},({\overline{{a}}}^{\rm out}_{{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime},k^{\prime}})^{\star}\Bigr\}=\delta(k-k^{\prime})\,\delta_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}},

(137)

with all other anticommutators vanishing.

Since both the
in
and
out
basis are complete, each can be expanded in terms of the other. For each
k\geq 0
we have

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{u}}^{\rm in}_{-,k}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}e^{-2ik\epsilon}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{r_{0}}\right)^{-ieQ}\,{\mathfrak{u}}^{\rm out}_{-,k}(x)+{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}e^{-i(2k+ev)\epsilon}\,e^{i{\mathfrak{a}}}\,{\mathfrak{u}}^{\rm out}_{+,k+ev}(x)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{v}}^{\rm in}_{+,k}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm in}}e^{2ik\epsilon}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{r_{0}}\right)^{ieQ}\,{\mathfrak{v}}^{\rm out}_{+,k}(x)+{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm in}}e^{i(2k+ev)\epsilon}\,e^{-i{\mathfrak{a}}}\,{\mathfrak{v}}^{\rm out}_{-,k+ev}(x)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{u}}^{\rm in}_{+,k}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm in}}e^{-2ik\epsilon}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{r_{0}}\right)^{ieQ}\,{\mathfrak{u}}^{\rm out}_{+,k}(x)

(138)

\displaystyle\qquad+{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm in}}e^{-i(2k-ev)\epsilon}\,e^{-i{\mathfrak{a}}}\Bigl[\Theta\left(k-ev\right){\mathfrak{u}}^{\rm out}_{-,k-ev}(x)+\Theta\left(-k+ev\right){\mathfrak{v}}^{\rm out}_{-,-k+ev}(x)\Bigr]

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{v}}^{\rm in}_{-,k}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}e^{2ik\epsilon}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{r_{0}}\right)^{-ieQ}\,{\mathfrak{v}}^{\rm out}_{-,k}(x)

\displaystyle\qquad+{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}e^{i(2k-ev)\epsilon}\,e^{i{\mathfrak{a}}}\Bigl[\Theta\left(k-ev\right){\mathfrak{v}}^{\rm out}_{+,k-ev}(x)+\Theta\left(-k+ev\right){\mathfrak{u}}^{\rm out}_{+,-k+ev}(x)\Bigr],

and similarly

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{u}}^{\rm out}_{-,k}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm out}}e^{2ik\epsilon}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{r_{0}}\right)^{ieQ}\,{\mathfrak{u}}^{\rm in}_{-,k}(x)+{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm out}}e^{i(2k+ev)\epsilon}\,e^{i{\mathfrak{a}}}\,{\mathfrak{u}}^{\rm in}_{+,k+ev}(x)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{v}}^{\rm out}_{+,k}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm out}}e^{-2ik\epsilon}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{r_{0}}\right)^{-ieQ}\,{\mathfrak{v}}^{\rm in}_{+,k}(x)+{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm out}}e^{-i(2k+ev)\epsilon}\,e^{-i{\mathfrak{a}}}\,{\mathfrak{v}}^{\rm in}_{-,k+ev}(x)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{u}}^{\rm out}_{+,k}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm out}}e^{2ik\epsilon}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{r_{0}}\right)^{-ieQ}\,{\mathfrak{u}}^{\rm in}_{+,k}(x)

(139)

\displaystyle\qquad+{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm out}}e^{i(2k-ev)\epsilon}\,e^{-i{\mathfrak{a}}}\Bigl[\Theta\left(k-ev\right){\mathfrak{u}}^{\rm in}_{-,k-ev}(x)+\Theta\left(-k+ev\right){\mathfrak{v}}^{\rm in}_{-,-k+ev}(x)\Bigr]

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{v}}^{\rm out}_{-,k}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm out}}e^{-2ik\epsilon}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{r_{0}}\right)^{ieQ}\,{\mathfrak{v}}^{\rm in}_{-,k}(x)
