\displaystyle\qquad+{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm out}}e^{-i(2k-ev)\epsilon}\,e^{i{\mathfrak{a}}}\Bigl[\Theta\left(k-ev\right){\mathfrak{v}}^{\rm in}_{+,k-ev}(x)+\Theta\left(-k+ev\right){\mathfrak{u}}^{\rm in}_{+,-k+ev}(x)\Bigr].

These lead to the following Bogoliubov relations between the
in
and
out
operators for each
k\geq 0
(see appendix
E
for derivation)

\displaystyle a^{\rm out}_{-,k}=\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}\;a^{\rm in}_{-,k}+{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm in}}\,e^{-i{\mathfrak{a}}}\;a^{\rm in}_{+,k+ev}\,,\qquad({\overline{a}}^{\rm out}_{+,k})^{\star}=\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm in}}\;({\overline{a}}^{\rm in}_{+,k})^{\star}+{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}\,e^{i{\mathfrak{a}}}\;({\overline{a}}^{\rm in}_{-,k+ev})^{\star}

\displaystyle\qquad a^{\rm out}_{+,k}=\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm in}}a^{\rm in}_{+,k}+{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}\,e^{i{\mathfrak{a}}}\Bigl[\Theta\left(k-ev\right)a^{\rm in}_{-,k-ev}+\Theta\left(-k+ev\right)({\overline{a}}^{\rm in}_{-,-k+ev})^{\star}\Bigr]

(140)

\displaystyle\quad({\overline{a}}^{\rm out}_{-,k})^{\star}=\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}({\overline{a}}^{\rm in}_{-,k})^{\star}+{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm in}}\,e^{-i{\mathfrak{a}}}\Bigl[\Theta\left(k-ev\right)({\overline{a}}^{\rm in}_{+,k-ev})^{\star}+\Theta\left(-k+ev\right)a^{\rm in}_{+,-k+ev}\Bigr],

as well as their inverses

\displaystyle a^{\rm in}_{-,k}=\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm out}}\;a^{\rm out}_{-,k}+{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm out}}\,e^{-i{\mathfrak{a}}}\;a^{\rm out}_{+,k+ev}\,,\qquad({\overline{a}}^{\rm in}_{+,k})^{\star}=\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm out}}\;({\overline{a}}^{\rm out}_{+,k})^{\star}+{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm out}}\,e^{i{\mathfrak{a}}}\;({\overline{a}}^{\rm out}_{-,k+ev})^{\star}

\displaystyle\qquad a^{\rm in}_{+,k}=\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm out}}a^{\rm out}_{+,k}+{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm out}}\,e^{i{\mathfrak{a}}}\Bigl[\Theta\left(k-ev\right)a^{\rm out}_{-,k-ev}+\Theta\left(-k+ev\right)({\overline{a}}^{\rm out}_{-,-k+ev})^{\star}\Bigr]

(141)

\displaystyle({\overline{a}}^{\rm in}_{-,k})^{\star}=\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm out}}({\overline{a}}^{\rm out}_{-,k})^{\star}+{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm out}}\,e^{-i{\mathfrak{a}}}\Bigl[\Theta\left(k-ev\right)({\overline{a}}^{\rm out}_{+,k-ev})^{\star}+\Theta\left(-k+ev\right)a^{\rm out}_{+,-k+ev}\Bigr].

These are consistent with the anticommutation relations (
137
) by virtue of the unitarity identities (
73
) and (
74
) – and their counterparts (
83
) and (
84
) – satsified by the
{\cal T}
’s. As in §
5
, we drop powers of
k\epsilon
,
k^{\prime}\epsilon
and
ev\epsilon
and shift
{\mathfrak{a}}
and rephase the out-state creation and-annihilation operators to remove an
r_{0}
-dependent phase.

6.1.2
Pair production

The mixing of creation and annihilation operators in the Bogoliubov transformations (
140
) and (
141
) shows that the system is unstable to pair production if
{\cal T}_{{\rm in}/{\rm out}}^{+-}
is nonzero.

To compute the pair-production rate define the
in
and
out
vacua
\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}
and
\ket{0_{{\rm out}}}
as usual:

\bm{a}^{\rm in}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}={\overline{\bm{a}}}^{\rm in}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}=0\quad\hbox{and}\quad\bm{a}^{\rm out}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}\ket{0_{{\rm out}}}={\overline{\bm{a}}}^{\rm out}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}\ket{0_{{\rm out}}}=0\,.

(142)

We switch here for convenience to discretely normalized momentum states, for which we denote the creation and annihilation operators using bold-faced fonts, as in (
\bm{a},{\overline{\bm{a}}}
). (See appendix
E
for relation between discrete and continuum normalized states.)

Writing the total vacuum as a tensor product over momenta,
\ket{0}=\underset{k>0}{\prod}\ket{0^{\,k}}
, and using the above Bogoliubov transformations implies for each
k\geq 0
we have

\ket{{0}^{\,{k}}_{{\rm in}}}=\left\{\Theta\left({k}-ev\right)+\Theta\left(-{k}+ev\right)\left[\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}+{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}e^{i{\mathfrak{a}}}(\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,{k}})^{\star}(\bm{\overline{a}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,{-k+ev}})^{\star}\right]\right\}\ket{{0}^{\,{k}}_{{\rm out}}}

(143)

and

\ket{{0}^{\,{k}}_{{\rm out}}}=\left\{\Theta\left({k}-ev\right)+\Theta\left(-{k}+ev\right)\left[\mathcal{T}^{--\,*}_{{\rm in}}+{\cal T}^{-+\,*}_{{\rm in}}e^{i{\mathfrak{a}}}(\bm{a}^{{\rm in}}_{+,{k}})^{\star}(\bm{\overline{a}}^{{\rm in}}_{-,{-k+ev}})^{\star}\right]\right\}\ket{{0}^{\,{k}}_{{\rm in}}}\,,

(144)

where as usual
\Theta(x)
denotes the Heaviside step function. See appendix
F
for derivation of the above expressions.

If
{\mathcal{T}}^{+-}=0
then (
73
) shows that
{{\cal T}}^{-+}
also vanishes and
|{\cal T}^{--}|=|{\cal T}^{++}|=1
. In this case (
143
) and (
144
) imply the
in
and
out
vacua are the same state. But when
{\mathcal{T}}^{+-}\neq 0
for some momenta the
in
vacuum contains occupied
out
particles. This shows that when charge-changing effective dyon-fermion interactions are present the dyon spontaneously emits a fermion with charge
+\frac{1}{2}e
and the antiparticle of the charge
-\frac{1}{2}e
state, ensuring the net charge emission of
\frac{1}{2}e+\frac{1}{2}e=e
.

Total electric charge is conserved because this emission is accompanied by a transition between rotor levels for
{\mathfrak{a}}
that removes one unit
+e
of charge from the dyon, as can be seen
22
22

22

This is made very explicit in §
5
for those who need convincing.
from the
{\mathfrak{a}}
-dependence of (
145
). The sign of the charge removed is dictated by the overall sign of the dyon charge
Q
which we’ve chosen to be positive:
Q>0
. These pairs are produced through the Schwinger effect
[
57
,
48
]
by the external voltage
v
of the dyon between
r\to\infty
and
r=0
, and act to discharge the dyon’s net charge.

The energetics of the process is somewhat obscured within the Born-Oppenheimer approximation because the rotor is treated as a classical field, which if regarded as an eigenstate of
{\mathfrak{a}}
is not an energy eigenstate. When calculated
perturbatively in §
5
we do find that fermion emission extracts an energy given by the spacing between rotor steps, which is of order
{\cal I}^{-1}\sim\alpha m_{g}
. But in a semiclassical approximation this energy transfer is the same size (relative to
m_{g}
) as loop corrections, which to this order we neglect, and this is why (
145
) allows pair-production for the entire momentum range
k\in(0,ev)
. Implicit in this treatment is the assumption that the fermion energies of interest are much higher than the rotor gap; one manifestation of the noncommuting order of limits discussed below eq. (
21
).

The amplitude for this emission (for fixed
{\mathfrak{a}}
) in a specific momentum mode is

\bra{0^{\,k}_{{\rm out}}}\,\bm{{\overline{a}}}^{\rm out}_{-,-k+ev}\,\bm{a}^{\rm out}_{+,k}\ket{0^{\,k}_{\rm in}}=\Theta(-k+ev)\,{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}\,e^{i{\mathfrak{a}}}\,,

(145)

which has squared modulus
{\cal P}_{k}=|{\cal T}^{+-}_{\rm in}|^{2}=|{\cal T}^{+-}_{\rm out}|^{2}
when
0<k<ev
. The vacuum-survival amplitude for this specific mode is similarly

\braket{0^{\,k}_{{\rm out}}|{0}^{\,{k}}_{{\rm in}}}=\Theta\left({k}-ev\right)+\Theta\left(-{k}+ev\right)\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}\,,

(146)

which squares to unity when
k>ev
but has squared modulus
|\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}|^{2}=1-|\mathcal{T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}|^{2}=1-{\cal P}_{k}
when
0<k<ev
. The likelihood of producing zero or one pairs sums to unity because for fermions these are the only possible options.

The
exclusive
probability for the full vacuum to produce exactly one pair in a specific mode
k\in(0,ev)
is given by combining the above result for all
k
, giving

{P}_{\text{pair}}(k)=|\bra{0_{{\rm out}}}\bm{{\overline{a}}}^{\rm out}_{-,-k+ev}\,\bm{a}^{\rm out}_{+,k}\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}|^{2}={\cal P}_{k}\overset{q<ev}{\prod_{q\neq k}}(1-{\cal P}_{q})=|{\cal T}^{+-}_{\rm in}(k)|^{2}\overset{q<ev}{\prod_{q\neq k}}|\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}(q)|^{2}.

(147)

The total probability factorizes because the likelihood of pair-production in each mode is independent of what happens for the other modes. More useful is the
inclusive
probability for pair production of a specific mode with the other modes unmeasured (and so marginalized over). The above expressions show this is given by

p_{\text{pair}}(k)={\cal P}_{k}=|{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}|^{2}\quad\hbox{and so}\quad p_{\rm no\,pair}(k)=1-{\cal P}_{k}=|\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}|^{2}\,.

(148)

We can calculate the average number of particles produced by making use of the Bogoliubov relations in the form

\displaystyle\bra{0_{{\rm in}}}N_{\rm out}\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum^{\infty}_{k=0}\bra{0_{{\rm in}}}(\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+k})^{\star}\,\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+k}\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}

(149)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum^{ev}_{k=0}|{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}|^{2}\bra{0_{{\rm in}}}\overline{\bm{a}}^{{\rm in}}_{-,-k+ev}(\overline{\bm{a}}^{{\rm in}}_{-,-k+ev})^{\star}\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}=\frac{ev}{\Delta k}|{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}|^{2},

where
\Delta k=\pi/L
is the spacing between momentum states (when these are discretely normalized).
23
23

23

We discretize momenta by putting the system in a box
-L<r<L
with near-dyon boundary conditions imposed at
r=\epsilon\approx 0
. In these conventions the relevant length of the system is
2L
, and the density of states is
2\pi/(2L)=\pi/L
The number of particles per unit length is obtained by dividing by
2L
, and this has a sensible continuum limit as
L\to\infty
, with
{\rm d}\langle N\rangle/{\rm d}x=ev|{\cal T}_{\rm in}^{+-}|^{2}/(2\pi)
. Since each produced particle moves to larger
r
at the speed of light this means that these particles emerge at infinity with a rate
\Gamma_{\infty}=ev|{\cal T}_{\rm in}^{+-}|^{2}/(2\pi)
. A similar counting also applies to the number of produced antiparticles. Because each pair contains one positively charged particle and one positively charged antiparticle the number of produced
pairs
is also

\Gamma_{\rm pair}=\frac{ev}{2\pi}|{\cal T}_{\rm in}^{+-}|^{2}\,.

(150)

This result is independently computed below using the expectation values for the fermionic currents.

Eq. (
150
) can also be compared with (
130
) (when restricted to the perturbative domain). To this end we must expand the coefficients
{\cal T}_{\rm in}
perturbatively to the same order in the
\delta{\cal C}
s used in §
5
. To linear order the
{\cal T}_{{\rm in}}
amplitudes are given by

{\cal T}^{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}}_{{\rm in}}=1+i(\delta{\cal C}^{v}_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}}-\delta{\cal C}^{s}_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}})\quad\text{and}\quad{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}=-{\cal T}^{-+\,*}_{{\rm in}}=i(\delta{\cal C}^{v}_{+-}-\delta{\cal C}^{s}_{+-}),

(151)

once the rank-2 conditions
\delta{\cal C}^{ps}_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}=\delta{\cal C}^{pv}_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}=0
are used. Using these in (
150
) then agrees with (
130
).

6.1.3
Vacuum currents

An alternative characterization of dyonic pair production that lends itself to the continuum limit is the integrated contribution of the produced pairs to current flow in the fermion sector. To display these currents we evaluate the expectation value of the various conserved currents in the
in
vacuum. Since these expectation values in general diverge we regulate them by point-splitting the two fermion fields involved by a distance
\varepsilon
, writing

\overline{\bm{\chi}}(r,t)M\bm{\chi}(r,t)\to\overline{\bm{\chi}}(r+\varepsilon/2,t)M\bm{\chi}(r-\varepsilon/2,t)\,,

(152)

with
\varepsilon\to 0
taken at the end, after renormalizing. We quote here expressions for the regularized currents – see appendix
G
for details of the matrix-element calculations.

The regularized components of the fermion number current
24
24

24

We do not go through the exercise of renormalizing the fermion number current here, since we only use
\bra{0_{{\rm in}}}{j}^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle B}(x)\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}
to evaluate the conservation equations for
j^{\alpha}_{\scriptscriptstyle F},j^{\alpha}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
.

j_{\scriptscriptstyle B}^{\alpha}=i{\overline{{\bm{\chi}}}}\Gamma^{\alpha}{\bm{\chi}}
are given by

\bra{0_{{\rm in}}}{j}^{0}_{\scriptscriptstyle B}(x)\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}=\bra{0_{{\rm out}}}{j}^{0}_{\scriptscriptstyle B}(x)\ket{0_{{\rm out}}}=0\quad\hbox{and}\quad\bra{0_{{\rm in}}}{j}^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle B}(x)\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}=\bra{0_{{\rm out}}}{j}^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle B}(x)\ket{0_{{\rm out}}}=\frac{2i}{\pi\varepsilon},

(153)

while those of the fermionic electromagnetic current
j^{\alpha}_{\scriptscriptstyle F}=\frac{1}{2}ie{\overline{{\bm{\chi}}}}\Gamma^{\alpha}\tau_{3}{\bm{\chi}}
are

\displaystyle\bra{0_{{\rm in}}}{j}^{0}_{\scriptscriptstyle F}(r,t)\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bra{0_{{\rm out}}}{j}^{0}_{\scriptscriptstyle F}(r,t)\ket{0_{{\rm out}}}=\frac{e^{2}v}{2\pi}|\mathcal{T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}|^{2}-\frac{e^{2}Q}{2\pi r}

\displaystyle\hbox{and}\quad\bra{0_{{\rm in}}}{j}^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle F}(r,t)\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\bra{0_{{\rm out}}}{j}^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle F}(r,t)\ket{0_{{\rm out}}}=\frac{e^{2}v}{2\pi}|\mathcal{T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}|^{2}\,.

(154)

We define the axial current by
j^{\alpha}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}=i{\overline{{\bm{\chi}}}}\Gamma^{\alpha}\Gamma_{\scriptscriptstyle A}{\bm{\chi}}
with
\Gamma_{\scriptscriptstyle A}\coloneqq\Gamma_{c}\,\tau_{3}
rather than
\Gamma_{c}
because this agrees with the 4D axial current
i{\overline{{\bm{\psi}}}}\gamma^{\mu}\gamma_{5}{\bm{\psi}}
for
S
-wave states (up to the usual 2D normalization factor of
4\pi r^{2}
). Its vacuum matrix elements are

\displaystyle\bra{0_{{\rm in}}}{j}^{0}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}(r,t)\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\bra{0_{{\rm out}}}{j}^{0}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}(r,t)\ket{0_{{\rm out}}}=-\frac{ev}{\pi}|\mathcal{T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}|^{2}

\displaystyle\hbox{and}\quad\bra{0_{{\rm in}}}{j}^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}(r,t)\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bra{0_{{\rm out}}}{j}^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}(r,t)\ket{0_{{\rm out}}}=-\frac{ev}{\pi}|\mathcal{T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}|^{2}+\frac{eQ}{\pi r}\,.

(155)

Recalling that the 2D modes are normalized so that the 2D flux
j^{1}
gives the integrated radial flux
4\pi r^{2}J^{r}
for the corresponding 4D
S
-wave current, we see that at spatial infinity there is a nonzero flux of both electric and axial charge:

\bra{0_{{\rm in}}}{j}^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle F}(\infty,t)\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}=e{\mathfrak{F}}\quad\hbox{and}\quad\bra{0_{{\rm in}}}{j}^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}(\infty,t)\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}=-2{\mathfrak{F}}\quad\hbox{where}\quad{\mathfrak{F}}=\frac{ev}{2\pi}|{\cal T}_{\rm in}^{+-}|^{2}\,.

(156)

This has a simple interpretation as a flux of pair-produced particles, since each such pair carries electric charge
e
and axial charge
25
25

25

See appendix
E
for discussion of asymptotic charges of
in,out
states.

-2
(and no net fermion number), with integrated particle flux (or rate with which particle pairs appear at infinity) given by
\Gamma_{\rm pair}={\mathfrak{F}}
, in agreement with (
150
). Our result for the flux of the axial current is consistent with
[
48
]
, when the
{\cal T}
amplitudes are chosen to match theirs
26
26

26

Note that the axial current in
[
48
]
has a relative minus sign compared to our definition.

i.e.
when
|{\cal T}^{+-}|=1
.

These expressions are also consistent with (anomalous) current conservation. As shown in appendix
G
, the above currents satisfy

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha}j^{\alpha}_{\scriptscriptstyle B}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{eQ}{2r^{2}}\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}{\lim}\Bigl[\varepsilon\,\overline{\bm{\chi}}(r+\varepsilon/2,t)\Gamma^{0}\,\tau_{3}\bm{\chi}(r-\varepsilon/2,t)\Bigr]=-\frac{iQ}{r^{2}}\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}{\lim}\Bigl[\varepsilon j^{0}_{\scriptscriptstyle F}(x)\Bigr]=0,

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha}j^{\alpha}_{\scriptscriptstyle F}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{e^{2}Q}{4r^{2}}\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}{\lim}\Bigl[\varepsilon\,\overline{\bm{\chi}}(r+\varepsilon/2,t)\Gamma^{0}\bm{\chi}(r-\varepsilon/2,t)\Bigr]=-\frac{ie^{2}Q}{4r^{2}}\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}{\lim}\Bigl[\varepsilon j^{0}_{\scriptscriptstyle B}(x)\Bigr]=0,

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha}j^{\alpha}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{eQ}{2r^{2}}\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}{\lim}\Bigl[\varepsilon\,\overline{\bm{\chi}}(r+\varepsilon/2,t)\Gamma^{1}\bm{\chi}(r-\varepsilon/2,t)\Bigr]=\frac{ieQ}{2r^{2}}\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}{\lim}\Bigl[\varepsilon j^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle B}(x)\Bigr]=-\frac{eQ}{\pi r^{2}}\,.

(157)

These agree with the standard 2D anomaly expressions, which in the present instance tell us that
j^{\alpha}_{\scriptscriptstyle B}
and
j^{\alpha}_{\scriptscriptstyle F}
are anomaly free and give

\partial_{\alpha}j^{\alpha}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}=\left[\frac{e}{2}-\left(-\frac{e}{2}\right)\right]\frac{1}{2\pi}\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}{\cal F}_{\alpha\beta}=\frac{e}{\pi}\Bigl[\partial_{0}\mathcal{A}_{r}-\partial_{r}\mathcal{A}_{0}\Bigr]=\frac{1}{\pi}\partial_{r}\left(\frac{eQ}{r}-ev\right)=-\frac{eQ}{\pi r^{2}},

(158)

when evaluated with the background dyonic Coulomb field.

It is also noteworthy that (
154
) implies that particle production significantly polarizes the fermionic ground state, inducing a charge density with the opposite sign to the dyon charge that (for massless fermions) falls off only as a power law as one moves away from the dyon. As has been remarked elsewhere
[
10
,
33
,
34
,
35
,
36
,
37
]
, such charging of the fermion ground state puffs up the dyon into a much bigger object than was the underlying classical dyon configuration.

6.1.4
Dyon-fermion scattering

The Bogoliubov relations in the 2D EFT also allow us to calculate the cross section for any
S
-wave fermion-dyon scattering process, by evaluating amplitudes of the form
\langle A_{\rm out}|B_{\rm in}\rangle
. The Bogoliubov transformation provides a succinct listing of the options for
\bra{A_{\rm out}}
that give nonzero amplitudes given a single-particle
\ket{B_{\rm in}}
that can be seen by expanding the incoming state
(a^{{\rm in}}_{\mathfrak{s},k})^{\star}
in terms of
out
operators – see (
141
). The presence of pair-production means that any such process can be accompanied by some number of spontaneously produced pairs.

For instance the
4
D chirality-changing amplitude
for
f_{-}(k)\to f_{-}(k^{\prime})
accompanied by the emission of
n
pairs is given by

\displaystyle\bra{0_{{\rm out}}}\overline{a}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-q_{n}+ev}{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,q_{n}}...\overline{a}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-q_{1}+ev}{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,q_{1}}\,{a}^{{\rm out}}_{-,k^{\prime}}({a}^{{\rm in}}_{-,k})^{\star}\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}

(159)

\displaystyle\qquad=\delta({k-k^{\prime}})\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}\bra{0_{{\rm out}}}\overline{a}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-q_{n}+ev}{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,q_{n}}...\overline{a}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-q_{1}+ev}{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,q_{1}}\ket{0_{{\rm in}}},

where
n=0,1,2,\cdots
. The amplitude for
charge-changing processes like
f_{\pm}(k)\to f_{\mp}(k^{\prime})
(accompanied by
n
spontaneously produced pairs) is similarly

\displaystyle\bra{0_{{\rm out}}}\overline{a}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-q_{n}+ev}{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,q_{n}}...\overline{a}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-q_{1}+ev}{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,q_{1}}\,{a}^{{\rm out}}_{-,k^{\prime}}({a}^{{\rm in}}_{+,k})^{\star}\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}

(160)

\displaystyle\qquad=\delta(k^{\prime}+ev-k)e^{-i{\mathfrak{a}}}{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm in}}\bra{0_{{\rm out}}}\overline{a}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-q_{n}+ev}{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,q_{n}}...\overline{a}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-q_{1}+ev}{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,q_{1}}\ket{0_{{\rm in}}},

and

\displaystyle\bra{0_{{\rm out}}}\overline{a}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-q_{n}+ev}{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,q_{n}}...\overline{a}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-q_{1}+ev}{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,q_{1}}\,{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,k^{\prime}}({a}^{{\rm in}}_{-,k})^{\star}\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}

(161)

\displaystyle\qquad=\delta({k^{\prime}-ev-k})e^{i{\mathfrak{a}}}{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}\bra{0_{{\rm out}}}\overline{a}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-q_{n}+ev}{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,q_{n}}...\overline{a}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-q_{1}+ev}{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,q_{1}}\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}\,.

In particular the different voltages seen by the two charge states imply the reaction
f_{+}(k)\to f_{-}(k^{\prime})
(plus pair production) vanishes unless
k>ev
for want of fermion final states with the required energy.

The remaining reaction obtained with an initial incoming positively charged fermion is slightly more complicated. On one hand the
4
D chirality-flipping
process
f_{+}(k)\to f_{+}(k^{\prime})
(plus the production of
n
pairs) proceeds much as above. However when
k<ev
the part of the Bogoliubov transformation relating
({a}^{{\rm in}}_{+,k})^{\star}
to
{\overline{a}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-k+ev}
also contributes to give an amplitude for spontaneously emitting
n+1
pairs from the vacuum (with one of the pairs corresponding to a particle and antiparticle of momentum
k^{\prime}
and
-k+ev
, respectively), leading to:

\displaystyle\bra{0_{{\rm out}}}\overline{a}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-q_{n}+ev}{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,q_{n}}...\overline{a}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-q_{1}+ev}{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,q_{1}}\,{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,k^{\prime}}({a}^{{\rm in}}_{+,k})^{\star}\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}

\displaystyle\qquad=\delta(k-k^{\prime})\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm in}}\bra{0_{{\rm out}}}\overline{a}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-q_{n}+ev}{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,q_{n}}...\overline{a}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-q_{1}+ev}{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,q_{1}}\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}

(162)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\Theta\left(-k+ev\right)e^{-i{\mathfrak{a}}}{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm in}}\bra{0_{{\rm out}}}\overline{a}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-q_{n}+ev}{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,q_{n}}...\overline{a}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-q_{1}+ev}{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,q_{1}}{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,k^{\prime}}{\overline{a}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-k+ev}\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}.

In
equations (
159
)-(
162
), the momenta
k^{\prime},q_{1},\cdots q_{n}
are all distinct and satisfy
ev>q_{1},\cdots q_{n}>0
as well as
k,k^{\prime}>0
. Similar formulae can be derived for the amplitudes with a single antiparticle in the initial state.

The next step is to evaluate the pair production amplitudes appearing in (
159
)-(
162
) by using the

out
particle content of the
in
vacuum, as in appendix
F
.
When counting pairs it is more convenient to switch to a discrete normalization for momentum eigenstates, as we now do, in which case the
maximum number of
out
pairs in the
in
vacuum,
N
, is a finite but large number
27
27

27

N
is defined as the maximum value of
\bra{0_{{\rm in}}}N_{\rm out}\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}={ev}/{\Delta k}\,|{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}|^{2}
and so is given by
N={ev}/{\Delta k}
. In the continuum limit,
N
goes to infinity as the spacing
\Delta k
between states vanishes (see Appendix
E
for details).
.
The
discrete normalization analogue of the
amplitude for emitting
n
pairs appearing in (
159
) to (
161
) and the first line of the right-hand side of (
162
) evaluates to

\bm{{\cal A}}^{n}_{\rm pair}=\bra{0_{{\rm out}}}\overline{\bm{a}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-q_{n}+ev}\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,q_{n}}...\overline{\bm{a}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-q_{1}+ev}{\bm{a}}^{{\rm out}}_{+,q_{1}}\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}=\left({\cal T}_{{\rm in}}^{--}\right)^{N-n}\left({\cal T}_{{\rm in}}^{+-}\right)^{n}e^{in{\mathfrak{a}}},

(163)

for
n\leq N
, and vanishes otherwise.
28
28

28

We denote transition amplitudes between discretely normalized states by
\bm{{\cal A}}
and their continuum normalization counterparts by
{\cal A}
.
For
k<ev
the amplitude for emitting
n+1
pairs encountered in the second line of (
162
) similarly corresponds to

\displaystyle\bm{{\cal A}}^{n+1}_{\rm pair}(k,k^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bra{0_{{\rm out}}}\overline{\bm{a}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-q_{n}+ev}\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,q_{n}}...\overline{\bm{a}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-q_{1}+ev}\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,q_{1}}\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,k^{\prime}}{\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-k+ev}\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}

(164)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\delta_{kk^{\prime}}\left({\cal T}_{{\rm in}}^{--}\right)^{N-(n+1)}\left({\cal T}_{{\rm in}}^{+-}\right)^{n+1}e^{i(n+1){\mathfrak{a}}},

when
n<N
and vanishes otherwise. The two lines on the right-hand side of (
162
) then combine to become

\displaystyle\bra{0_{{\rm out}}}\overline{\bm{a}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-q_{n}+ev}\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,q_{n}}...\overline{\bm{a}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-q_{1}+ev}\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,q_{1}}\,\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,k^{\prime}}(\bm{a}^{{\rm in}}_{+,k})^{\star}\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}

\displaystyle\qquad=\delta_{kk^{\prime}}\Bigl[\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm in}}\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}-{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm in}}{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}\Bigr]\left({\cal T}_{{\rm in}}^{--}\right)^{N-(n+1)}\left({\cal T}_{{\rm in}}^{+-}\right)^{n}e^{in{\mathfrak{a}}},

(165)

for
k<ev
and
n<N
, when evaluated using discretely normalized states. The corresponding continuum normalization amplitudes have a very similar form.

Notice that the amplitudes (
159
)-(
161
) factorize into a product of a single-particle transition amplitude,
{\cal A}_{sc}
, from a particle with quantum numbers
\mathfrak{s},k
to one with quantum numbers
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime},k^{\prime}
, times a product of pair-production (or vacuum survival) amplitudes for all the modes. The amplitude (
162
) factorizes in the same way for initial momenta
k>ev
, while for
k<ev
the product over pair production (or vacuum survival) amplitudes runs over all but the
k
-th mode
29
29

29

For
k<ev
, the amplitude (
162
) factorizes differently than (
159
)-(
161
)
because it describes two processes in which the number of produced pairs is not the same.
We define
{\cal A}_{sc}
for this process as in (
169
) so that the single-particle amplitude captures the relevant contribution of (
162
) to inclusive observables.

.
The amplitudes factorize in this way because scattering and pair production for different modes are statistically independent. Inspection of the above formulae shows that the single-particle transition amplitudes
\mathcal{A}_{sc}[f_{{\mathfrak{s}}}(k)\to f_{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}}(k^{\prime})]
are given by

\mathcal{A}_{sc}[f_{-}(k)\to f_{-}(k^{\prime})]=\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}\;\delta({k-k^{\prime}}),

(166)

\mathcal{A}_{sc}[f_{+}(k)\to f_{-}(k^{\prime})]=e^{-i{\mathfrak{a}}}{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm in}}\;\delta(k^{\prime}+ev-k),

(167)

\mathcal{A}_{sc}[f_{-}(k)\to f_{+}(k^{\prime})]=e^{i{\mathfrak{a}}}{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}\;\delta({k^{\prime}-ev-k})\,,

(168)

and

\mathcal{A}_{sc}[f_{+}(k)\to f_{+}(k^{\prime})]=\Bigl[\Theta(k-ev)\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm in}}+\Theta\left(-k+ev\right)\left[{\cal T}^{++}_{{\rm in}}{\cal T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}-{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm in}}\right]\Bigr]\,\delta(k-k^{\prime})\,.

(169)

Notice that the unitarity constraints on the
{\cal T}_{{\rm in}}
amplitudes given in (
73
) and (
74
) imply that the expression
{\cal T}^{++}_{{\rm in}}{\cal T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}-{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm in}}
simplifies to a phase.

Low-energy scattering rates can now be computed much as in §
5
by projecting any incoming plane wave onto the
S
-wave. 4D cross sections can then be computed by dividing by the appropriate incident particle flux. For instance, factoring out the energy-conserving delta function from amplitudes (
166
)-(
168
) as
{\cal A}_{sc}={\cal M}\,\delta(E_{f}-E_{i})
, the
4
D cross section for scattering with no pair production is

{\rm d}\sigma^{s}_{\rm exclusive}\Bigl[f_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{c}}}(k)\to f_{{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}}(k^{\prime})\Bigr]=\frac{p_{s}}{{\mathfrak{F}}_{i}}\;{\rm d}\Gamma_{\rm exclusive}=\frac{\pi}{k^{2}}\,\delta_{{\mathfrak{s}},{\mathfrak{c}}}\delta_{{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime},-{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}}\,|{\cal M}|^{2}\;|\braket{0_{{\rm out}}|0_{{\rm in}}}|^{2}\,\delta(E_{f}-E_{i})\,{\rm d}k^{\prime}\,,

(170)

where the probability for the plane wave to be found in an
S
-wave,
p_{s}
and the initial particle flux
{\mathfrak{F}}_{i}
are defined as in §
5
and are calculated in Appendix
E
along with the 2D exclusive differential interaction rate
{\rm d}\Gamma_{\rm exclusive}
. Similarly, the cross section for scattering with no additional pair production for the final amplitude (
169
) is

\displaystyle{\rm d}\sigma^{s}_{\rm exclusive}\Bigl[f_{++}(k)\to f_{+-}(k^{\prime})\Bigr]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{k^{2}}\,|{\cal M}|^{2}\;|\braket{0_{{\rm out}}|0_{{\rm in}}}|^{2}\,\delta(E_{f}-E_{i})\,{\rm d}k^{\prime}

(171)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad\times\Bigl[\Theta(k-ev)+\Theta(-k+ev)\left|{\cal T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}\right|^{-2}\Bigr]\,,

where
{\cal M}
is defined through
{\cal A}_{sc}={\cal M}\,\delta(E_{f}-E_{i})
and the factor of
\left|{\cal T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}\right|^{-2}
is cancelled by similar factors in the overlap
|\braket{0_{{\rm out}}|0_{{\rm in}}}|^{2}
, making the cross section finite even in the
{\cal T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}\to 0
limit.

Of more practical interest are
inclusive
cross sections for which the number of associated pair productions is unmeasured and so marginalized over. Appendix
F
– see the discussion below eq. (
330
) – explicitly performs the marginalization over the number of produced pairs and shows that the resulting cross section
can be expressed purely in terms of the single-particle scattering amplitudes
{\cal A}_{sc}
, given in (
166
)-(
169
). The ability to do so is a consequence of unitarity, and is also the reason the parameters
n
and
N
drop out of our final results. The inclusive 4D cross section becomes

{\rm d}\sigma_{s}\Bigl[f_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{c}}}(k)\to f_{{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}}(k^{\prime})\Bigr]=\frac{p_{s}}{{\mathfrak{F}}_{i}}\;{\rm d}\Gamma_{\rm inclusive}=\frac{\pi}{k^{2}}\,\delta_{{\mathfrak{s}},{\mathfrak{c}}}\delta_{{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime},-{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}}\,|{\cal M}|^{2}\,\delta(E_{f}-E_{i})\,{\rm d}k^{\prime}\,\,,

(172)

where the 2D inclusive differential interaction rate
{\rm d}\Gamma_{\rm inclusive}
is defined in Appendix
E
.

Combining results and integrating over the final momentum
k^{\prime}
leads to the total inclusive
S
-wave cross sections. For charge-exchange processes with
\mathfrak{s}=\mathfrak{c}
we have

\sigma_{s}[f_{\pm}\to f_{\mp}]=\frac{\pi}{k^{2}}\Bigl|\mathcal{T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}\Bigr|^{2}\Theta(k-{\mathfrak{s}}ev).

(173)

Similarly for processes in which the
4
D chirality changes, but the charge doesn’t the cross section when
\mathfrak{s}=\mathfrak{c}=-
is

\sigma_{s}[f_{-}\to f_{-}]=\frac{\pi}{k^{2}}\Bigl|\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}\Bigr|^{2},

(174)

and

\sigma_{s}[f_{+}\to f_{+}]=\frac{\pi}{k^{2}}\Big[\Theta(k-ev)\Bigl|\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm in}}\Bigr|^{2}+\Theta\left(-k+ev\right)\Big],

(175)

when
\mathfrak{s}=\mathfrak{c}=+
. These agree with the corresponding perturbative expressions when their domains of validity overlap, and are consistent with the cross section results given in
[
53
]
, when we restrict to their choices:
|{\cal T}^{++}_{{\rm in}}|=|{\cal T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}|=1
,
{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}={\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm in}}=0
.

These cross sections display catalysis inasmuch as they are independent of the scale
R\sim m_{g}^{-1}
of the underlying classical dyon
[
10
,
11
,
12
]
. They also do not depend directly on the dyon magnetic or electric charge (though there is a large Coulomb phase that drops out of the cross section that does see the dyon’s electric charge). Their size scales like the unitarity bound
\sigma\propto 1/k^{2}
whose origin comes purely from the projection of the incoming plane wave onto the
S
-wave that dominates at low energies. Finally the rates are directly controlled by the size of the effective couplings hidden within the
\mathcal{T}_{{\rm in}}
amplitudes, rather than through the more microscopic scales associated with the RG-invariants that would normally be needed once couplings are renormalized to remove the spurious
\epsilon
-dependence. In the present instance – and just for the special kinematics of the monopole
S
-wave – the existence of only a single solution to the radial equation implies that the dimensionless magnitudes
|{\cal T}_{\rm in}|
themselves are already RG-invariant.

6.2
Effective dyon dynamics

Having described fermion behaviour in the approximation where the ‘slow’ degree of freedom
{\mathfrak{a}}
is a fixed background we here return to the question of how
{\mathfrak{a}}
responds to fermion scattering on longer time-scales. The leading dynamics of
{\mathfrak{a}}
is governed by the hamiltonian (
134
)

H_{\rm dyon}=\frac{1}{2{\cal I}}\left[{\mathfrak{p}}(t)-\frac{\vartheta}{2\pi}\right]^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\Bigl[{\overline{\bm{\chi}}}\,O_{\cal B}({\mathfrak{a}})\,{\bm{\chi}}\Bigr]_{r=\epsilon,t}

(176)

where
{\mathfrak{p}}
is the conjugate momentum for
{\mathfrak{a}}
given in (
42
), repeated here for convenience:

{\mathfrak{p}}:=\frac{\vartheta}{2\pi}+{\cal I}\dot{{\mathfrak{a}}}\,.

(177)

6.2.1
Charge conservation and rotor evolution

In the Heisenberg picture the momentum
{\mathfrak{p}}
satisfies the equation of motion

e\,\dot{{\mathfrak{p}}}(t)=\frac{i}{2}\left({\overline{\bm{\chi}}}\,\left[O_{{\cal B}}({\mathfrak{a}}(t)),\frac{e}{2}\tau_{3}\right]\,\bm{\chi}\right)_{r=\epsilon}=j^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle F}(\epsilon,t)\,,

(178)

where
O_{\cal B}({\mathfrak{a}})
is given in (
51
) and the last equality equating the result to the fermionic electromagnetic current flux is obtained by using the boundary condition (
50
)
c.f.
eq. (
52
):

\left[\Gamma^{1}+O_{{\cal B}}({\mathfrak{a}}(t))\right]\bm{\chi}(\epsilon,t)=0.

(179)

Eq. (
178
) expresses conservation of total electric charge in the sense that it equates the change in the dyon charge
-e{\mathfrak{p}}(t)
to the radial flux of fermion electric charge
j^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle F}(\epsilon,t)
evaluated near the dyon.

Equation (
178
) integrates to give

{\mathfrak{p}}(t)={\mathfrak{p}}(t_{0})+\frac{1}{e}\int^{t}_{t_{0}}{\rm d}t^{\prime}j^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle F}(\epsilon,t^{\prime}),

(180)

and this result can be used in (
177
) to evolve
{\mathfrak{a}}
, leading to

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{a}}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{a}}(t_{0})+\frac{1}{{\cal I}}\int^{t}_{t_{0}}{\rm d}t^{\prime}\left[{\mathfrak{p}}(t_{0})-\frac{\vartheta}{2\pi}+\frac{1}{e}\int^{t^{\prime}}_{t_{0}}{\rm d}t^{{}^{\prime\prime}}j^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle F}(\epsilon,t^{\prime\prime})\right]

(181)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{a}}(t_{0})+\frac{t-t_{0}}{{\cal I}}\left[{\mathfrak{p}}(t_{0})-\frac{\vartheta}{2\pi}\right]+\frac{1}{e{\cal I}}\int^{t}_{t_{0}}{\rm d}t^{\prime}\int^{t^{\prime}}_{t_{0}}{\rm d}t^{{}^{\prime\prime}}j^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle F}(\epsilon,t^{\prime\prime}).

These expressions show how the rotor operator acquires a component that acts within the fermionic part of the Hilbert space for times
t>t_{0}
.

Now comes the main point. We wish to use the above expressions to determine
how the slow variable
{\mathfrak{a}}
evolves in response to its interactions with the relativistic fermion field. If we use how nuclei are handled within the Born-Oppenheimer approximation applied to atoms, the answer seems simple. For atoms one first computes the electronic state
|\Psi({\bf R})\rangle
as a function of fixed classical nuclear positions,
{\bf R}
, and then determines the nuclear positions by minimizing the nuclear energy
V({\bf R})=\langle\Psi({\bf R})|H|\Psi({\bf R})\rangle
given these atomic states. In the present instance the first step corresponds to computing the fermion state
\ket{\psi_{i};{\mathfrak{a}}}
as a function of a classical initial value for
{\mathfrak{a}}
. Then we compute
an interaction Hamiltonian that captures the correct dynamics within the fermionic state
\ket{\psi_{i};{\mathfrak{a}}}
and use it to find
how the field
{\mathfrak{a}}
evolves.

Suppose we assume that the rotor and fermion sectors are initially unrelated to one another at
t=t_{0}
, at which point
{\mathfrak{a}}(t_{0})={\mathfrak{a}}_{0}
. The above reasoning suggests these slow-moving rotor degrees of freedom see only an average over the fast variables and so (
178
) is approximately given by

\dot{{\mathfrak{p}}}_{\rm eff}(t)\simeq\frac{1}{e}\bra{{\mathfrak{a}}_{0};\psi_{i}}j^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle F}(\epsilon,t)\ket{\psi_{i};{\mathfrak{a}}_{0}}\,,

(182)

which integrates to

{\mathfrak{p}}_{\rm eff}(t)={\mathfrak{p}}(t_{0})+\frac{1}{e}\int^{t}_{t_{0}}{\rm d}t^{\prime}\bra{{\mathfrak{a}}_{0};\psi_{i}}j^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle F}(\epsilon,t^{\prime})\ket{\psi_{i};{\mathfrak{a}}_{0}},

(183)

as well as

{\mathfrak{a}}_{\rm eff}(t)={\mathfrak{a}}_{0}+\frac{t-t_{0}}{{\cal I}}\left[{\mathfrak{p}}(t_{0})-\frac{\vartheta}{2\pi}\right]+\frac{1}{e{\cal I}}\int^{t}_{t_{0}}{\rm d}t^{\prime}\int^{t^{\prime}}_{t_{0}}{\rm d}t^{{}^{\prime\prime}}\bra{{\mathfrak{a}}_{0};\psi_{i}}j^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle F}(\epsilon,t^{\prime\prime})\ket{\psi_{i};{\mathfrak{a}}_{0}}.

(184)

For instance, for fermions initially prepared in the vacuum state
\ket{\psi_{i};{\mathfrak{a}}_{0}}=\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}
eq. (
154
) gives a time-independent current expectation value

\dot{{\mathfrak{p}}}_{\rm eff}(t)\simeq\frac{1}{e}\bra{0_{\rm in}}j^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle F}(\epsilon,t)\ket{0_{\rm in}}=\frac{ev}{2\pi}|{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}|^{2}\,,

(185)

and so the time-evolution of
{\mathfrak{a}}
and its conjugate momentum
{\mathfrak{p}}
are approximately given by

{\mathfrak{p}}_{\rm eff}(t)={\mathfrak{p}}(t_{0})+\frac{ev(t-t_{0})}{2\pi}|{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}|^{2},

(186)

and

{\mathfrak{a}}_{\rm eff}(t)={\mathfrak{a}}(t_{0})+\frac{t-t_{0}}{{\cal I}}\left[{\mathfrak{p}}(t_{0})-\frac{\vartheta}{2\pi}\right]+\frac{ev(t-t_{0})^{2}}{4\pi{\cal I}}|{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}|^{2}\simeq{\mathfrak{a}}_{0}\,,

(187)

where the final approximate equality drops
\alpha
-supressed terms involving
{\cal I}^{-1}\sim\alpha m_{g}
(which also assumes
t-t_{0}
is not too large).

6.2.2
Effective rotor hamiltonian

One can ask: is there an effective rotor hamiltonian whose equations of motion have the same form as eqs. (
177
) and (
178
)? Strictly speaking, such a hamiltonian need not exist because the rotor is an open quantum system once the fermion degrees of freedom are ignored. For such systems an effective hamiltonian only exists to the extent that there is a mean-field description for which fluctuations in the ignored degrees of freedom – the ‘environment’ – can be neglected relative to the mean evolution (for a review of these issues, including a more precise statement of the mean-field criterion – see
[
16
]
).

But even if an effective rotor hamiltonian exists, its description is likely not as simple as generating a potential
V({\mathfrak{a}})
for
{\mathfrak{a}}
, which would be the analogy expected based on the Born-Oppenheimer description of the energetics of nuclear positions within an atom. In particular any such hamiltonian must produce nonzero
\dot{\mathfrak{p}}
that is independent of
{\mathfrak{a}}
. But while it is true that Hamilton’s equation
\dot{\mathfrak{p}}=-\partial H/\partial{\mathfrak{a}}
seems to imply that nonzero
\dot{\mathfrak{p}}
requires
H
to depend on
{\mathfrak{a}}
– such as by adding a potential
V({\mathfrak{a}})
to
H
– there is also no choice for a potential satisfying
V({\mathfrak{a}}+2\pi)=V({\mathfrak{a}})
that can produce a nonzero
\dot{\mathfrak{p}}
that is independent of
{\mathfrak{a}}
.

To explore what a successful choice for a rotor Hamiltonian would look like consider as a starting point the rotor dynamics implied by the Lagrangian of (
40
), repeated here (including the coupling to
\widehat{A}_{0}
)

L_{\rm dyon}\ni\frac{\vartheta_{\rm eff}(t)}{2\pi}\Bigl(\dot{\mathfrak{a}}-e\widehat{A}_{0}\Bigr)+\frac{{\cal I}}{2}\Bigl(\dot{\mathfrak{a}}-e\widehat{A}_{0}\Bigr)^{2}+\cdots\,,

(188)

which writes the terms in order of dominance at low-energies. For later purposes we temporarily entertain the possibility that
\vartheta_{\rm eff}=\vartheta_{\rm eff}(t)
is a specified function of time.

Consider first keeping just the leading term,

S_{\rm rotor}=\int_{\scriptscriptstyle W}{\rm d}t\;\frac{\vartheta_{\rm eff}(t)}{2\pi}\Bigl(\dot{\mathfrak{a}}-e\widehat{A}_{0}\Bigr)\,,

(189)

in which case the canonical momentum and Hamiltonian are

{\mathfrak{p}}:=\frac{\delta S_{\rm rotor}}{\delta\dot{\mathfrak{a}}}=\frac{\vartheta_{\rm eff}(t)}{2\pi}\quad\hbox{and}\quad H={\mathfrak{p}}\,\dot{\mathfrak{a}}-L_{\rm rotor}=\frac{e\vartheta_{\rm eff}(t)}{2\pi}\,\widehat{A}_{0}\,,

(190)

where the momentum equation can be regarded as a constraint. The Hamiltonian reveals the sole energy associated with this interaction to be the Coulomb energy due to the dyon acquiring an additional induced charge
-e\vartheta_{\rm eff}/2\pi
(as expected from the Witten effect
[
55
]
). The evolution equation for
{\mathfrak{p}}
is then

\dot{\mathfrak{p}}=\frac{\dot{\vartheta}_{\rm eff}}{2\pi}.

(191)

Interestingly, this equation does agree with (
182
) provided we identify

\vartheta_{\rm eff}(t)\simeq\vartheta_{0}+\frac{2\pi}{e}\int_{t_{0}}^{t}{\rm d}t^{\prime}\;\bra{{\mathfrak{a}}_{0};\psi_{i}}j^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle F}(\epsilon,t^{\prime})\ket{\psi_{i};{\mathfrak{a}}_{0}}\,.

(192)

In the special case where
\ket{\psi_{i};{\mathfrak{a}}_{0}}=\ket{0_{\rm in}}
this becomes

\vartheta_{\rm eff}(t)\simeq\vartheta_{0}+ev|{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}|^{2}(t-t_{0})\,.

(193)

The idea that fermion scattering might cause vacuum angle evolution was earlier discussed in
[
40
]
.

Extending the above to include the subdominant kinetic term for
{\mathfrak{a}}
appearing in (
188
) – and again entertaining the possibility that
\vartheta_{\rm eff}=\vartheta_{\rm eff}(t)
is a function of time – instead leads to the canonical momentum and Hamiltonian

{\mathfrak{p}}:=\frac{\delta S}{\delta\dot{\mathfrak{a}}}=\frac{\vartheta_{\rm eff}(t)}{2\pi}+{\cal I}(\dot{\mathfrak{a}}-e\widehat{A}_{0})\quad\hbox{and}\quad H={\mathfrak{p}}\,\dot{\mathfrak{a}}-L=e{\mathfrak{p}}\,\widehat{A}_{0}+\frac{1}{2{\cal I}}\left({\mathfrak{p}}-\frac{\vartheta_{\rm eff}}{2\pi}\right)^{2}\,,

(194)

which shows how the previous momentum constraint
{\mathfrak{p}}=\vartheta_{\rm eff}/(2\pi)
emerges as the momentum choice that minimizes the energy, for fixed
e\widehat{A}_{0}
.
To the extent that the rotor evolution minimizes its energy one expects

\dot{\mathfrak{p}}\simeq\frac{\dot{\vartheta}_{\rm eff}}{2\pi}\quad\hbox{and}\quad\dot{\mathfrak{a}}=\frac{1}{{\cal I}}\left({\mathfrak{p}}-\frac{\vartheta_{\rm eff}}{2\pi}\right)\simeq 0\,,

(195)

which agree with eqs. (
177
) and (
185
) in the limit that
{\cal I}^{-1}
can be neglected (so
\dot{\mathfrak{a}}\simeq 0
), when
\vartheta_{\rm eff}(t)
is given by (
193
). Although the rotor charge
{\mathfrak{p}}
evolves as it follows
\vartheta_{\rm eff}
the minimized value of the energy remains unchanged.

The above picture apparently relies on
{\mathfrak{a}}
and
{\mathfrak{p}}
approximately behaving as slow classical variables so that
{\mathfrak{p}}
can evolve continuously with time as
\vartheta_{\rm eff}
does. This is indeed a good approximation for the fermion energies
\alpha m_{g}\ll E\ll m_{g}
for which the Born-Oppenheimer approximation applies. In a fuller quantum treatment the initial value
{\mathfrak{a}}_{0}
appearing in (
184
) should be regarded as an operator satisfying
[{\mathfrak{a}}_{0}\,,{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}]=i
, and commutes with the fermion degrees of freedom evaluated at
t_{0}
. The requirement that
{\mathfrak{a}}_{0}
also be a periodic variable with
{\mathfrak{a}}_{0}\sim{\mathfrak{a}}_{0}+2\pi
implies that its canonical momentum
{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}
is quantized with eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}=n
where
n
is an arbitrary integer. Evaluating the hamiltonian of (
194
) as a function of
{\mathfrak{p}}_{\rm eff}(t)
given in (
186
) with
\vartheta_{\rm eff}(t)
given by (
193
) then shows – in the absence of
\widehat{A}_{0}
– that

H=\frac{1}{2{\cal I}}\left[{\mathfrak{p}}_{\rm eff}(t)-\frac{ev}{2\pi}|{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}|^{2}(t-t_{0})-\frac{\vartheta_{0}}{2\pi}\right]^{2}=\frac{1}{2{\cal I}}\left({\mathfrak{p}}_{0}-\frac{\vartheta_{0}}{2\pi}\right)^{2}

(196)

and so has energy eigenvalues

E_{n}=\frac{1}{2{\cal I}}\left(n-\frac{\vartheta_{0}}{2\pi}\right)^{2}\,,

(197)

that are independent of time and quantized with step size of order
{\cal I}^{-1}\sim\alpha m_{g}
even as
{\mathfrak{p}}_{\rm eff}(t)
varies continuously.

6.3
Vacuum-angle evolution

The upshot of the previous section is that – somewhat surprisingly – an effective Hamiltonian can exist that captures the rotor’s evolution equations (
177
) and (
178
) if tracing out the fermion were to produce an effective contribution to the effective lagrangian of the form
\Delta L=\frac{1}{2\pi}\vartheta_{\rm eff}(t)D{\mathfrak{a}}
with
\vartheta_{\rm eff}(t)
satisfying the matching condition (
192
). How might such an effective interaction actually be generated when explicitly integrating out the bulk fermion?
30
30

30

This issue is also discussed in
[
40
]
, though in a way that invokes a bulk coupling of
{\mathfrak{a}}
to fermions.

An effective lagrangian of the form (
188
) with a time-dependent
\vartheta_{\rm eff}
might arise if the fermion-dyon interaction involves operators that contain one extra derivative relative to those in (
40
), such as

\Delta L={\cal O}({\overline{{\bm{\chi}}}},{\bm{\chi}})\,D{\mathfrak{a}}\,,

(198)

where
{\cal O}
is an operator built from the bulk fermion field and
D{\mathfrak{a}}=\dot{\mathfrak{a}}-e\widehat{A}_{0}

as before. Taking the expectation value of this in the
in
vacuum shows that
\langle{\cal O}\rangle
plays the role of
\vartheta_{\rm eff}/(2\pi)
and this can be time-dependent (and calculable) if
\langle{\cal O}\rangle
is. In principle the operator we seek should satisfy

\partial_{t}{\cal O}\simeq\frac{1}{e}j^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle F}(\epsilon,t^{\prime})=-\frac{1}{2}j^{0}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}(\epsilon,t^{\prime})\,.

(199)

in order to ensure that (
192
) is true.

This last condition suggests a guess for what the operator
{\cal O}
should be. In 1+1 dimensions our two Dirac fermions
\chi_{\mathfrak{s}}
can be bosonized into two real scalars
\phi_{\mathfrak{s}}
, where the map between bosons and fermions implies – see also (
52
)

j^{\alpha}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}(\epsilon,t)\propto\partial^{\alpha}\phi_{+}-\partial^{\alpha}\phi_{-}\,.

(200)

Comparing this to (
199
) suggests that the operator
{\cal O}
we seek has a simple expression in terms of the bosonized field:
{\cal O}\propto\phi_{+}-\phi_{-}
. If so then (
198
) represents a dyon-localized kinetic mixing between
\phi_{\mathfrak{s}}
and
{\mathfrak{a}}
.

We have not yet found a convincing derivation of why
\Delta L
of the form (
198
) is generated once the fermions are integrated out, or why it arises with the right coefficients. But the above discussion reinforces earlier work
[
9
,
34
,
10
,
40
]
that suggests that the bosonized formulation of the scattered fermions might be more useful for understanding how the dyonic excitation
{\mathfrak{a}}
evolves.

7
Conclusions

This paper identifies an EFT description of fermion-dyon interactions and uses this to compute several simple reaction rates in the presence of a dyon. This is done by adapting a framework – point-particle effective field theories (PPEFTs) – that are designed to describe how small compact sources interact with surrounding relativistic ‘bulk’ fields.

As typical for an EFT approach the starting point is an effective action – in this case a world-line action that describes how bulk fields couple to one another and to dyon-localized degrees of freedom (such as collective coordinates). Such an action is organized as usual as a succession of operators of higher and higher dimension, with the lowest-dimension operators dominating at low energies. For the dyon case the important terms in this action governing the interactions with light fermions are given in (
37
) and are not that remarkable since they resemble the kinds of terms that also arise in more prosaic examples (like those describing nuclei within atoms
[
26
]
).

The main novelty in PPEFT methods is an algorithm that maps this effective action onto a set of boundary conditions for the bulk fields as they approach the source. These boundary conditions depend explicitly on the couplings appearing in the effective action and this is ultimately how the news about the nature of the source reaches the observables of low-energy bulk physics. For fermions interacting with dyons this leads to the boundary conditions (
45
) or (
46
), which are also not that different from the kinds of things that appear for fermions interacting with other compact sources.

The novelty of dyons and monopoles enters once one projects the bulk fields onto the
S
-wave mode that dominates the interactions at low energies since the dyon’s magnetic charge gives this mode a distinctive angular dependence that is not shared by
S
-waves for sources without magnetic charges. The projection of the low-energy physics onto this
S
-wave allows the full 4D bulk physics to be described by an effective 2D bulk action that couples to a boundary situated outside of the underlying dyon field at
r=\epsilon
, with the effects of dyonic structure communicated through the boundary conditions at
r=\epsilon
following from the PPEFT action. For dyons the
S
-wave projection become scale invariant in a way that excludes the emergence of a microscopic length scale
R_{*}
on which low-energy observables can depend, leading to potentially enormous interaction cross sections that are not suppressed at all by powers of the physical size of the underlying source (unlike for nuclei in atoms).

We show how the resulting dynamics of the bulk fermion coupled to the dyon’s ‘rotor’ mode captures the well-known fermion-dyon physics, but in a way that is very generally characterized by only a few parameters –
e.g.
the
{\cal T}_{\rm in}^{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}
of (
72
) – whose values can be obtained by matching with microscopic physics for specific dyon configurations. Our general expressions for
e.g.
scattering as a function of the
{\cal T}_{\rm in}^{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}
reduce to those of the literature once these matched values are used. These expressions also capture how scattering includes the nonperturbative effects of fermion-rotor interactions as studied in
[
39
]
, which can be regarded as providing a more complicated matching prescription to the microscopic physics that changes the values found for the
{\cal T}_{\rm in}^{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}
but not the expressions for how observables depend on these parameters.

We also explore how the fermion scattering causes the dyonic excitions to evolve and identify an effective hamiltonian that captures the dynamics required by the model’s conservation laws at low energy. Although we do not yet have a microscopic derivation of this Hamiltonian we explore several preliminary options.

Appendix A
Gamma-matrix conventions

This appendix summarizes our Dirac matrix conventions in both four and two dimensions.

A.1
4D Dirac matrices

We follow Weinberg’s spinor and metric conventions, so our metric has signature
(-,+,+,+)
and a Weyl representation for the gamma matrices in four dimensions is given by

\gamma^{0}=-i\,\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&&I\\
I&&0\end{array}\right),\quad\gamma^{j}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&&-i\sigma_{j}\\
i\sigma_{j}&&0\end{array}\right),

(201)

where
I
is the
2
by
2
unit matrix and
\sigma_{j}
are Pauli matrices (acting in spin space, as opposed to the Pauli matrices
\tau_{a}
acting on gauge doublet indices). These satisfy the Clifford algebra
\left\{\gamma^{\mu},\gamma^{\nu}\right\}=2\eta^{\mu\nu}
where
\eta^{\mu\nu}
is the inverse Minkowski metric, and the representation is chosen to diagonalize

\gamma^{5}=-i\gamma^{0}\gamma^{1}\gamma^{2}\gamma^{3}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}I&&0\\
0&&-I\end{array}\right)\,.

(202)

Dirac conjugation in these conventions is given by
\overline{\psi}=i\psi^{\dagger}\gamma^{0}
. In these conventions the left- and right-handed chirality projectors are
\gamma_{\scriptscriptstyle L}:=\frac{1}{2}(1+\gamma_{5})
and
\gamma_{\scriptscriptstyle R}:=\frac{1}{2}(1-\gamma_{5})
.

Spherical coordinates

The gamma matrices adapted to spherical coordinates are given in terms of the unit coordinate vectors

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{r}}:=\hat{r}_{i}\,{\bf e}_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sin\theta\cos{\phi}\,{\bf e}_{x}+\sin\theta\sin\phi\,{\bf e}_{y}+\cos\theta\,{\bf e}_{z},

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{\theta}}:=\hat{\theta}_{i}\,{\bf e}_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\cos\theta\cos{\phi}\,{\bf e}_{x}+\cos\theta\sin\phi\,{\bf e}_{y}-\sin\theta\,{\bf e}_{z},

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{\phi}}:=\hat{\phi}_{i}\,{\bf e}_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sin{\phi}\,{\bf e}_{x}+\cos\phi\,{\bf e}_{y}.

(203)

by

\gamma^{r}:=\gamma^{i}\,\hat{r}_{i}
,
\gamma^{\theta}:=\gamma^{i}\,\hat{\theta}_{i}
and
\gamma^{\phi}:=\gamma^{i}\,\hat{\phi}_{i}/\sin\theta
,
and so

\gamma^{r}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&&-i\sigma^{r}\\
i\sigma^{r}&&0\end{array}\right)\,,\quad\gamma^{\theta}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&&-i\sigma^{\theta}\\
i\sigma^{\theta}&&0\end{array}\right)\quad\hbox{and}\quad\gamma^{\phi}=\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\,\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&&-i\sigma^{\phi}\\
i\sigma^{\phi}&&0\end{array}\right).

(204)

where the Pauli matrices in spherical coordinates are defined by

\sigma^{r}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\cos\theta&e^{-i\phi}\sin\theta\\
e^{i\phi}\sin\theta&-\cos\theta\end{array}\right),\;\sigma^{\theta}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-\sin\theta&e^{-i\phi}\cos\theta\\
e^{i\phi}\cos\theta&\sin\theta\end{array}\right)\;\hbox{and}\;\;\sigma^{\phi}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&-ie^{-i\phi}\\
ie^{i\phi}&0\end{array}\right).

(205)

A.2
2D Dirac matrices

In
D=1+1
spacetime dimensions we label coordinates with
x^{\alpha}=\{t,r\}
for
\alpha=0,1
and use the following representation of gamma matrices

\Gamma^{0}=-i\sigma_{1},\quad\Gamma^{1}=\sigma_{2}\,.

(206)

These satisfy the algebra
\{\Gamma^{\alpha},\Gamma^{\beta}\}=2\eta^{\alpha\beta}
, where
\eta^{\alpha\beta}=\hbox{diag}(-1,1)
is the inverse Minkowski metric in
1+1
dimensions. The chiral matrix in 1+1 dimensions we then define to be

\Gamma_{c}:=\Gamma^{0}\Gamma^{1}=\sigma_{3}\,,

(207)

which has eigenvalues
\pm 1
(and is diagonal in the basis used here). Notice that these definitions imply
\Gamma_{c}\Gamma^{\alpha}=\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}\Gamma_{\beta}
where our Levi-Civita convention chooses
\epsilon^{01}=+1
. Dirac conjugation is again given by
\overline{\chi}=i\chi^{\dagger}\Gamma^{0}
and in these conventions the left- and right-handed helicity projectors are
\Gamma_{+}:=\frac{1}{2}(1+\Gamma_{c})
and
\Gamma_{-}:=\frac{1}{2}(1-\Gamma_{c})
.

Appendix B
PPEFTs and relativistic fermions

This Appendix summarizes the results of
[
26
]
describing how PPEFTs couple to relativistic fermions. Formally we wish to couple a Dirac fermion to a point-like source located at the origin

S=-\int{\rm d}^{4}x\Bigl[\overline{\psi}(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to8.55695pt{\hfil/\hfil}\hss}D+m)\psi+\overline{\psi}N\psi\,\delta^{3}(x)\Bigr]\,,

(208)

where
N
is a Dirac matrix. For definiteness in this Appendix we follow
[
26
]
and consider the rotational and parity invariant case
N=\hat{\mathfrak{c}}_{s}+i\hat{\mathfrak{c}}_{v}\gamma^{0}
where
\hat{\mathfrak{c}}_{s}
and
\hat{\mathfrak{c}}_{v}
are coupling constants (but more general possibilities are entertained in the main text). The
\psi
equation of motion including the coupling to the source is

(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to8.55695pt{\hfil/\hfil}\hss}D+m)\psi+N\psi\,\delta^{3}(x)=0\,.

(209)

Formally we’d like to trade the delta function for a near-source boundary condition, and following usual practice this would be obtained by integrating (
209
) over a small Gaussian pillbox,
P
, of radius
\epsilon
centred on the source. This gives, in the limit
\epsilon\to 0
of vanishingly small pillbox, the result

\int_{\partial P}{\rm d}^{2}x\;n_{\mu}\gamma^{\mu}\psi=\int{\rm d}^{2}\Omega\,\epsilon^{2}\;\gamma^{r}\,\psi\simeq-N\psi(0)\,,

(210)

where the solid-angle measure is
{\rm d}^{2}\Omega=\sin\theta\,{\rm d}\theta{\rm d}\phi
and
n_{\mu}
is an outward-pointing unit normal to the pillbox so in polar coordinates
n_{\mu}{\rm d}x^{\mu}={\rm d}r
and
\psi(0)
denotes the value of the field at the position of the source. The final approximate equality drops the
m\psi
term, as is appropriate for a sufficiently small pillbox since this vanishes as
\epsilon\to 0
provided
\psi
is sufficiently smooth near the origin. Our conventions on gamma-matrices in polar coordinates are given in Appendix
A
.

B.1
Boundary action

The problem with the formal argument is that bulk fields like
\psi
are typically
not
smooth as
r\to 0
, which both complicates the neglect of the
m\psi
term when integrating (
209
) over the pillbox and makes
\psi(0)
undefined. The PPEFT way for dealing with both of these issues is essentially to regulate the source action by replacing it by a boundary action on the boundary of the pillbox
\partial P
at
r=\epsilon
. For configurations that are spherically symmetric
31
31

31

Non-spherically symmetric configurations can also be handled by decomposing into spherical harmonics and treating each harmonic separately on
\partial P
.
very near the source this is particularly simple to do by replacing the world-line action by its value integrated over
\partial P
:

\int_{r=0}{\rm d}t\;{\overline{\psi}}N\psi\to\frac{1}{4\pi\epsilon^{2}}\int_{\partial P}{\rm d}^{2}\Omega\,{\rm d}t\;\epsilon^{2}\,{\overline{\psi}}N\psi\,.

(211)

This procedure makes the replacement
N\psi(0)\to(4\pi\epsilon^{2})^{-1}\int{\rm d}^{2}\Omega\,\epsilon^{2}N(\epsilon)\psi(\epsilon)
on the right-hand side of (
210
), leading (in the limit where
\epsilon
is much smaller than all other scales of interest) to the regulated boundary condition

\int_{r=\epsilon}{\rm d}^{2}\Omega\;\left[\gamma^{r}+\frac{N}{4\pi\epsilon^{2}}\right]\psi=\int_{r=\epsilon}{\rm d}^{2}\Omega\;\left[\gamma^{r}+\frac{1}{4\pi\epsilon^{2}}\Bigl(\hat{\mathfrak{c}}_{s}+i\hat{\mathfrak{c}}_{v}\gamma^{0}\Bigr)\right]\psi=0\,.

(212)

Notice this boundary condition is trivially satisfied if we’d tried to make the same derivation using a pillbox that does
not
contain the source position. This is because in this case the
N\psi
term is no longer present and so the boundary condition states
\int{\rm d}^{2}\Omega\,\gamma^{r}\psi=0
. Since
\psi
varies very slowly in a small enough region not containing the source, it can be taken to be approximately constant across the pillbox and so the integral over all directions of
\gamma^{r}
gives zero trivially without restricting
\psi
.

Returning to the case where the pillbox does enclose the source, the boundary condition (
212
) can be written as
\int{\rm d}^{2}\Omega\;B_{\epsilon}\,\psi(\epsilon)=0
where

B_{\epsilon}:=\gamma^{r}+\frac{N}{4\pi\epsilon^{2}}=\gamma^{r}+\hat{\cal C}_{s}+i\hat{\cal C}_{v}\gamma^{0}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\hat{\cal C}_{s}&\hat{\cal C}_{v}-i\sigma^{r}\\
{}\hat{\cal C}_{v}+i\sigma^{r}&\hat{\cal C}_{s}\end{array}\right)\,,

(213)

where
\hat{\cal C}_{s}=\hat{\mathfrak{c}}_{s}/(4\pi\epsilon^{2})
and
\hat{\cal C}_{v}=\hat{\mathfrak{c}}_{v}/(4\pi\epsilon^{2})
are now dimensionless effective couplings. The subscript
\epsilon
on
B_{\epsilon}
is meant to emphasize that the constants
\hat{\cal C}_{a}
(and in general also the original couplings
\hat{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}
themselves) also must carry an implicit
\epsilon
-dependence if physical quantities are to remain unchanged as
\epsilon
is varied (more about which below). In terms of the left- and right-handed parts of
\psi
the boundary condition becomes

-\hat{\cal C}_{s}\int_{\epsilon}{\rm d}^{2}\Omega\,\psi^{\pm}_{\scriptscriptstyle L}=\int_{\epsilon}{\rm d}^{2}\Omega\,\Bigl(\hat{\cal C}_{v}-i\sigma^{r}\Bigr)\psi^{\pm}_{\scriptscriptstyle R}\quad\hbox{and}\quad-\int_{\epsilon}{\rm d}^{2}\Omega\,\Bigl(\hat{\cal C}_{v}+i\sigma^{r}\Bigr)\,\psi^{\pm}_{\scriptscriptstyle L}=\hat{\cal C}_{s}\int_{\epsilon}{\rm d}^{2}\Omega\,\psi^{\pm}_{\scriptscriptstyle R}\,.

(214)

To see what these boundary conditions imply, imagine solving the bulk equation
(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to8.55695pt{\hfil/\hfil}\hss}D+m)\psi=0
for
r>\epsilon
and decomposing the result into rotation and parity eigenstates. The parity-even solutions are

\psi^{+}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\psi^{+}_{\scriptscriptstyle L}\\
\psi^{+}_{\scriptscriptstyle R}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}f_{+}(r)\,U^{+}(\theta,\phi)+ig_{+}(r)\,U^{-}(\theta,\phi)\\
f_{+}(r)\,U^{+}(\theta,\phi)-ig_{+}(r)\,U^{-}(\theta,\phi)\end{array}\right)\,,

(215)

while the parity-odd ones are

\psi^{-}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\psi^{-}_{\scriptscriptstyle L}\\
\psi^{-}_{\scriptscriptstyle R}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}f_{-}(r)\,U^{-}(\theta,\phi)+ig_{-}(r)\,U^{+}(\theta,\phi)\\
f_{-}(r)\,U^{-}(\theta,\phi)-ig_{-}(r)\,U^{+}(\theta,\phi)\end{array}\right)\,,

(216)

where
U^{\pm}
are the spinor harmonics that combine the particle’s spin-half with orbital angular momenta
\ell=j\mp\frac{1}{2}
to give total angular momentum
j=\frac{1}{2},\,\frac{3}{2},\cdots
. The radial functions
f_{\pm}(r)
and
g_{\pm}(r)
with mode frequency
\omega
solve the radial equations

f_{+}^{\prime}=\left(m+\omega\right)g_{+}\quad\hbox{and}\quad g_{+}^{\prime}+\frac{2g_{+}}{r}=\left(m-\omega\right)f_{+}\,,

(217)

together with

g_{-}^{\prime}=\left(m-\omega\right)f_{-}\quad\hbox{and}\quad f_{-}^{\prime}+\frac{2f_{-}}{r}=\left(m+\omega\right)g_{-}\,,

(218)

where primes denote differentiation with respect to
r
. The boundary conditions (
214
) fix the ratio of the functions
f
and
g
at
r=\epsilon
once the angular integrations are performed, giving

\hat{\cal C}_{s}+\hat{\cal C}_{v}=\left(\frac{g_{+}}{f_{+}}\right)_{r=\epsilon}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad\hat{\cal C}_{s}-\hat{\cal C}_{v}=\left(\frac{f_{-}}{g_{-}}\right)_{r=\epsilon}\,.

(219)

B.2
RG evolution

Eq. (
219
) provides the solution to how the properties of the source influence the bulk solutions for
\psi
in the source’s vicinity. Given the general solution,

f_{\pm}(r)=C^{\pm}_{1}f_{1\pm}(r)+C^{\pm}_{2}f_{2\pm}(r)\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad g_{\pm}(r)=C^{\pm}_{1}g_{1\pm}(r)+C^{\pm}_{2}g_{2\pm}(r)\,,

(220)

to the radial part of the Dirac field equation we see that (
219
) show that the couplings
\hat{\cal C}_{s}
and
\hat{\cal C}_{v}
determine the ratios of integration constants
C^{+}_{2}/C^{+}_{1}
and
C^{-}_{2}/C^{-}_{1}
that specify
(g_{\pm}/f_{\pm})_{r=\epsilon}
. Energy levels for states of either parity and scattering amplitudes are then determined by the values of
C^{\pm}_{2}/C^{\pm}_{1}
.

But it is still a potential puzzle why physical predictions can depend on the radius,
r=\epsilon
, of the Gaussian pillbox which is not a physical scale (arising just as a way to regularize the boundary conditions). The precise value of
\epsilon
must therefore drop out of predictions for observables (unlike the physical size,
R
, of the underlying source, say). In detail, this happens because any explicit
\epsilon
-dependence arising in a calculation of an observable cancels an implicit
\epsilon
-dependence buried within the ‘bare’ quantities
\hat{\mathfrak{c}}_{s}
and
\hat{\mathfrak{c}}_{v}
. Physical predictions remain
\epsilon
-independent if
\hat{\mathfrak{c}}_{s}(\epsilon)
and
\hat{\mathfrak{c}}_{v}(\epsilon)
are chosen to ensure the ratios
C^{\pm}_{2}/C^{\pm}_{1}
are held fixed as
\epsilon
is varied.

This gives us another way to interpret eq. (
219
). Rather than reading (
219
) as fixing
f_{\pm}/g_{\pm}
at a specific radius given known values of
\epsilon
,
\hat{\mathfrak{c}}_{s}
and
\hat{\mathfrak{c}}_{v}
we can instead read the equations

\hat{\mathfrak{c}}_{s}(\epsilon)=\left[\frac{g_{+}(\epsilon)}{f_{+}(\epsilon)}+\frac{f_{-}(\epsilon)}{g_{-}(\epsilon)}\right]2\pi\epsilon^{2}\quad\hbox{and}\quad\hat{\mathfrak{c}}_{v}(\epsilon)=\left[\frac{g_{+}(\epsilon)}{f_{+}(\epsilon)}-\frac{f_{-}(\epsilon)}{g_{-}(\epsilon)}\right]2\pi\epsilon^{2}\,,

(221)

as telling us how
\hat{\mathfrak{c}}_{s}(\epsilon)
and
\hat{\mathfrak{c}}_{v}(\epsilon)
must depend on
\epsilon
in order to ensure that
C^{\pm}_{2}/C^{\pm}_{1}
remains
\epsilon
-independent. Since we choose
\epsilon
much smaller than the typical scale of the external problem (such as the Bohr radius, for applications to atoms), it suffices to use the leading small-
r
form of the solutions
f_{\pm}
and
g_{\pm}
when using (
221
). In this regime solutions are usually well described by power laws, with (
220
) reducing to

f_{\pm}(r)=C^{\pm}_{1}\left(\frac{r}{a}\right)^{{\mathfrak{z}}-1}+C^{\pm}_{2}\left(\frac{r}{a}\right)^{-{\mathfrak{z}}-1}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad g_{\pm}(r)=\widetilde{C}^{\pm}_{1}\left(\frac{r}{a}\right)^{{\mathfrak{z}}-1}+\widetilde{C}^{\pm}_{2}\left(\frac{r}{a}\right)^{-{\mathfrak{z}}-1}\,,

(222)

with
\widetilde{C}^{\pm}_{i}\propto C^{\pm}_{i}
in a way that depends on the relative small-
r
asymptotic behaviour of
f_{i}(r)
and
g_{i}(r)
. For such solutions the choice of
C^{\pm}_{2}/C^{\pm}_{1}
controls the precise radius at which one of these solutions dominates the other one, and as a result the RG evolution of the couplings implied by (
221
) in this regime describes the cross-over between these two types of evolution.

Appendix C
Regularized codimension-1 boundary action

This Appendix addresses the question of how to regularize the fermion-dyon interactions defined on the dyon world-line, which appear in equations such as (
40
).

As argued in the main text, the lowest-dimension interactions between the fermion doublet
{\bm{\psi}}(x)
and the dyon collective coordinates
y^{\mu}(t),{\mathfrak{a}}(t)
are given by

\displaystyle S^{\rm int}_{\rm dyon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\int{\rm d}t\,{\overline{\bm{\psi}}}\,{\mathfrak{C}}({\mathfrak{a}})\,\bm{\psi}

(223)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\int{\rm d}t\,{\overline{\bm{\psi}}}\Bigl[\hat{\mathfrak{c}}^{s}_{1}+i\,\hat{\mathfrak{c}}^{ps}_{1}\gamma_{5}-i\,\hat{\mathfrak{c}}^{v}_{1}\gamma^{0}-i\,\hat{\mathfrak{c}}^{pv}_{1}\gamma_{5}\gamma^{0}+\Bigl(\hat{\mathfrak{c}}^{s}_{3}+i\,\hat{\mathfrak{c}}^{ps}_{3}\gamma_{5}-i\,\hat{\mathfrak{c}}^{v}_{3}\gamma^{0}-i\,\hat{\mathfrak{c}}^{pv}_{3}\gamma_{5}\gamma^{0}\Bigr)\tau_{3}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\Bigl(\hat{\mathfrak{c}}^{s}_{+}+i\,\hat{\mathfrak{c}}^{ps}_{+}\gamma_{5}-i\,\hat{\mathfrak{c}}^{v}_{+}\gamma^{0}-i\,\hat{\mathfrak{c}}^{pv}_{+}\gamma_{5}\gamma^{0}\Bigr)e^{i{\mathfrak{a}}}\tau_{+}+\Bigl(\hat{\mathfrak{c}}^{s}_{-}+i\,\hat{\mathfrak{c}}^{ps}_{-}\gamma_{5}-i\,\hat{\mathfrak{c}}^{v}_{-}\gamma^{0}-i\,\hat{\mathfrak{c}}^{pv}_{-}\gamma_{5}\gamma^{0}\Bigr)e^{-i{\mathfrak{a}}}\tau_{-}\Bigr]\,\bm{\psi}

where
{\bm{\psi}}
is evaluated at
\bm{r}=0
and we specialize to the dyon rest frame and neglect dyon recoil effects so that
\dot{y}^{\mu}\approx\delta^{\mu}_{0}
.
We can regulate the operators appearing in
S^{\rm int}_{\rm dyon}
by replacing them with appropriate interaction terms, defined on the boundary of a Gaussian pillbox at
r=\epsilon
. Appendix
B
shows that for fermion field configurations which are spherically symmetric near the source, the regularization procedure amounts to replacing fermion bilinears such as
{\overline{\bm{\psi}}}M{\bm{\psi}}
, where
M
is a matrix in spin and isospin space, with their average over the pillbox boundary
({4\pi\epsilon^{2}})^{-1}\int{\rm d}^{2}\Omega\,\epsilon^{2}\,{\overline{\bm{\psi}}}(\epsilon){M}{\bm{\psi}}(\epsilon)
. This is a valid prescription for operators in (
223
) describing
\psi_{+},\psi_{-}
self-interactions (mediated by
\hat{\mathfrak{c}}_{1},\hat{\mathfrak{c}}_{3}
). For operators that couple
\psi_{+}
and
\psi_{-}
we must use another prescription however, since the angular dependence of the two components of the doublet is different, even when restricting to the same partial wave. As a result, regularized fermion bilinears such as
{\overline{\bm{\psi}}}(\epsilon)\,\hat{\mathfrak{c}}^{s}_{+}\tau_{+}{\bm{\psi}}(\epsilon)
are generally not rotation-invariant and so can vanish after integration over the boundary of the pillbox. This can be seen explicitly by specializing to
S
-wave states for which we get
e.g.
:

\frac{1}{4\pi\epsilon^{2}}\int{\rm d}^{2}\Omega\,\epsilon^{2}\,{\overline{\bm{\psi}}}(\epsilon)\,\hat{\mathfrak{c}}^{s}_{+}\tau_{+}{\bm{\psi}}(\epsilon)=\frac{f^{*}_{+}(\epsilon,t)\,g_{-}(\epsilon,t)+g^{*}_{+}(\epsilon,t)\,f_{-}(\epsilon,t)}{4\pi\epsilon^{2}}\hat{\mathfrak{c}}^{s}_{+}\int{{\rm d}^{2}\Omega}\,\eta^{\dagger}_{+}\,\eta_{-}=0,

(224)

in the gauge where the Julia-Zee solution has the form (
8
) and
S
-wave fermions are given by (
24
). That the form of angular momentum eigenstates depends on their electric charge can be traced back to the expression for the angular momentum operator
\vec{J}
, which includes a contribution from the gauge isospin
\vec{T}
, as in (
23
).

To couple the two components of the doublet at the
r=\epsilon
boundary, it suffices to introduce additional matrices
P_{\pm}
, which turn the angular dependence of
\psi_{+}
into that of
\psi_{-}
and vice versa. For
S
-wave states and in the ‘abelian’ gauge of (
8
), these matrices act in spin space and are defined through:

P_{+}\,\frac{1}{r}\begin{pmatrix}f_{+}(r,t)\,\eta_{+}\\
g_{+}(r,t)\,\eta_{+}\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{r}\begin{pmatrix}f_{+}(r,t)\,\eta_{-}\\
g_{+}(r,t)\,\eta_{-}\end{pmatrix},\quad\text{and}\quad P_{-}\,\frac{1}{r}\begin{pmatrix}f_{-}(r,t)\,\eta_{-}\\
g_{-}(r,t)\,\eta_{-}\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{r}\begin{pmatrix}f_{-}(r,t)\,\eta_{+}\\
g_{-}(r,t)\,\eta_{+}\end{pmatrix}.

(225)

Since these equations do not uniqely determine
P_{\pm}
, we further choose:

P_{-}\,\frac{1}{r}\begin{pmatrix}f_{+}(r,t)\,\eta_{+}\\
g_{+}(r,t)\,\eta_{+}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\end{pmatrix},\quad\text{and}\quad P_{+}\,\frac{1}{r}\begin{pmatrix}f_{-}(r,t)\,\eta_{-}\\
g_{-}(r,t)\,\eta_{-}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\end{pmatrix},

(226)

which leads us to the following expressions for
P_{\pm}
in the
R_{-}
patch

P_{+}=-\frac{ie^{i\phi}}{2}\begin{pmatrix}i\sigma_{\theta}+\sigma_{\phi}&&0\\
0&&i\sigma_{\theta}+\sigma_{\phi}\end{pmatrix},\quad\text{and}\quad P_{-}=-\frac{ie^{-i\phi}}{2}\begin{pmatrix}i\sigma_{\theta}-\sigma_{\phi}&&0\\
0&&i\sigma_{\theta}-\sigma_{\phi}\end{pmatrix},

(227)

as well as
P^{\prime}_{\pm}\coloneqq e^{\mp 2i\phi}P_{\pm}
in the
R_{+}
patch. It follows that for
S
-wave states, the world-line interactions in (
223
) can be regulated using the following boundary action:

\displaystyle I^{\rm int}_{\rm dyon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\underset{r=\epsilon}{\int}{\rm d}t\,{\rm d}^{2}\Omega\,\epsilon^{2}\,{\overline{\bm{\psi}}}\Bigl[\hat{{\cal C}}^{s}_{1}+i\,\hat{{\cal C}}^{ps}_{1}\gamma_{5}-i\,\hat{{\cal C}}^{v}_{1}\gamma^{0}-i\,\hat{{\cal C}}^{pv}_{1}\gamma_{5}\gamma^{0}+\Bigl(\hat{{\cal C}}^{s}_{3}+i\,\hat{{\cal C}}^{ps}_{3}\gamma_{5}-i\,\hat{{\cal C}}^{v}_{3}\gamma^{0}-i\,\hat{{\cal C}}^{pv}_{3}\gamma_{5}\gamma^{0}\Bigr)\tau_{3}

(228)

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\Bigl(\hat{{\cal C}}^{s}_{+}+i\,\hat{{\cal C}}^{ps}_{+}\gamma_{5}-i\,\hat{{\cal C}}^{v}_{+}\gamma^{0}-i\,\hat{{\cal C}}^{pv}_{+}\gamma_{5}\gamma^{0}\Bigr)e^{i{\mathfrak{a}}}P_{-}\,\tau_{+}+\Bigl(\hat{{\cal C}}^{s}_{-}+i\,\hat{{\cal C}}^{ps}_{-}\gamma_{5}-i\,\hat{{\cal C}}^{v}_{-}\gamma^{0}-i\,\hat{{\cal C}}^{pv}_{-}\gamma_{5}\gamma^{0}\Bigr)e^{-i{\mathfrak{a}}}P_{+}\,\tau_{-}\Bigr]\,\bm{\psi}

where we introduce the boundary couplings
\hat{{\cal C}}^{A}_{I}=\frac{1}{4\pi\epsilon^{2}}\,\hat{\mathfrak{c}}^{A}_{I}
. The boundary condition satisfied by
S
-wave fermions is then given by

\displaystyle 0=\Bigl\{\Bigl[\gamma^{r}+\Bigl(\hat{{\cal C}}^{s}_{1}+i\,\hat{{\cal C}}^{ps}_{1}\gamma_{5}-i\,\hat{{\cal C}}^{v}_{1}\gamma^{0}-i\,\hat{{\cal C}}^{pv}_{1}\gamma_{5}\gamma^{0}\Bigr)+\Bigl(\hat{{\cal C}}^{s}_{3}+i\,\hat{{\cal C}}^{ps}_{3}\gamma_{5}-i\,\hat{{\cal C}}^{v}_{3}\gamma^{0}-i\,\hat{{\cal C}}^{pv}_{3}\gamma_{5}\gamma^{0}\Bigr)\tau_{3}

(229)

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\Bigl(\hat{{\cal C}}^{s}_{+}+i\,\hat{{\cal C}}^{ps}_{+}\gamma_{5}-i\,\hat{{\cal C}}^{v}_{+}\gamma^{0}-i\,\hat{{\cal C}}^{pv}_{+}\gamma_{5}\gamma^{0}\Bigr)e^{i{\mathfrak{a}}}P_{-}\,\tau_{+}+\Bigl(\hat{{\cal C}}^{s}_{-}+i\,\hat{{\cal C}}^{ps}_{-}\gamma_{5}-i\,\hat{{\cal C}}^{v}_{-}\gamma^{0}-i\,\hat{{\cal C}}^{pv}_{-}\gamma_{5}\gamma^{0}\Bigr)e^{-i{\mathfrak{a}}}P_{+}\,\tau_{-}\Bigr]{\bm{\psi}}\Bigr\}_{r=\epsilon},

instead of (
46
) and is equivalent to the
2
D boundary condition (
49
) when the
4
D and
2
D boundary couplings are related as follows:

{\cal C}^{s}_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}}=\hat{{\cal C}}^{s}_{1}+{\mathfrak{s}}\hat{{\cal C}}^{s}_{3},\quad{\cal C}^{ps}_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}}={\mathfrak{s}}\hat{{\cal C}}^{ps}_{1}+\hat{{\cal C}}^{ps}_{3},\quad{\cal C}^{v}_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}}=\hat{{\cal C}}^{v}_{1}+{\mathfrak{s}}\hat{{\cal C}}^{v}_{3},\quad{\cal C}^{pv}_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}}=-({\mathfrak{s}}\hat{{\cal C}}^{pv}_{1}+\hat{{\cal C}}^{pv}_{3})

(230)

as well as

{\cal C}^{s}_{+-}=-\hat{{\cal C}}^{v}_{+},\quad{\cal C}^{ps}_{+-}=i\hat{{\cal C}}^{pv}_{+},\quad{\cal C}^{v}_{+-}=-\hat{{\cal C}}^{s}_{+},\quad{\cal C}^{pv}_{+-}=i\hat{{\cal C}}^{ps}_{+}

(231)

and

{\cal C}^{s}_{-+}=-\hat{{\cal C}}^{v}_{-},\quad{\cal C}^{ps}_{-+}=-i\hat{{\cal C}}^{pv}_{-},\quad{\cal C}^{v}_{-+}=-\hat{{\cal C}}^{s}_{-},\quad{\cal C}^{pv}_{-+}=-i\hat{{\cal C}}^{ps}_{-}.

(232)

Notice that there is a mismatch between
2
D and
4
D chirality, which explains why some (pseudo)scalar and (pseudo)vector
4
D couplings correspond to (pseudo)vector and (pseudo)scalar
2
D couplings respectively, as well as the presence of additional minus signs in the matching of pseudoscalar and pseudovector couplings.

The
S
-wave boundary action could have equivalently been formulated in the original spherical gauge of (
3
), in which we get:

\displaystyle I^{\rm int}_{\rm dyon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\underset{r=\epsilon}{\int}{\rm d}t\,{\rm d}^{2}\Omega\,\epsilon^{2}\,{\overline{\bm{\psi}}}\Bigl[\hat{{\cal C}}^{s}_{1}+i\,\hat{{\cal C}}^{ps}_{1}\gamma_{5}-i\,\hat{{\cal C}}^{v}_{1}\gamma^{0}-i\,\hat{{\cal C}}^{pv}_{1}\gamma_{5}\gamma^{0}+\Bigl(\hat{{\cal C}}^{s}_{3}+i\,\hat{{\cal C}}^{ps}_{3}\gamma_{5}-i\,\hat{{\cal C}}^{v}_{3}\gamma^{0}-i\,\hat{{\cal C}}^{pv}_{3}\gamma_{5}\gamma^{0}\Bigr)(-\tau_{r})

(233)

\displaystyle-\Bigl(\hat{{\cal C}}^{s}_{+}+i\,\hat{{\cal C}}^{ps}_{+}\gamma_{5}-i\,\hat{{\cal C}}^{v}_{+}\gamma^{0}-i\,\hat{{\cal C}}^{pv}_{+}\gamma_{5}\gamma^{0}\Bigr)e^{i{\mathfrak{a}}}P_{-}\,\tau^{-r}_{+}-\Bigl(\hat{{\cal C}}^{s}_{-}+i\,\hat{{\cal C}}^{ps}_{-}\gamma_{5}-i\,\hat{{\cal C}}^{v}_{-}\gamma^{0}-i\,\hat{{\cal C}}^{pv}_{-}\gamma_{5}\gamma^{0}\Bigr)e^{-i{\mathfrak{a}}}P_{+}\,\tau^{-r}_{-}\Bigr]\,\bm{\psi},

where
P_{\pm}
are given by (
227
) and
\tau^{-r}_{\pm}\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}e^{\pm i\phi}\left(\tau_{\theta}\mp i\tau_{\phi}\right)
act as raising and lowering operators on eigenstates of
-\tau_{r}
, that is

\tau^{-r}_{+}\eta_{+}(\theta,\phi)=\eta_{-}(\theta,\phi),\quad\tau^{-r}_{-}\eta_{-}(\theta,\phi)=\eta_{+}(\theta,\phi),\quad\text{and}\quad\tau^{-r}_{-}\eta_{+}(\theta,\phi)=\tau^{-r}_{+}\eta_{-}(\theta,\phi)=0,

(234)

where
\eta_{\pm}(\theta,\phi)
are vectors in isospin space given by (
25
), which satisfy
(-\tau_{r})\eta_{\pm}(\theta,\phi)=\mp\eta_{\pm}(\theta,\phi)
.

Appendix D
Properties of the amplitudes
\mathcal{T}_{{\rm in}},\mathcal{T}_{{\rm out}}

This appendix derives and summarizes several useful properties of the amplitudes
\mathcal{T}_{{\rm in}}
and
\mathcal{T}_{{\rm out}}
that appear in the construction of the
in
and
out
states. We assume when doing so that the dyon-fermion action is real (and so the couplings
{\cal C}^{\scriptscriptstyle A}_{ij}
are hermitian).

D.1
Definition of the amplitudes

The main text shows that the
\mathcal{T}_{{\rm in}},\mathcal{T}_{{\rm out}}
amplitudes are given in terms of the boundary matrix
\hat{\mathcal{B}}
by

\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm in}}=-\frac{\left|\begin{array}[]{ll}\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{21}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{24}\\
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{41}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{44}\end{array}\right|}{\left|\begin{array}[]{ll}\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{22}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{24}\\
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{42}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{44}\end{array}\right|},{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}=-\frac{\left|\begin{array}[]{ll}\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{23}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{24}\\
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{43}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{44}\end{array}\right|}{\left|\begin{array}[]{ll}\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{22}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{24}\\
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{42}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{44}\end{array}\right|},{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm in}}=-\frac{\left|\begin{array}[]{ll}\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{22}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{21}\\
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{42}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{41}\end{array}\right|}{\left|\begin{array}[]{ll}\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{22}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{24}\\
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{42}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{44}\end{array}\right|},\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}=-\frac{\left|\begin{array}[]{ll}\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{22}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{23}\\
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{42}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{43}\end{array}\right|}{\left|\begin{array}[]{ll}\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{22}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{24}\\
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{42}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{44}\end{array}\right|},

(235)

and

\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm out}}=-\frac{\left|\begin{array}[]{ll}\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{12}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{13}\\
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{32}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{33}\end{array}\right|}{\left|\begin{array}[]{ll}\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{11}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{13}\\
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{31}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{33}\end{array}\right|},{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm out}}=-\frac{\left|\begin{array}[]{ll}\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{11}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{12}\\
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{31}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{32}\end{array}\right|}{\left|\begin{array}[]{ll}\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{11}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{13}\\
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{31}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{33}\end{array}\right|},{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm out}}=-\frac{\left|\begin{array}[]{ll}\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{14}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{13}\\
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{34}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{33}\end{array}\right|}{\left|\begin{array}[]{ll}\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{11}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{13}\\
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{31}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{33}\end{array}\right|},\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm out}}=-\frac{\left|\begin{array}[]{ll}\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{11}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{14}\\
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{31}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{34}\end{array}\right|}{\left|\begin{array}[]{ll}\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{11}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{13}\\
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{31}&\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{33}\end{array}\right|}.

(236)

Written directly in terms of the boundary couplings, these become

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm in}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(\mathcal{C}^{s}_{++}+i\mathcal{C}^{ps}_{++})(i-\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{--}+\mathcal{C}^{v}_{--})+(\mathcal{C}^{s}_{-+}+i\mathcal{C}^{ps}_{-+})(\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{+-}-\mathcal{C}^{v}_{+-})}{-|\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{+-}-\mathcal{C}^{v}_{+-}|^{2}+(-i+\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{++}-\mathcal{C}^{v}_{++})(\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{--}-\mathcal{C}^{v}_{--}-i)},

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(\mathcal{C}^{s}_{--}+i\mathcal{C}^{ps}_{--})(i-\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{++}+\mathcal{C}^{v}_{++})+(\mathcal{C}^{s}_{+-}+i\mathcal{C}^{ps}_{+-})(\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{-+}-\mathcal{C}^{v}_{-+})}{-|\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{+-}-\mathcal{C}^{v}_{+-}|^{2}+(-i+\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{++}-\mathcal{C}^{v}_{++})(\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{--}-\mathcal{C}^{v}_{--}-i)},

(237)

\displaystyle{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm in}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(\mathcal{C}^{s}_{-+}+i\mathcal{C}^{ps}_{-+})(i-\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{++}+\mathcal{C}^{v}_{++})+(\mathcal{C}^{s}_{++}+i\mathcal{C}^{ps}_{++})(\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{-+}-\mathcal{C}^{v}_{-+})}{-|\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{+-}-\mathcal{C}^{v}_{+-}|^{2}+(-i+\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{++}-\mathcal{C}^{v}_{++})(\mathcal{C}^{pv}_{--}-\mathcal{C}^{v}_{--}-i)},

\displaystyle{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}

\displaystyle=
