]
respectively, with an implied ‘monopole strength’ of
q=\frac{\mathfrak{s}}{2}
. We find the coefficients of interest
A^{{\mathfrak{c}}}_{0,\mathfrak{s}},B^{{\mathfrak{c}}}_{0,\mathfrak{s}}
by multiplying the above equation with
\xi^{\dagger}_{\mathfrak{s}}\otimes\begin{pmatrix}{\eta}^{\dagger}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\theta,\phi)&0\end{pmatrix}
,
\xi^{\dagger}_{\mathfrak{s}}\otimes\begin{pmatrix}0&{\eta}^{\dagger}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\theta,\phi)\end{pmatrix}
and performing the angular integrals. This gives

A^{{\mathfrak{c}}}_{0,\mathfrak{s}}=\delta_{\mathfrak{c},+}\int\mathrm{d}^{2}\Omega\,\eta^{\dagger}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\theta,\phi)\begin{pmatrix}0\\
e^{i\varphi^{\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle U}(\theta)}\end{pmatrix}\delta(1+\cos\theta)=\delta_{\mathfrak{c},+}\delta_{\mathfrak{s},+}e^{i\varphi^{\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle U}(\pi)}\sqrt{\pi},

(303)

and

B^{{\mathfrak{c}}}_{0,\mathfrak{s}}=\delta_{\mathfrak{c},-}\int\mathrm{d}^{2}\Omega\,\eta^{\dagger}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\theta,\phi)\begin{pmatrix}e^{i\varphi^{\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle U}(\theta)}\\
0\end{pmatrix}\delta(1+\cos\theta)=-\delta_{\mathfrak{c},-}\delta_{\mathfrak{s},-}e^{i\varphi^{\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle U}(\pi)}\sqrt{\pi},

(304)

with
{\eta}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\theta,\phi)
given by (
25
) and where we use the fact that
{\eta}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\theta,\phi),{\eta}^{(1)}_{j,j_{z},\mathfrak{s}}(\theta,\phi),{\eta}^{(2)}_{j,j_{z},\mathfrak{s}}(\theta,\phi)
are orthonormal. Using (
303
), (
304
) in equation (
302
) shows that

\left({U}_{\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{c},k\hat{z}}(x)\right)_{\text{inc.}}\underset{r\rightarrow\infty}{\sim}{{\cal N}}\,\frac{i\sqrt{\pi}}{k}e^{i\varphi^{\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle U}(\pi)}\left(\delta_{\mathfrak{c},+}\delta_{\mathfrak{s},+}\,u^{{\rm in}}_{+,k}(x)-\delta_{\mathfrak{c},-}\delta_{\mathfrak{s},-}\,u^{{\rm in}}_{-,k}(x)\right)_{\text{inc.}}+(F(x))_{\text{inc.}},

(305)

and so also that

{U}_{\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{c},k\hat{z}}(x)\underset{r\rightarrow\infty}{\sim}{{\cal N}}\,\frac{i\sqrt{\pi}}{k}e^{i\varphi^{\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle U}(\pi)}\left(\delta_{\mathfrak{c},+}\delta_{\mathfrak{s},+}\,u^{{\rm in}}_{+,k}(x)-\delta_{\mathfrak{c},-}\delta_{\mathfrak{s},-}\,u^{{\rm in}}_{-,k}(x)\right)+F(x),

(306)

where
F(x)
has no projection onto the
S
-wave. Note that the
S
-wave projection vanishes unless
\mathfrak{c}\mathfrak{s}=+1
, as expected since
S
-wave states satisfy
\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{c}\mathfrak{s}
and we match
\mathfrak{h}=+1
spherical waves on each side of the above equation. The coefficients
\mathcal{D}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{c},k\hat{z}}_{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime},k^{\prime}}
are then:

\mathcal{D}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{c},k\hat{z}}_{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime},k^{\prime}}=\frac{i{{\cal N}}}{\sqrt{4\pi}k}e^{i\varphi^{\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle U}(\pi)}\delta(k-k^{\prime})\delta_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}\left(\delta_{\mathfrak{c},+}\delta_{\mathfrak{s},+}-\delta_{\mathfrak{c},-}\delta_{\mathfrak{s},-}\right).

(307)

S
-wave scattering cross sections

We can now calculate the probability for a particle in a plane-wave state to be found in the
S
-wave, which we denote
p_{s}
in the main text. Since
the probability of being in a specific momentum eigenstate tends to zero in the continuum limit, we compute
p_{s}
for a state described by a phase-space distribution function
\rho(\vec{k})
which is normalized so that
{\rm d}{\rm n}=\rho(\vec{k})\,{\cal V}\,{\rm d}^{3}k/(2\pi)^{3}
is the number of momentum states in a volume
{\rm d}^{3}k
around momentum
\vec{k}=k\,\hat{\bm{z}}
. For such an initial state (with electric charge
{\mathfrak{s}}
and
4
D chirality
{\mathfrak{c}}
),
p_{s}
is given by

\displaystyle p_{s}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\sum_{{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}=\pm}\int^{\infty}_{0}\left|\bra{0_{{\rm in}}}a^{{\rm in}}_{{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime},k^{\prime}}(a_{{\mathfrak{s}},{\mathfrak{c}},k\hat{z}})^{\star}\ket{0}\right|^{2}\,{\rm d}k^{\prime}\right]\,\rho(\vec{k})\,{\rm d}^{3}k

(308)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\sum_{{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}=\pm}\int^{\infty}_{0}\left|\bra{0_{{\rm in}}}a^{{\rm in}}_{{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime},k^{\prime}}(a^{{\rm in}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k})^{\star}\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}\right|^{2}\,{\rm d}k^{\prime}\right]\,\delta_{{\mathfrak{s}},{\mathfrak{c}}}\,{\cal N}^{2}/(4\pi k^{2})\,\rho(\vec{k})\,{\rm d}^{3}k,

which evaluates to

p_{s}=\delta_{{\mathfrak{s}},{\mathfrak{c}}}\,\frac{{\cal N}^{2}}{4\pi k^{2}}\frac{L}{\pi}\rho(\vec{k})\,{\rm d}^{3}k.

(309)

In the above, the factor of
{L}/{\pi}
is introduced to regulate the momentum-conserving delta function and is cancelled in the final cross section result by a similar factor in the
2
D scattering rate. The differential
S
-wave scattering rates
{\rm d}\Gamma_{2}
and
{\rm d}\Gamma_{\rm inclusive}
can be calculated from the perturbative amplitudes listed in §
5
and single-particle scattering amplitudes given in §
6
respectively, both of which can be written as
{\cal A}={\cal M}\,\delta(E_{f}-E_{i})
. Using these amplitudes in Fermi’s golden rule gives

{\rm d}\Gamma=2\pi|i{\cal M}/(2L)|^{2}\delta(E_{f}-E_{i})\frac{{\rm d}k^{\prime}\,L}{\pi}=|{\cal M}|^{2}\delta(E_{f}-E_{i})\,{\rm d}k^{\prime}/(2L),

(310)

for both the interaction picture and inclusive Heisenberg picture differential rate
{\rm d}\Gamma_{2}
and
{\rm d}\Gamma_{\rm inclusive}
, respectively.
The factor of
1/(2L)
in the above differential rate again comes about because we cannot choose the initial
S
-wave state to have a specific momentum when working in the continuum limit. In principle we could remove this factor in the same way as above, by redefining our initial state in terms of a distribution function in momentum space, however we do not do so here as the definition of the initial
S
-wave state is purely an intermediate step in the cross section calculation. Exclusive
2
D rates,
\Gamma_{\rm exclusive}
, can be calculated similarly with
e.g.
differential rates for processes in which no pairs are produced given by (
310
) where
{\cal A}
is an exclusive amplitude such as (
159
) - (
162
).

The
4
D differential rates for the scattering processes described here
are then independent of the system length
2L
, as advertised, and become

{\rm d}\Gamma_{\scriptscriptstyle 4D}=\frac{{\cal N}^{2}}{8\pi^{2}k^{2}}\,\delta_{{\mathfrak{s}},{\mathfrak{c}}}\,|{\cal M}|^{2}\delta(E_{f}-E_{i})\,{\rm d}k^{\prime}\,\rho(\vec{k})\,{\rm d}^{3}k.

(311)

The differential cross section is obtained from
{\rm d}\Gamma_{\scriptscriptstyle 4D}
by factoring out the flux of initial particles, given by

{\mathfrak{F}}_{i}={\rm d}n\,v_{\rm rel}={\rm d}n
, where
v_{\rm rel}=1
is the relative velocity between the dyon and incident fermions and
{\rm d}n={\cal N}^{2}[\rho(\vec{k})/(2\pi)^{3}]{\rm d}^{3}k
is the number density of incident fermions. We define
{\rm d}n
as the product of
{\rm d}\rm n/{\cal V}
, the density of states, and
i{\overline{U}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},{\mathfrak{c}},k\hat{z}}(x)\gamma^{0}U_{{\mathfrak{s}},{\mathfrak{c}},k\hat{z}}(x)={\cal N}^{2}
, the contribution of a plane wave state to the fermion number density.
Finally, we get that the
4
D single-particle
S
-wave differential cross sections
{\rm d}\sigma_{s}
simplify to

{\rm d}\sigma_{s}[f_{{\mathfrak{s}},{\mathfrak{c}}}(k)\to f_{{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}(k^{\prime})]=\frac{{\rm d}\Gamma_{\scriptscriptstyle 4D}}{{\mathfrak{F}}_{i}}=\frac{p_{s}}{{\mathfrak{F}}_{i}}{\rm d}\Gamma=\frac{\pi}{k^{2}}\delta_{{\mathfrak{s}},{\mathfrak{c}}}|{\cal M}|^{2}\delta(E_{f}-E_{i})\,{\rm d}k^{\prime},

(312)

as we claim in the main text.

Appendix F
The
in
and
out
vacuum

The Bogoliubov relations imply that the
in
and
out
vacua, defined as

\bm{a}^{{\rm in}}_{\mathfrak{s},k}\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}={\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm in}}_{\mathfrak{s},k}\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}=0,\quad\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{\mathfrak{s},k}\ket{0_{{\rm out}}}={\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm out}}_{\mathfrak{s},k}\ket{0_{{\rm out}}}=0,

(313)

do not necessarily coincide due to the possibility of pair production. This appendix shows how to expand the
in
vacuum in terms
out
states and vice versa. We also show how these expansions can be used to evaluate
inclusive
scattering observables, such as the cross sections given in §
6
.

Expansion of
in
vacuum in terms of
out
states

We first note that all
out
particles and antiparticles with momentum
k>ev
annihilate the
in
vacuum, that is

\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}={\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm out}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}=0,\quad\text{for}\quad k>ev.

(314)

For smaller momenta, the above equation remains satisfied only for negatively charged particles and antiparticles

\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{-,k}\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}={\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm out}}_{+,k}\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}=0,\quad\text{for}\quad 0<k<ev,

(315)

meaning the
in
vacuum only contains positively charged particles and antiparticles with momenta in the range
0<k<ev
.

Since the total vacuum can be written as a tensor product over the vacua for each momentum,
\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}=\underset{k>0}{\prod}\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm in}}}
, the expansion of the total vacuum in terms of
out
states can be done on a mode-by-mode basis.
As explained above, for momenta
k>ev
the single-momentum vacuum must be equal to the corresponding
out
vacuum up to a phase

\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm in}}}=e^{i{\delta}_{k}}\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm out}}},\quad\text{for}\quad k>ev

(316)

where
\delta_{k}
is an arbitrary phase. For momenta
k<ev
, it is convenient to define the single-mode vacuum
\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm in}}}
in the following way

\bm{a}^{{\rm in}}_{+,k}\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm in}}}={\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm in}}_{+,k}\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm in}}}=0\quad\text{and}\quad\bm{a}^{{\rm in}}_{-,-k+ev}\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm in}}}={\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm in}}_{-,-k+ev}\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm in}}}=0,

(317)

as opposed to
\bm{a}^{{\rm in}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm in}}}={\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm in}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm in}}}=0
, since the Bogoliubov relations (which impose energy conservation) relate
{\mathfrak{s}}=+
particle operators of momentum
k
to
{\mathfrak{s}}=-
antiparticle operators of momentum
-k+ev
. With this definition, the most general form of the single-mode vacuum is:

\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm in}}}=\bigl[B_{0;0}+B_{1;0}(\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,k})^{\star}+B_{0;1}({\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-k+ev})^{\star}+B_{1;1}(\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,k})^{\star}({\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-k+ev})^{\star}\Bigr]\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm out}}}.

(318)

The action of any
out
operator on the single-mode vacuum
\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm in}}}
can be calculated in more than one way: Directly, by using the above expansion in terms of
out
states, or by rewriting the operator using the Bogoliubov relations and then acting on the state. This can be used to determine the
B_{i;j}
coefficients in (
318
), as we now show. Consider the state
\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,k}\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm in}}}
, which is equal to:

\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,k}\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm in}}}=\bigl[B_{1;0}+B_{1;1}({\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-k+ev})^{\star}\Bigr]\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm out}}},

(319)

but can also be rewritten as

\displaystyle\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,k}\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm in}}}={\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}e^{i{\mathfrak{a}}}\,({\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm in}}_{-,-k+ev})^{\star}\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm in}}}

(320)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}e^{i{\mathfrak{a}}}\,\left({\cal T}^{--}_{{\rm out}}({\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-k+ev})^{\star}+{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm out}}e^{-i{\mathfrak{a}}}\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,k}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\times\bigl[B_{0;0}+B_{1;0}(\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,k})^{\star}+B_{0;1}({\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-k+ev})^{\star}+B_{1;1}(\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,k})^{\star}({\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-k+ev})^{\star}\Bigr]\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm out}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}{\cal T}^{--}_{{\rm out}}e^{i{\mathfrak{a}}}\Bigl[B_{0;0}({\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-k+ev})^{\star}-B_{1;0}(\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,k})^{\star}({\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-k+ev})^{\star}\Bigr]\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm out}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt+{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm out}}\Bigl[B_{1;0}+B_{1;1}({\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-k+ev})^{\star}\Bigr]\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm out}}}.

In the above, we use the Bogoliubov relations first to rewrite
\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,k}
and then again to rewrite
({\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm in}}_{-,-k+ev})^{\star}
. Comparing (
319
) and (
320
) shows that, when
{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}{\cal T}^{--}_{{\rm out}}\neq 0
36
36

36

This condition also implies that
B_{1;1}\neq 0
, otherwise
B_{1;0}=0
would imply
\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,k}\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm in}}}=0
which is not consistent since
\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,k}\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm in}}}={\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}e^{i{\mathfrak{a}}}\,({\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm in}}_{-,-k+ev})^{\star}\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm in}}}\neq 0
.

B_{1;0}=0\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{B_{0;0}}{B_{1;1}}=\frac{1-{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm out}}}{{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}{\cal T}^{--}_{{\rm out}}}e^{-i{\mathfrak{a}}}=\frac{{\cal T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}}{{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}}e^{-i{\mathfrak{a}}},

(321)

where in the last line we rewrite the
{\cal T}_{{\rm out}}
amplitudes in terms of
{\cal T}_{{\rm in}}
amplitudes and use (
73
). Similarly, the state
{\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-k+ev}\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm in}}}
is equal to:

{\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-k+ev}\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm in}}}=\bigl[B_{0;1}-B_{1;1}({\bm{a}}^{{\rm out}}_{+,k})^{\star}\Bigr]\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm out}}},

(322)

but can also be rewritten as

\displaystyle{\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-k+ev}\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm in}}}={\cal T}^{-+\,*}_{{\rm in}}e^{i{\mathfrak{a}}}\,({\bm{a}}^{{\rm in}}_{+,k})^{\star}\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm in}}}

(323)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\cal T}^{-+\,*}_{{\rm in}}e^{i{\mathfrak{a}}}\,\left({\cal T}^{++\,*}_{{\rm out}}({\bm{a}}^{{\rm out}}_{+,k})^{\star}+{\cal T}^{+-\,*}_{{\rm out}}e^{-i{\mathfrak{a}}}\,{\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-k+ev}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\times\bigl[B_{0;0}+B_{0;1}({\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-k+ev})^{\star}+B_{1;1}(\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,k})^{\star}({\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-k+ev})^{\star}\Bigr]\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm out}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\cal T}^{-+\,*}_{{\rm in}}{\cal T}^{++\,*}_{{\rm out}}e^{i{\mathfrak{a}}}\bigl[B_{0;0}({\bm{a}}^{{\rm out}}_{+,k})^{\star}+B_{0;1}({\bm{a}}^{{\rm out}}_{+,k})^{\star}({\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-k+ev})^{\star}\Bigr]\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm out}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt+{\cal T}^{-+\,*}_{{\rm in}}{\cal T}^{+-\,*}_{{\rm out}}\,\bigl[B_{0;1}-B_{1;1}(\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,k})^{\star}\Bigr]\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm out}}},

which implies that

B_{0;1}=0,

(324)

when compared to (
322
). The remaining coefficients
B_{0;0}
and
B_{1;1}
can be determined up to a phase, by imposing the normalization condition
\braket{0^{k}_{{\rm in}}|0^{k}_{{\rm in}}}=1
and using (
321
). For
{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}},{\cal T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}\neq 0
, we then get:

\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm in}}}=e^{i\delta_{k}}\Bigl[{\cal T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}+{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}e^{i{\mathfrak{a}}}(\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,k})^{\star}({\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-k+ev})^{\star}\Bigr]\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm out}}},

(325)

for momenta
k<ev
, where
\delta_{k}
is an arbitrary phase. When
{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}=0
or
{\cal T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}=0
, we define the expansion of
\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm in}}}
in terms of
out
states as the
{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}\to 0
and
{\cal T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}\to 0
limit of the above equation respectively, where the surviving amplitude is necessarily a phase.

For general momentum
k
and choice of
{\cal T}_{{\rm in}}
amplitudes, the single-mode vacuum is:

\displaystyle\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm in}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{i\delta_{k}}\left\{\Theta(k-ev)+\Theta(-k+ev)\Bigl[{\cal T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}+{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}e^{i{\mathfrak{a}}}(\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,k})^{\star}({\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-k+ev})^{\star}\Bigr]\right\}\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm out}}}.

(326)

Finally, we can write the full
in
vacuum in the
out
basis as

\displaystyle\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{k>0}{\prod}\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm in}}}=\left(\underset{k>ev}{\prod}\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm in}}}\right)\left(\overset{k<{ev}}{\underset{k>0}{\prod}}\ket{0^{k}_{{\rm in}}}\right)

(327)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{i\delta}\left(\overset{k<{ev}}{\underset{k>0}{\prod}}\left[{\cal T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}+{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}e^{i{\mathfrak{a}}}(\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,k})^{\star}({\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-k+ev})^{\star}\right]\right)\ket{0_{{\rm out}}},

where
\delta\coloneqq\underset{k>0}{\sum}\delta_{k}
is an arbitrary phase which we set to
0
in the main text, as it is not observable
37
37

37

The expansion of
in
states in terms of the
out
basis is only relevant when evaluating transition amplitudes. Since all amplitudes include the
e^{i\delta}
factor, no interference experiment can be constructed to measure
\delta
.
. The product over all
k<ev
modes evaluates to

\displaystyle\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{i\delta}\,\overset{\frac{ev}{\Delta k}}{\underset{n=0}{\sum}}\,\frac{1}{n!}(\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm in}})^{\frac{ev}{\Delta k}-n}({\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}})^{n}\,e^{in{\mathfrak{a}}}

(328)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\Bigl[\sum^{ev}_{k_{1}=0}...\sum^{ev}_{k_{n}=0}\,(\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,{k_{1}}})^{\star}({{\overline{\bm{a}}}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,{-k_{1}+ev}})^{\star}...(\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,{k_{n}}})^{\star}({{\overline{\bm{a}}}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,{-k_{n}+ev}})^{\star}\Bigr]\ket{0_{{\rm out}}}.

The full
in
vacuum is normalized, since the coefficients
B_{i;j}
were chosen such that the
expansion of each single-mode
in
vacuum corresponds to a normalized state.

Expansion of
out
vacuum in terms of
in
states

The above procedure can be repeated for the
out
vacuum. We similarly get:

\displaystyle\ket{0_{{\rm out}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-i\delta}\,\overset{\frac{ev}{\Delta k}}{\underset{n=0}{\sum}}\,\frac{1}{n!}(\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm out}})^{\frac{ev}{\Delta k}-n}({\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm out}})^{n}\,e^{in{\mathfrak{a}}}

(329)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\Bigl[\sum^{ev}_{k_{1}=0}...\sum^{ev}_{k_{n}=0}\,(\bm{a}^{{\rm in}}_{+,{k_{1}}})^{\star}({{\overline{\bm{a}}}}^{{\rm in}}_{-,{-k_{1}+ev}})^{\star}...(\bm{a}^{{\rm in}}_{+,{k_{n}}})^{\star}({{\overline{\bm{a}}}}^{{\rm in}}_{-,{-k_{n}+ev}})^{\star}\Bigr]\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-i\delta}\,\overset{\frac{ev}{\Delta k}}{\underset{n=0}{\sum}}\,\frac{1}{n!}(\mathcal{T}^{--\,*}_{{\rm in}})^{\frac{ev}{\Delta k}-n}({\cal T}^{-+\,*}_{{\rm in}})^{n}\,e^{in{\mathfrak{a}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\Bigl[\sum^{ev}_{k_{1}=0}...\sum^{ev}_{k_{n}=0}\,(\bm{a}^{{\rm in}}_{+,{k_{1}}})^{\star}({{\overline{\bm{a}}}}^{{\rm in}}_{-,{-k_{1}+ev}})^{\star}...(\bm{a}^{{\rm in}}_{+,{k_{n}}})^{\star}({{\overline{\bm{a}}}}^{{\rm in}}_{-,{-k_{n}+ev}})^{\star}\Bigr]\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}.

Inclusive scattering cross sections in the Born-Oppenheimer approximation

In the main text, we remark that the scattering observables of physical interest are often
inclusive
observables, for which the number of pairs produced by the dyon is unmeasured. We now show how such observables can be calculated, focusing on the inclusive
S
-wave scattering cross sections of §
6
. For convenience, we work in discrete normalization as in the rest of this appendix.

To start, we note that in most cases of interest in the main text,
exclusive
amplitudes
\bm{{\cal A}}_{n}
can be written as

\bm{{\cal A}}_{n}=\bm{{\cal A}}_{sc}\,\bm{{\cal A}}^{n}_{\rm pair},

(330)

that is they factorize into a single-particle scattering amplitude
\bm{{\cal A}}_{sc}
and an amplitude to produce
n
pairs,
\bm{{\cal A}}^{n}_{\rm pair}
. As a result, the exclusive probability for the process to happen,
P_{n}
, will be the product
of the probability for the single-particle scattering event,
p_{sc}
, and the probability to produce
n
pairs,
P^{n}_{\rm pair}
,

P_{n}=p_{sc}\,P^{n}_{\rm pair}.

(331)

The inclusive probability for the single-particle scattering process is given by

p=p_{sc}\,p_{\rm vac}=p_{sc}\,\sum^{N}_{n=0}P^{n}_{\rm pair},

(332)

where
N\coloneqq\frac{ev}{\Delta k}
is the maximum number of
out
pairs in the
in
vacuum, while
p_{\rm vac}
is defined through the above equation and
is equal to

\displaystyle p_{\rm vac}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\braket{0_{{\rm out}}|0_{{\rm in}}}|^{2}+\sum^{ev}_{q_{1}=0}|\braket{0_{{\rm out}}|{\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-q_{1}+ev}\,\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,q_{1}}|0_{{\rm in}}}|^{2}+\cdots

(333)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{n!}\sum^{ev}_{q_{1},\cdots q_{n}=0}|\braket{0_{{\rm out}}|{\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-q_{n}+ev}\,\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,q_{n}}\,\cdots{\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-q_{1}+ev}\,\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,q_{1}}|0_{{\rm in}}}|^{2}+\cdots

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{N!}\sum^{ev}_{q_{1},\cdots q_{N}=0}|\braket{0_{{\rm out}}|{\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-q_{N}+ev}\,\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,q_{N}}\,\cdots{\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-q_{1}+ev}\,\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,q_{1}}|0_{{\rm in}}}|^{2}.

In the above, the combinatorial prefactors are added to ensure we only sum over distinct final states. Keeping in mind that
out
states with doubly-occupied pairs do not contribute to the momentum sums in (
333
),
p_{\rm vac}
simplifies to

\displaystyle p_{\rm vac}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(|{\cal T}_{{\rm in}}^{--}|^{2}\right)^{N}+N\left(|{\cal T}_{{\rm in}}^{--}|^{2}\right)^{N-1}|{\cal T}_{{\rm in}}^{+-}|^{2}+\cdots

(334)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{N!}{n!(N-n)!}\left(|{\cal T}_{{\rm in}}^{--}|^{2}\right)^{N-n}\left(|{\cal T}_{{\rm in}}^{+-}|^{2}\right)^{n}+\cdots+\left(|{\cal T}_{{\rm in}}^{+-}|^{2}\right)^{N}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(|{\cal T}_{{\rm in}}^{--}|^{2}+|{\cal T}_{{\rm in}}^{+-}|^{2}\right)^{N}=1.

This shows that the inclusive probability for the scattering process described by
\bm{{\cal A}}_{sc}
is

p=p_{sc},

(335)

and so the corresponding inclusive rates and cross sections can be computed in the usual way, using only the single-particle amplitude
\bm{{\cal A}}_{sc}
or its continuum-normalization counterpart
{\cal A}_{sc}
.

The previous argument can be used to justify the inclusive cross section formulas given in §
6
, for all but one of the processes considered in that section. The exception is the process described by the amplitude
(
162
), when the momentum of the initial particle,
k
, belongs in the pair production range
k<ev
. In this case, the exclusive amplitude factorizes as follows

\displaystyle\bm{{\cal A}}_{n}=\bra{0_{{\rm out}}}\overline{\bm{a}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-q_{n}+ev}{\bm{a}}^{{\rm out}}_{+,q_{n}}...\overline{\bm{a}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-q_{1}+ev}{\bm{a}}^{{\rm out}}_{+,q_{1}}\,\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,k^{\prime}}(\bm{a}^{{\rm in}}_{+,k})^{\star}\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}

\displaystyle\qquad=\delta_{kk^{\prime}}\Bigl[\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm in}}\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}-{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm in}}{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}\Bigr]\left({\cal T}_{{\rm in}}^{--}\right)^{N-(n+1)}\left({\cal T}_{{\rm in}}^{+-}\right)^{n}e^{in{\mathfrak{a}}}

\displaystyle\qquad=\delta_{kk^{\prime}}\Bigl[\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm in}}\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}-{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm in}}{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}\Bigr]\left[\bm{{\cal A}}^{n}_{\rm pair}/{\cal T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}\right],

(336)

when
n<N
and
k^{\prime},q_{1},\cdots,q_{n}
are distinct momenta, and vanishes otherwise.
The inclusive probability for the relevant single-particle process is given by

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k^{\prime}}\Bigg[|\braket{0_{{\rm out}}|0_{{\rm in}}}|^{2}+\sum^{ev}_{q_{1}=0}|\braket{0_{{\rm out}}|{\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-q_{1}+ev}\,\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,q_{1}}|0_{{\rm in}}}|^{2}+\cdots

(337)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{(N-1)!}\sum^{ev}_{q_{1},\cdots q_{{\scriptscriptstyle N}-1}=0}|\braket{0_{{\rm out}}|{\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-q_{N-1}+ev}\,\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,q_{N-1}}\,\cdots{\overline{\bm{a}}}^{{\rm out}}_{-,-q_{1}+ev}\,\bm{a}^{{\rm out}}_{+,q_{1}}|0_{{\rm in}}}|^{2}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times|{\cal T}_{{\rm in}}^{--}|^{-2}\left|\delta_{kk^{\prime}}\Bigl[\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm in}}\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}-{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm in}}{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}\Bigr]\right|^{2}.

To evaluate the above sums, note that the Bogoliubov relations impose that the momentum
k^{\prime}
is equal to the momentum of the initial particle,
k
, and so lies in the pair production range
0<k^{\prime}<ev
.
As one of the particle states in the pair production range is already occupied, there are now at most
N-1
different momenta that each
q_{i}
can be equated to. The inclusive probability,
p
, is then equal to

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k^{\prime}}\Bigg[\left(|{\cal T}_{{\rm in}}^{--}|^{2}\right)^{N-1}+(N-1)\left(|{\cal T}_{{\rm in}}^{--}|^{2}\right)^{N-2}|{\cal T}_{{\rm in}}^{+-}|^{2}+\cdots+\left(|{\cal T}_{{\rm in}}^{+-}|^{2}\right)^{N-1}\Bigg]

(338)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\left|\delta_{kk^{\prime}}\Bigl[\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm in}}\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}-{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm in}}{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}\Bigr]\right|^{2},

which simplifies to

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k^{\prime}}\left(|{\cal T}_{{\rm in}}^{--}|^{2}+|{\cal T}_{{\rm in}}^{+-}|^{2}\right)^{N-1}\left|\delta_{kk^{\prime}}\Bigl[\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm in}}\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}-{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm in}}{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}\Bigr]\right|^{2}

(339)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k^{\prime}}\left|\delta_{kk^{\prime}}\Bigl[\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm in}}\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}-{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm in}}{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}\Bigr]\right|^{2}.

The last line of (
339
) shows that the inclusive cross section for this process can be calculated in the usual way, if the single-particle amplitude

\bm{{\cal A}}_{sc}=\delta_{kk^{\prime}}\Bigl[\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm in}}\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}-{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm in}}{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}\Bigr],

(340)

or its continuum-normalization counterpart (
169
) (which is valid for all
k
), is used instead of exclusive amplitudes.

Appendix G
Bilinear currents

In this appendix, we show how one can regularize local fermion bilinear operators and compute the
in
and
out
vacuum expectation values of the fermion number, electric charge and axial currents, as well as the interaction picture interacting hamiltonian. We further derive the conservation (or non-conservation) equations satisfied by the fermionic currents and show how the boundary condition can be used to directly evaluate their radial components at
r=\epsilon
.

Vacuum expectation values of fermion bilinears

Writing

{\bm{\chi}}(x)=\sum_{{\mathfrak{s}}=\pm}\int^{\infty}_{0}\frac{{\rm d}k}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\;\Bigl[{\mathfrak{u}}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}(x)\;{a}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}+{\mathfrak{v}}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}(x)\;({\overline{a}}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k})^{\star}\Bigr]

(341)

where
\text{d}=\{{\rm in},{\rm out}\}
, we seek the expectation values of fermion bilinears like

\displaystyle{\overline{\bm{\chi}}}M{\bm{\chi}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{{\mathfrak{s}},{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}=\pm}\int^{\infty}_{0}\frac{{\rm d}k\,{\rm d}k^{\prime}}{2\pi}\;\Bigl[{\overline{{\mathfrak{u}}}}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}M\,{\mathfrak{u}}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime},k^{\prime}}\left(({a}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k})^{\star}\,{a}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime},k^{\prime}}\right)+{\overline{{\mathfrak{v}}}}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}M\,{\mathfrak{v}}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime},k^{\prime}}\left({\overline{a}}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}\,({\overline{a}}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime},k^{\prime}})^{\star}\right)

(342)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+{\overline{{\mathfrak{u}}}}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}M\,{\mathfrak{v}}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime},k^{\prime}}\left(({a}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k})^{\star}\,({\overline{a}}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime},k^{\prime}})^{\star}\right)+{\overline{{\mathfrak{v}}}}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}M\,{\mathfrak{u}}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime},k^{\prime}}\left({\overline{a}}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}\,{a}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime},k^{\prime}}\right)\Bigr]

where we use the operator-ordering notation for fermions:
(AB):=\frac{1}{2}[A,B]
that ensures classically hermitian expressions remain hermitian.

The
in
and
out
vacuum expectation values of local bilinear operators are formally given by

\displaystyle\bra{0_{\text{d}}}{\overline{\bm{\chi}}}M{\bm{\chi}}\ket{0_{\text{d}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{{\mathfrak{s}},{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}=\pm}\int^{\infty}_{0}\frac{{\rm d}k\,{\rm d}k^{\prime}}{4\pi}\;\Bigl[-{\overline{{\mathfrak{u}}}}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}M\,{\mathfrak{u}}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime},k^{\prime}}\bra{0_{\text{d}}}{a}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime},k^{\prime}}({a}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k})^{\star}\ket{0_{\text{d}}}+{\overline{{\mathfrak{v}}}}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}M\,{\mathfrak{v}}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime},k^{\prime}}\bra{0_{\text{d}}}{\overline{a}}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}({\overline{a}}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime},k^{\prime}})^{\star}\ket{0_{\text{d}}}\Bigr]

(343)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{{\mathfrak{s}}=\pm}\int^{\infty}_{0}\frac{{\rm d}k}{4\pi}\;\Bigl[-{\overline{{\mathfrak{u}}}}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}M\,{\mathfrak{u}}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}+{\overline{{\mathfrak{v}}}}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}M\,{\mathfrak{v}}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}\Bigr].

At face value, the above expectation values vanish for any matrix
M
acting in spin and isospin space, since
{\mathfrak{u}}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}(x)={\mathfrak{v}}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},-k}(x)
and so

{\overline{{\mathfrak{u}}}}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}(x)M\,{\mathfrak{u}}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}(x)={\overline{{\mathfrak{v}}}}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}(x)M\,{\mathfrak{v}}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}(x)
for both
in
and
out
modes
38
38

38

This is true for
M
such that
{\overline{{\mathfrak{u}}}}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}M\,{\mathfrak{u}}^{\text{d}}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}
is independent of
k
.
. This turns out not to be the case, since the presence of anomalies means more care must be taken when evaluating local operators. To this end, we regularize the fermion bilinear operator
{\overline{\bm{\chi}}}(x)M\,{\bm{\chi}}(x)
by evaluating
\bm{\chi},{\overline{\bm{\chi}}}
at slightly different points, namely
{\overline{\bm{\chi}}}(r+\varepsilon/2,t)M\,{\bm{\chi}}(r-\varepsilon/2,t)
39
39

39

At
r=\epsilon
, we instead consider the operators
{\overline{\bm{\chi}}}(\epsilon+\varepsilon,t)M\,{\bm{\chi}}(\epsilon,t)
since
r\geq\epsilon
.
, and take the limit
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
after calculating any matrix elements of interest. Note that the point-split operator
{\overline{\bm{\chi}}}(r+\varepsilon/2,t)M\,{\bm{\chi}}(r-\varepsilon/2,t)
is gauge invariant without the addition of a Wilson line, since we point split only in the radial direction along which the gauge field vanishes,
{\cal A}_{r}(x)=0
. More explicitly, we calculate vacuum expectation values through

\displaystyle\bra{0_{\text{d}}}{\overline{\bm{\chi}}}\,M{\bm{\chi}}\ket{0_{\text{d}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}{\lim}\Bigl[\bra{0_{\text{d}}}{\overline{\chi}}_{+}(x_{+})\,M\,{\chi}_{+}(x_{-})\ket{0_{\text{d}}}+\bra{0_{\text{d}}}{\overline{\chi}}_{-}(x_{+})\,M\,{\chi}_{-}(x_{-})\ket{0_{\text{d}}}\Bigr],

(344)

where
x_{\pm}\coloneqq(r_{\pm},t)=(r\pm\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon,t)
. Applying this to the radial component of the axial current (for which
M=i\Gamma^{1}\Gamma_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
) gives the following contributions to the
in
vacuum expectation value:

\displaystyle\bra{0_{{\rm in}}}{\overline{\chi}}_{+}(x_{+})\,i\Gamma^{1}\Gamma_{\scriptscriptstyle A}\,{\chi}_{+}(x_{-})\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}=\int^{\infty}_{0}\frac{{\rm d}k}{4\pi}\;\Bigl[-{\overline{{\mathfrak{u}}}}^{\rm in}_{+,k}(x_{+})i\Gamma^{1}\Gamma_{\scriptscriptstyle A}\,{\mathfrak{u}}^{\rm in}_{+,k}(x_{-})+{\overline{{\mathfrak{v}}}}^{\rm in}_{+,k}(x_{+})i\Gamma^{1}\Gamma_{\scriptscriptstyle A}\,{\mathfrak{v}}^{\rm in}_{+,k}(x_{-})\Bigr]

\displaystyle\quad=\left[\left(-1-e^{iev\varepsilon}|{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm in}}|^{2}\right)\left(\frac{r_{+}}{r_{-}}\right)^{-\frac{ieQ}{2}}+|\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm in}}|^{2}\left(\frac{r_{+}}{r_{-}}\right)^{\frac{ieQ}{2}}\right]\int^{\infty}_{0}\frac{{\rm d}k}{4\pi}\;(e^{-ik\varepsilon}-e^{ik\varepsilon}),

(345)

as well as

\displaystyle\bra{0_{{\rm in}}}{\overline{\chi}}_{-}(x_{+})\,i\Gamma^{1}\Gamma_{\scriptscriptstyle A}\,{\chi}_{-}(x_{-})\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}=\int^{\infty}_{0}\frac{{\rm d}k}{4\pi}\;\Bigl[-{\overline{{\mathfrak{u}}}}^{\rm in}_{-,k}(x_{+})i\Gamma^{1}\Gamma_{\scriptscriptstyle A}\,{\mathfrak{u}}^{\rm in}_{-,k}(x_{-})+{\overline{{\mathfrak{v}}}}^{\rm in}_{-,k}(x_{+})i\Gamma^{1}\Gamma_{\scriptscriptstyle A}\,{\mathfrak{v}}^{\rm in}_{-,k}(x_{-})\Bigr]

\displaystyle\quad=\left[\left(1+e^{-iev\varepsilon}|{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}|^{2}\right)\left(\frac{r_{+}}{r_{-}}\right)^{\frac{ieQ}{2}}-|\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}|^{2}\left(\frac{r_{+}}{r_{-}}\right)^{-\frac{ieQ}{2}}\right]\int^{\infty}_{0}\frac{{\rm d}k}{4\pi}\;(e^{-ik\varepsilon}-e^{ik\varepsilon}).

(346)

The momentum integrals in the above equations can be evaluated using the integral representation of the Heaviside theta function

\int^{\infty}_{0}\frac{{\rm d}k}{4\pi}e^{\pm ik\varepsilon}=\underset{\beta\rightarrow 0+}{\lim}\,\frac{i}{2\pi}\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\frac{{\rm d}k\,{\rm d}\tau}{4\pi(\tau+i\beta)}e^{-ik(\tau\mp\varepsilon)}=\underset{\beta\rightarrow 0+}{\lim}\,\frac{i}{4\pi(\pm\varepsilon+i\beta)},

(347)

an using (
347
) in (
345
) and (
346
) shows that the
in
vacuum expectation value of the radial component of the axial current can be written as

\displaystyle\bra{0_{{\rm in}}}{\overline{\bm{\chi}}}\,i\Gamma^{1}\Gamma_{\scriptscriptstyle A}{\bm{\chi}}\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}{\lim}\,\left(-\frac{i}{2\pi\varepsilon}\right)\biggl[\left(1+e^{-iev\varepsilon}|{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}|^{2}+|\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm in}}|^{2}\right)\left(\frac{r_{+}}{r_{-}}\right)^{\frac{ieQ}{2}}

(348)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\left(1+e^{iev\varepsilon}|{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm in}}|^{2}+|\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}|^{2}\right)\left(\frac{r_{+}}{r_{-}}\right)^{-\frac{ieQ}{2}}\biggr].

Finally, we simplify the above equation by expanding in
\varepsilon
and using (
73
) to get:

\bra{0_{{\rm in}}}j^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}(x)\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}=\bra{0_{{\rm in}}}{\overline{\bm{\chi}}}\,i\Gamma^{1}\Gamma_{\scriptscriptstyle A}{\bm{\chi}}\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}=-\frac{ev}{\pi}|\mathcal{T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}|^{2}+\frac{eQ}{\pi r}.

(349)

The
in
vacuum expectation value of other current operators of interest can similarly be written as

\displaystyle\bra{0_{{\rm in}}}{\overline{\bm{\chi}}}M\,{\bm{\chi}}\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}{\lim}\,\left(-\frac{i}{2\pi\varepsilon}\right)\biggl[\left(q^{{\scriptscriptstyle M},\,{\rm in}}_{-}-q^{{\scriptscriptstyle M},\,{\rm in}}_{+-}e^{-iev\varepsilon}|{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}|^{2}-q^{{\scriptscriptstyle M},\,{\rm in}}_{++}|\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm in}}|^{2}\right)\left(\frac{r_{+}}{r_{-}}\right)^{\frac{ieQ}{2}}

(350)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\left(q^{{\scriptscriptstyle M},\,{\rm in}}_{+}-q^{{\scriptscriptstyle M},\,{\rm in}}_{-+}e^{iev\varepsilon}|{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm in}}|^{2}-q^{{\scriptscriptstyle M},\,{\rm in}}_{--}|\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm in}}|^{2}\right)\left(\frac{r_{+}}{r_{-}}\right)^{-\frac{ieQ}{2}}\biggr],

where
q^{{\scriptscriptstyle M},\,{\rm in}}_{{\mathfrak{s}}},q^{{\scriptscriptstyle M},\,{\rm in}}_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}
are listed in table
2
for several choices of the matrix
M
. The calculation of current vacuum expectation values goes through in the same way for the
out
vacuum. We get:

\displaystyle\bra{0_{{\rm out}}}{\overline{\bm{\chi}}}M\,{\bm{\chi}}\ket{0_{{\rm out}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}{\lim}\,\frac{i}{2\pi\varepsilon}\biggl[\left(q^{{\scriptscriptstyle M},\,{\rm out}}_{-}-q^{{\scriptscriptstyle M},\,{\rm out}}_{+-}e^{iev\varepsilon}|{\cal T}^{+-}_{{\rm out}}|^{2}-q^{{\scriptscriptstyle M},\,{\rm out}}_{++}|\mathcal{T}^{++}_{{\rm out}}|^{2}\right)\left(\frac{r_{+}}{r_{-}}\right)^{-\frac{ieQ}{2}}

(351)

\displaystyle\qquad+\left(q^{{\scriptscriptstyle M},\,{\rm out}}_{+}-q^{{\scriptscriptstyle M},\,{\rm out}}_{-+}e^{-iev\varepsilon}|{\cal T}^{-+}_{{\rm out}}|^{2}-q^{{\scriptscriptstyle M},\,{\rm out}}_{--}|\mathcal{T}^{--}_{{\rm out}}|^{2}\right)\left(\frac{r_{+}}{r_{-}}\right)^{\frac{ieQ}{2}}\biggr],

where
q^{{\scriptscriptstyle M},\,{\rm out}}_{{\mathfrak{s}}},q^{{\scriptscriptstyle M},\,{\rm out}}_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}
are listed in table
3
.

We see that the regularized vacuum matrix elements of the components of the fermion number current are given by

\bra{0_{{\rm in}}}{j}^{0}_{\scriptscriptstyle B}(x)\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}=\bra{0_{{\rm out}}}{j}^{0}_{\scriptscriptstyle B}(x)\ket{0_{{\rm out}}}=0\quad\hbox{and}\quad\bra{0_{{\rm in}}}{j}^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle B}(x)\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}=\bra{0_{{\rm out}}}{j}^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle B}(x)\ket{0_{{\rm out}}}=\frac{2i}{\pi\varepsilon},

(352)

while those of the electromagnetic current are

\displaystyle\bra{0_{{\rm in}}}{j}^{0}_{{\scriptscriptstyle F}}(r,t)\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bra{0_{{\rm out}}}{j}^{0}_{{\scriptscriptstyle F}}(r,t)\ket{0_{{\rm out}}}=\frac{e^{2}v}{2\pi}|\mathcal{T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}|^{2}-\frac{e^{2}Q}{2\pi r}

\displaystyle\hbox{and}\quad\bra{0_{{\rm in}}}{j}^{1}_{{\scriptscriptstyle F}}(r,t)\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\bra{0_{{\rm out}}}{j}^{1}_{{\scriptscriptstyle F}}(r,t)\ket{0_{{\rm out}}}=\frac{e^{2}v}{2\pi}|\mathcal{T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}|^{2}\,.

(353)

Finally, the axial current vacuum matrix elements are

\displaystyle\bra{0_{{\rm in}}}{j}^{0}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}(r,t)\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\bra{0_{{\rm out}}}{j}^{0}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}(r,t)\ket{0_{{\rm out}}}=-\frac{ev}{\pi}|\mathcal{T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}|^{2}

\displaystyle\hbox{and}\quad\bra{0_{{\rm in}}}{j}^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}(r,t)\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bra{0_{{\rm out}}}{j}^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}(r,t)\ket{0_{{\rm out}}}=-\frac{ev}{\pi}|\mathcal{T}^{+-}_{{\rm in}}|^{2}+\frac{eQ}{\pi r}\,.

(354)

The vacuum expectation value of the interaction hamiltonian in the interaction picture (
111
) (with
\delta{\cal C}^{pv}=\delta{\cal C}^{ps}=0
) can be caclulated similarly. This expectation value is defined as

\displaystyle\bra{0}H_{\rm int}\ket{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{\varepsilon\to 0}{\lim}\sum_{{\mathfrak{s}}=\pm}\int^{\infty}_{0}\frac{{\rm d}k}{8\pi}\Bigl[-\overline{{\mathfrak{u}}}^{{\rm in}0}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}(\epsilon+\varepsilon,t)\Bigl(\delta\mathcal{C}^{s}_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}-i\delta\mathcal{C}^{v}_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}}\Gamma^{0}\Bigr)\,{\mathfrak{u}}^{{\rm in}0}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}(\epsilon,t)

(355)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\overline{{\mathfrak{v}}}^{{\rm in}0}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}(\epsilon+\varepsilon,t)\Bigl(\delta\mathcal{C}^{s}_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}}-i\delta\mathcal{C}^{v}_{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{s}}}\Gamma^{0}\Bigr)\,{\mathfrak{v}}^{{\rm in}0}_{{\mathfrak{s}},k}(\epsilon,t)\Bigr],

where
\ket{0}=\ket{0_{{\rm in}}}=\ket{0_{{\rm out}}}
in the interaction picture and is equal to

\displaystyle\bra{0}H_{\rm int}\ket{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{\varepsilon\to 0}{\lim}\sum_{\mathfrak{s}=\pm}(\delta\mathcal{C}^{s}_{\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{s}}-\delta\mathcal{C}^{v}_{\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{s}})\left[\left(\frac{\epsilon+\varepsilon}{\epsilon}\right)^{{i{\mathfrak{s}}eQ}/{2}}-\left(\frac{\epsilon+\varepsilon}{\epsilon}\right)^{{-i{\mathfrak{s}}eQ}/{2}}\right]\int^{\infty}_{0}\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{8\pi}\,(e^{ik\varepsilon}-e^{-ik\varepsilon})

(356)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{eQ}{4\pi\epsilon}\sum_{\mathfrak{s}=\pm}{\mathfrak{s}}\,(\delta\mathcal{C}^{s}_{\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{s}}-\delta\mathcal{C}^{v}_{\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{s}}),

which matches (
114
).

G.1
Current conservation equations

The fermionic currents satisfy conservation (or non-conservation) equations which can be derived directly from the
2
D Dirac equation. That is, the current
j^{\alpha}_{\scriptscriptstyle M}(x)\coloneqq i{\overline{\bm{\chi}}}(x)\Gamma^{\alpha}M\bm{\chi}(x)
satisfies the equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\,j^{t}_{\scriptscriptstyle M}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\,\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}{\lim}\left[\left(\partial_{t}\overline{\bm{\chi}}(r_{+},t)\right)\Gamma^{0}\,M\bm{\chi}(r_{-},t)+\overline{\bm{\chi}}(r_{+},t)\Gamma^{0}\,M\left(\partial_{t}\bm{\chi}(r_{-},t)\right)\right]

(357)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}{\lim}\Big[-\left(\partial_{r}\overline{\bm{\chi}}(r_{+},t)\right)\Gamma_{c}\,\Gamma^{0}\,M\bm{\chi}(r_{-},t)+\overline{\bm{\chi}}(r_{+},t)\Gamma^{0}\,M\,\Gamma_{c}\left(\partial_{r}\bm{\chi}(r_{-},t)\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{ie}{2}\Big(\mathcal{A}^{3}_{0}(r_{+},t)\,\overline{\bm{\chi}}(r_{+},t)\Gamma^{0}\tau_{3}\,M\bm{\chi}(r_{-},t)-\mathcal{A}^{3}_{0}(r_{-},t)\,\overline{\bm{\chi}}(r_{+},t)\Gamma^{0}\,M\tau_{3}\bm{\chi}(r_{-},t)\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}{\lim}\Big[\partial_{r}\left(\overline{\bm{\chi}}(r_{+},t)\Gamma^{0}\Gamma_{c}\,M\,\bm{\chi}(r_{-},t)\right)-\frac{ie\varepsilon}{2}(\partial_{r}\mathcal{A}^{3}_{0}(r))\overline{\bm{\chi}}(r_{+},t)\Gamma^{0}\,M\tau_{3}\bm{\chi}(r_{-},t)\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\partial_{r}{j}^{r}_{\scriptscriptstyle M}(x)+\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}{\lim}\,\frac{\varepsilon eQ}{2r^{2}}\,\overline{\bm{\chi}}(r_{+},t)\Gamma^{0}\,M\tau_{3}\bm{\chi}(r_{-},t),

where
M\in\{1,\frac{e}{2}\tau_{3},\Gamma_{\scriptscriptstyle A}\}
and we use
[M,\Gamma_{c}]=[M,\tau_{3}]=0
. This can be equivalently rewritten as

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha}j^{\alpha}_{\scriptscriptstyle B}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{eQ}{2r^{2}}\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}{\lim}\left(\varepsilon\,\overline{\bm{\chi}}(r_{+},t)\Gamma^{0}\,\tau_{3}\bm{\chi}(r_{-},t)\right)=-i\frac{eQ}{er^{2}}\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}{\lim}\left(\varepsilon j^{0}_{\scriptscriptstyle F}(x)\right),

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha}j^{\alpha}_{\scriptscriptstyle F}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{e(eQ)}{4r^{2}}\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}{\lim}\left(\varepsilon\,\overline{\bm{\chi}}(r_{+},t)\Gamma^{0}\bm{\chi}(r_{-},t)\right)=-i\frac{e(eQ)}{4r^{2}}\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}{\lim}\left(\varepsilon j^{0}_{\scriptscriptstyle B}(x)\right),

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha}j^{\alpha}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{eQ}{2r^{2}}\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}{\lim}\left(\varepsilon\,\overline{\bm{\chi}}(r_{+},t)\Gamma^{1}\bm{\chi}(r_{-},t)\right)=i\frac{eQ}{2r^{2}}\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}{\lim}\left(\varepsilon j^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle B}(x)\right),

(358)

for each choice of
M
, which shows that the conservation of the fermionic currents hinges on whether
j^{\alpha}_{\scriptscriptstyle B},j^{0}_{\scriptscriptstyle F}
are singular in the small
\varepsilon
limit, or not. The dominant contributions to the source terms in the above conservation equations come from the vacuum expectation values of
j^{\alpha}_{\scriptscriptstyle B},j^{0}_{\scriptscriptstyle F}
. Equations (
352
), (
353
) then imply that the fermion number and electric charge current are conserved, while the axial current satisfies the anomaly equation

\partial_{\alpha}j^{\alpha}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}(x)=-\frac{eQ}{\pi r^{2}}=\left[\frac{e}{2}-\left(-\frac{e}{2}\right)\right]\frac{1}{2\pi}\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}{\cal F}^{3}_{\alpha\beta}(x),

(359)

which we rewrite in the last line to emphasize the fact that the top and bottom components of the doublet contribute the usual factor of
\frac{q_{{\mathfrak{s}}}}{2\pi}\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}{\cal F}^{3}_{\alpha\beta}(x)
to the
4
-divergence of the axial current, where
q_{{\mathfrak{s}}}=\frac{1}{2}{\mathfrak{s}}e
. We take the difference, as opposed to the sum, of these two contributions since
j^{\alpha}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}(x)
is defined with an extra
\tau_{3}
matrix compared to the standard definition of an axial current in
2
D.

G.2
Boundary currents

At
r=\epsilon
the radial components of fermionic currents can be evaluated by using the boundary condition
\Gamma^{1}\bm{\chi}(\epsilon,t)=-O_{{\cal B}}({\mathfrak{a}})\,\bm{\chi}(\epsilon,t)
, which can also be rewritten as
{\overline{\bm{\chi}}}(\epsilon,t)\Gamma^{1}={\overline{\bm{\chi}}}(\epsilon,t)O_{{\cal B}}({\mathfrak{a}})
. We get:

\displaystyle j^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle B}(\epsilon,t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{2}\left({\overline{\bm{\chi}}}(\epsilon,t)\Gamma^{1}\bm{\chi}(\epsilon,t)+{\overline{\bm{\chi}}}(\epsilon,t)\Gamma^{1}\bm{\chi}(\epsilon,t)\right)=\frac{i}{2}\left({\overline{\bm{\chi}}}(\epsilon,t)O_{{\cal B}}({\mathfrak{a}})\bm{\chi}(\epsilon,t)-{\overline{\bm{\chi}}}(\epsilon,t)O_{{\cal B}}({\mathfrak{a}})\bm{\chi}(\epsilon,t)\right)=0,

\displaystyle j^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle F}(\epsilon,t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{ie}{4}\left({\overline{\bm{\chi}}}(\epsilon,t)\Gamma^{1}\tau_{3}\bm{\chi}(\epsilon,t)+{\overline{\bm{\chi}}}(\epsilon,t)\tau_{3}\Gamma^{1}\bm{\chi}(\epsilon,t)\right)=\frac{i}{2}{\overline{\bm{\chi}}}(\epsilon,t)\left[O_{{\cal B}}({\mathfrak{a}}),\frac{e}{2}\tau_{3}\right]\bm{\chi}(\epsilon,t),

(360)

\displaystyle j^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}(\epsilon,t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{2}\left({\overline{\bm{\chi}}}(\epsilon,t)\Gamma^{1}\Gamma_{\scriptscriptstyle A}\bm{\chi}(\epsilon,t)-{\overline{\bm{\chi}}}(\epsilon,t)\Gamma_{\scriptscriptstyle A}\Gamma^{1}\bm{\chi}(\epsilon,t)\right)=\frac{i}{2}{\overline{\bm{\chi}}}(\epsilon,t)\left\{O_{{\cal B}}({\mathfrak{a}}),\Gamma_{\scriptscriptstyle A}\right\}\bm{\chi}(\epsilon,t).

The above currents vanish when the boundary action (
48
) is invariant under field transformations of the form
\delta\bm{\chi}(\epsilon,t)=i\theta M\bm{\chi}(\epsilon,t)
, where
\theta
is a constant parameter and
M=1
gives the transformation corresponding to the fermion number current,
M=\frac{e}{2}\tau_{3}
to the electric charge current and
M=\Gamma_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
to the axial current.

As argued earlier in this appendix, the fermion bilinears appearing in (
360
) should be regulated by
e.g.
a point-splitting procedure. Such a regularization procedure generally need not preserve the equality in (
360
). However, if we regularize the boundary currents in the following way

\displaystyle(j^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle B}(\epsilon,t))_{\rm reg}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{2}\,\left({\overline{\bm{\chi}}}(\epsilon,t)\Gamma^{1}\bm{\chi}(\epsilon+\varepsilon,t)+{\overline{\bm{\chi}}}(\epsilon+\varepsilon,t)\Gamma^{1}\bm{\chi}(\epsilon,t)\right),

\displaystyle(j^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle F}(\epsilon,t))_{\rm reg}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\,\frac{ie}{4}\left({\overline{\bm{\chi}}}(\epsilon,t)\Gamma^{1}\tau_{3}\bm{\chi}(\epsilon+\varepsilon,t)+{\overline{\bm{\chi}}}(\epsilon+\varepsilon,t)\tau_{3}\Gamma^{1}\bm{\chi}(\epsilon,t)\right),

\displaystyle(j^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}(\epsilon,t))_{\rm reg}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\,\frac{i}{2}\left({\overline{\bm{\chi}}}(\epsilon,t)\Gamma^{1}\Gamma_{\scriptscriptstyle A}\bm{\chi}(\epsilon+\varepsilon,t)-{\overline{\bm{\chi}}}(\epsilon+\varepsilon,t)\Gamma_{\scriptscriptstyle A}\Gamma^{1}\bm{\chi}(\epsilon,t)\right),

(361)

and the remaining fermion bilinears in (
360
) as

\displaystyle\left(\frac{i}{2}{\overline{\bm{\chi}}}(\epsilon,t)\,[O_{{\cal B}}({\mathfrak{a}}),1]\,\bm{\chi}(\epsilon,t)\right)_{\rm reg}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{2}\,\left({\overline{\bm{\chi}}}(\epsilon,t)O_{{\cal B}}({\mathfrak{a}})\bm{\chi}(\epsilon+\varepsilon,t)-{\overline{\bm{\chi}}}(\epsilon+\varepsilon,t)O_{{\cal B}}({\mathfrak{a}})\bm{\chi}(\epsilon,t)\right),

\displaystyle\,\left(\frac{i}{2}{\overline{\bm{\chi}}}(\epsilon,t)\left[O_{{\cal B}}({\mathfrak{a}}),\frac{e}{2}\tau_{3}\right]\bm{\chi}(\epsilon,t)\right)_{\rm reg}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\,\frac{ie}{4}\left({\overline{\bm{\chi}}}(\epsilon,t)O_{{\cal B}}({\mathfrak{a}})\tau_{3}\bm{\chi}(\epsilon+\varepsilon,t)-{\overline{\bm{\chi}}}(\epsilon+\varepsilon,t)\tau_{3}O_{{\cal B}}({\mathfrak{a}})\bm{\chi}(\epsilon,t)\right),

\displaystyle\left(\frac{i}{2}{\overline{\bm{\chi}}}(\epsilon,t)\left\{O_{{\cal B}}({\mathfrak{a}}),\Gamma_{\scriptscriptstyle A}\right\}\bm{\chi}(\epsilon,t)\right)_{\rm reg}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\,\frac{i}{2}\left({\overline{\bm{\chi}}}(\epsilon,t)O_{{\cal B}}({\mathfrak{a}})\Gamma_{\scriptscriptstyle A}\bm{\chi}(\epsilon+\varepsilon,t)+{\overline{\bm{\chi}}}(\epsilon+\varepsilon,t)\Gamma_{\scriptscriptstyle A}O_{{\cal B}}({\mathfrak{a}})\bm{\chi}(\epsilon,t)\right),

then the regularization scheme does preserve the equality in (
360
), which can be seen by using the boundary conditions satisfied by
{\bm{\chi}}(\epsilon),{\overline{\bm{\chi}}}(\epsilon)
.
